1. The Official Remote Workstation

The CKA examination is delivered via the PSI Secure Browser, connecting candidates to a remote Linux desktop (Ubuntu 22.04 LTS running XFCE):

  • Terminal: Standard XFCE Terminal with bash.
  • Editor: vim or nano (VS Code is present but extensions are disabled; vim is recommended for maximum speed).
  • Browser: Single-tab Chromium browser restricted to official documentation domains:
    • https://kubernetes.io/docs/
    • https://kubernetes.io/blog/
    • https://helm.sh/docs/
  • Clipboard: Cut and paste operates within the remote desktop using standard Linux shortcuts (Ctrl+Shift+C and Ctrl+Shift+V).

CHAPTER 74: TIME MANAGEMENT & COMMAND-LINE SPEED

1. The 120-Minute Time Budget

The examination contains 15 to 20 performance tasks:

  • Average time per task: 6 to 8 minutes.
  • The 3-Minute Rule: If you cannot formulate a clear solution path within 3 minutes of reading a task, FLAG IT AND MOVE ON.
  • High-weight troubleshooting tasks (e.g., control plane outage, cluster upgrade, etcd restore) carry up to 8% to 10% each. Low-weight pod configuration tasks carry 3% to 4%.

2. Essential Time-Saving Aliases

At the start of the exam, run:

alias k=kubectl
export do="--dry-run=client -o yaml"
export now="--force --grace-period=0"

CHAPTER 77: THE TOP 10 DEADLIEST CANDIDATE MISTAKES

  1. Forgetting to Switch Context: Every task begins with a context switch command:
    kubectl config use-context <context-name>
    Failing to run this executes the solution on the wrong cluster, awarding zero points.
  2. Working in the Wrong Namespace: Always append -n <target-namespace> or verify current namespace.
  3. Using Deprecated Flags: Running kubectl ... --record or using obsolete batch/v1beta1 APIs.
  4. Writing YAML from Scratch: Always use kubectl run ... $do or copy from official docs.
  5. Overwriting the etcd Data Directory: Restoring etcd directly into /var/lib/etcd without specifying a new target directory.
  6. Forgetting to Uncordon Nodes: Leaving upgraded nodes cordoned.
  7. Indentation Errors in YAML: Mixing tabs and spaces in vim. Configure ~/.vimrc first!
  8. Ignoring Pod Readiness: Assuming a pod is working because phase is Running, without verifying readiness probes.
  9. Failing to Verify Cluster Status: Not testing kubectl get nodes or curl endpoints before moving to the next task.
  10. Panic and Time Freezing: Spending 25 minutes stuck on a 4% task instead of flagging it.

CHAPTER 79: SPEED DRILLS & IMPERATIVE MUSCLE MEMORY

Speed Drill 1: Rapid Workload Exposure

Generate a Deployment named api-server with 3 replicas of nginx:alpine, exposed internally on port 80 routing to container port 8080:

kubectl create deployment api-server --image=nginx:alpine --replicas=3
kubectl expose deployment api-server --name=api-svc --port=80 --target-port=8080

Time to execute: Under 20 seconds.

Speed Drill 2: Diagnostic JSONPath Extraction

Extract all pod names and their IP addresses sorted by IP across all namespaces:

kubectl get pods -A -o custom-columns=NAME:.metadata.name,IP:.status.podIP --sort-by=.status.podIP

Time to execute: Under 30 seconds.

Speed Drill 3: Instant Node Inspection

Identify which node in the cluster is consuming the highest CPU millicores:

kubectl top nodes --sort-by=cpu

VOLUME II: THE CKA PRACTICAL WORKBOOK

DOMAIN 1: CLUSTER ARCHITECTURE, INSTALLATION & CONFIGURATION (LABS 01–15)

LAB 01: Multi-Namespace RBAC Role and RoleBinding Deployment

  • Difficulty: Beginner
  • Estimated Duration: 8 minutes
  • Domain: Cluster Architecture, Installation & Configuration (25%)
  • Learning Objective: Author a namespace-scoped Role and bind it to a ServiceAccount to permit granular pod management while denying service modifications.
  • Prerequisites: Running cluster with kubectl configured.
  • Cluster Environment: Any standard Kubernetes cluster (kind, minikube, or kubeadm).
  • Initial State: Namespace finance does not exist.
  • Task Description:
    1. Create a namespace named finance.
    2. Create a ServiceAccount named finance-auditor in namespace finance.
    3. Create a Role named pod-reader in namespace finance that allows get, list, and watch verbs on pods and pods/log.
    4. Create a RoleBinding named bind-pod-reader binding the finance-auditor ServiceAccount to the pod-reader Role.
  • Constraints: Do not grant any permissions outside of namespace finance. Do not grant access to secrets or services.
  • Hints: Use kubectl create role and kubectl create rolebinding with --dry-run=client -o yaml for fast execution.
  • Expected Result: The finance-auditor ServiceAccount can list pods in finance, but cannot list pods in default or create deployments.
  • Verification:
    kubectl auth can-i list pods -n finance --as=system:serviceaccount:finance:finance-auditor
# Expected: yes
kubectl auth can-i create pods -n finance --as=system:serviceaccount:finance:finance-auditor
# Expected: no
kubectl auth can-i list pods -n default --as=system:serviceaccount:finance:finance-auditor
# Expected: no
  • Full Solution:
    kubectl create namespace finance
kubectl create serviceaccount finance-auditor -n finance
kubectl create role pod-reader -n finance --verb=get,list,watch --resource=pods,pods/log
kubectl create rolebinding bind-pod-reader -n finance --role=pod-reader --serviceaccount=finance:finance-auditor
  • Common Mistakes: Omitting -n finance on the Role or RoleBinding, creating them in default.
  • Troubleshooting: If auth can-i returns no, inspect kubectl get rolebinding bind-pod-reader -n finance -o yaml to check the subjects namespace.
  • Cleanup Instructions:
    kubectl delete namespace finance

LAB 02: Cluster-Wide Privilege Escalation Prevention via ClusterRoles

  • Difficulty: Intermediate
  • Estimated Duration: 10 minutes
  • Domain: Cluster Architecture, Installation & Configuration (25%)
  • Learning Objective: Create a ClusterRole and bind it with a namespace-scoped RoleBinding to constrain cluster-wide role definitions to a single namespace.
  • Prerequisites: Working cluster.
  • Cluster Environment: Multi-namespace cluster.
  • Initial State: Namespace engineering exists.
  • Task Description:
    1. Create a ClusterRole named node-and-deployment-viewer granting get, list, and watch on nodes and deployments.
    2. Create a ServiceAccount named eng-lead in namespace engineering.
    3. Bind the ClusterRole to eng-lead in namespace engineering using a RoleBinding named eng-lead-deploy-view.
  • Constraints: Do NOT use a ClusterRoleBinding.
  • Hints: A RoleBinding can reference a ClusterRole, but it only grants permissions on namespaced resources within its own namespace.
  • Expected Result: eng-lead can list deployments in engineering, but CANNOT list nodes or list deployments in default.
  • Verification:
    kubectl auth can-i list deployments -n engineering --as=system:serviceaccount:engineering:eng-lead
# Expected: yes
kubectl auth can-i list deployments -n default --as=system:serviceaccount:engineering:eng-lead
# Expected: no
kubectl auth can-i list nodes --as=system:serviceaccount:engineering:eng-lead
# Expected: no (nodes are cluster-scoped, RoleBinding cannot grant them)
  • Full Solution:
    kubectl create namespace engineering --dry-run=client -o yaml | kubectl apply -f -
kubectl create clusterrole node-and-deployment-viewer --verb=get,list,watch --resource=nodes,deployments.apps
kubectl create serviceaccount eng-lead -n engineering
kubectl create rolebinding eng-lead-deploy-view -n engineering --clusterrole=node-and-deployment-viewer --serviceaccount=engineering:eng-lead
  • Common Mistakes: Attempting to grant node permissions via a RoleBinding. Non-namespaced resources (like Nodes) require a ClusterRoleBinding.
  • Troubleshooting: Run kubectl describe rolebinding eng-lead-deploy-view -n engineering and verify RoleRef points to ClusterRole/node-and-deployment-viewer.
  • Cleanup Instructions:
    kubectl delete namespace engineering
kubectl delete clusterrole node-and-deployment-viewer

LAB 03: Safe Worker Node Maintenance (Cordon and Drain)

  • Difficulty: Intermediate
  • Estimated Duration: 12 minutes
  • Domain: Cluster Architecture, Installation & Configuration (25%)
  • Learning Objective: Drain all non-daemon workloads from a worker node, perform mock OS patching, and safely return the node to active service.
  • Prerequisites: Multi-node cluster (1 control plane, at least 2 worker nodes).
  • Cluster Environment: kubeadm multi-node cluster or multi-node kind.
  • Initial State: Deployment nginx-fleet with 4 replicas distributed across worker nodes.
  • Task Description:
    1. Identify worker node worker-1.
    2. Safely cordon worker-1 to prevent new Pod scheduling.
    3. Drain all workloads from worker-1, evicting pods cleanly while ignoring DaemonSets and handling local emptyDir volumes.
    4. Verify all 4 replicas of nginx-fleet are rescheduled onto other nodes.
    5. Uncordon worker-1.
  • Constraints: No downtime for nginx-fleet.
  • Hints: Use --ignore-daemonsets and --delete-emptydir-data.
  • Expected Result: Node worker-1 enters SchedulingDisabled, all pods migrate to other nodes, and then node returns to Ready.
  • Verification:
    kubectl get nodes worker-1
# During drain: SchedulingDisabled
kubectl get pods -l app=nginx-fleet -o wide
# None should reside on worker-1 during maintenance
  • Full Solution:
    kubectl create deployment nginx-fleet --image=nginx:1.27 --replicas=4
kubectl cordon worker-1
kubectl drain worker-1 --ignore-daemonsets --delete-emptydir-data --force
# Verify node is evacuated
kubectl get pods -A -o wide | grep worker-1
# Uncordon
kubectl uncordon worker-1
  • Common Mistakes: Forgetting --ignore-daemonsets, causing the drain command to fail immediately.
  • Troubleshooting: If drain hangs, check for a PodDisruptionBudget blocking eviction.
  • Cleanup Instructions:
    kubectl delete deployment nginx-fleet

LAB 04: Control Plane Version Upgrade with kubeadm

  • Difficulty: Advanced
  • Estimated Duration: 15 minutes
  • Domain: Cluster Architecture, Installation & Configuration (25%)
  • Learning Objective: Execute an in-place minor version upgrade of a Kubernetes control plane node using kubeadm, kubelet, and kubectl.
  • Prerequisites: SSH access to control plane node with sudo.
  • Cluster Environment: kubeadm cluster running v1.30.x.
  • Initial State: Cluster running v1.30.2.
  • Task Description:
    1. Upgrade kubeadm package to 1.31.2-1.1.
    2. Plan and execute kubeadm upgrade apply v1.31.2.
    3. Drain the control plane node.
    4. Upgrade kubelet and kubectl to 1.31.2-1.1.
    5. Restart kubelet and uncordon the control plane.
  • Constraints: Do not skip minor versions. Do not reboot the machine.
  • Hints: Remember to unhold packages before apt-get install and hold them immediately afterward.
  • Expected Result: kubectl get nodes reports the control plane running v1.31.2.
  • Verification:
    kubectl get nodes
kubeadm version
  • Full Solution:
    sudo apt-mark unhold kubeadm
sudo apt-get update && sudo apt-get install -y kubeadm=1.31.2-1.1
sudo apt-mark hold kubeadm

sudo kubeadm upgrade plan
sudo kubeadm upgrade apply v1.31.2 -y

kubectl drain $(hostname) --ignore-daemonsets --delete-emptydir-data

sudo apt-mark unhold kubelet kubectl
sudo apt-get install -y kubelet=1.31.2-1.1 kubectl=1.31.2-1.1
sudo apt-mark hold kubelet kubectl

sudo systemctl daemon-reload
sudo systemctl restart kubelet

kubectl uncordon $(hostname)
  • Common Mistakes: Upgrading kubelet before running kubeadm upgrade apply.
  • Troubleshooting: Check journalctl -u kubelet -e if kubelet fails to start after version upgrade.
  • Cleanup Instructions: None (cluster remains upgraded).

LAB 05: Worker Node Upgrade with kubeadm

  • Difficulty: Advanced
  • Estimated Duration: 12 minutes
  • Domain: Cluster Architecture, Installation & Configuration (25%)
  • Learning Objective: Upgrade a secondary worker node to match the upgraded control plane version.
  • Prerequisites: Control plane already upgraded to v1.31.2; SSH access to worker-1.
  • Cluster Environment: Multi-node kubeadm cluster.
  • Initial State: worker-1 is at v1.30.2 while control plane is at v1.31.2.
  • Task Description:
    1. From control plane, drain worker-1.
    2. SSH into worker-1.
    3. Upgrade kubeadm to 1.31.2-1.1.
    4. Execute sudo kubeadm upgrade node.
    5. Upgrade kubelet and kubectl to 1.31.2-1.1.
    6. Restart kubelet.
    7. From control plane, uncordon worker-1.
  • Constraints: Do not run kubeadm upgrade apply on a worker node.
  • Hints: Use kubeadm upgrade node on workers.
  • Expected Result: kubectl get nodes shows worker-1 running v1.31.2 in Ready status.
  • Verification:
    kubectl get node worker-1 -o custom-columns=NAME:.metadata.name,VERSION:.status.nodeInfo.kubeletVersion,STATUS:.status.conditions[-1].type
  • Full Solution:
    # On Control Plane:
kubectl drain worker-1 --ignore-daemonsets --delete-emptydir-data

# On worker-1:
sudo apt-mark unhold kubeadm
sudo apt-get update && sudo apt-get install -y kubeadm=1.31.2-1.1
sudo apt-mark hold kubeadm

sudo kubeadm upgrade node

sudo apt-mark unhold kubelet kubectl
sudo apt-get install -y kubelet=1.31.2-1.1 kubectl=1.31.2-1.1
sudo apt-mark hold kubelet kubectl

sudo systemctl daemon-reload
sudo systemctl restart kubelet
exit

# On Control Plane:
kubectl uncordon worker-1
  • Common Mistakes: Running kubeadm upgrade apply on worker nodes instead of kubeadm upgrade node.
  • Troubleshooting: If the node stays in SchedulingDisabled, check if kubectl uncordon was run on the control plane.
  • Cleanup Instructions: None.

LAB 06: etcd Automated Snapshot Backup with mTLS

  • Difficulty: Intermediate
  • Estimated Duration: 10 minutes
  • Domain: Cluster Architecture, Installation & Configuration (25%)
  • Learning Objective: Use etcdctl to capture a verified cryptographic snapshot of the cluster state.
  • Prerequisites: SSH access to control plane node with root/sudo access.
  • Cluster Environment: Single or multi-control-plane cluster with static pod etcd.
  • Initial State: Healthy etcd cluster listening on port 2379.
  • Task Description:
    1. Identify etcd client certificates in /etc/kubernetes/pki/etcd/.
    2. Export ETCDCTL_API=3.
    3. Save a snapshot to /var/backups/etcd-snapshot-manual.db.
    4. Verify the snapshot status and record its total keys and revision number.
  • Constraints: Must use TLS certificates: --cacert, --cert, --key.
  • Hints: Pass --endpoints=https://127.0.0.1:2379.
  • Expected Result: Snapshot file exists at /var/backups/etcd-snapshot-manual.db with status showing valid hash.
  • Verification:
    sudo ETCDCTL_API=3 etcdctl --write-out=table snapshot status /var/backups/etcd-snapshot-manual.db
  • Full Solution:
    sudo mkdir -p /var/backups
sudo ETCDCTL_API=3 etcdctl \
  --endpoints=https://127.0.0.1:2379 \
  --cacert=/etc/kubernetes/pki/etcd/ca.crt \
  --cert=/etc/kubernetes/pki/etcd/server.crt \
  --key=/etc/kubernetes/pki/etcd/server.key \
  snapshot save /var/backups/etcd-snapshot-manual.db
  • Common Mistakes: Forgetting ETCDCTL_API=3, causing the command to default to API v2 and fail.
  • Troubleshooting: If connection refused, verify port 2379 is listening with netstat -tlpn | grep 2379.
  • Cleanup Instructions: None (retain backup for Lab 07).

LAB 07: Disaster Recovery: etcd Snapshot Restoration

  • Difficulty: Advanced
  • Estimated Duration: 15 minutes
  • Domain: Cluster Architecture, Installation & Configuration (25%)
  • Learning Objective: Restore a previously captured etcd snapshot into a new data directory and update static pod manifests.
  • Prerequisites: Completed Lab 06 (snapshot at /var/backups/etcd-snapshot-manual.db).
  • Cluster Environment: kubeadm control plane node.
  • Initial State: Cluster running with data created after the snapshot.
  • Task Description:
    1. Create a canary deployment pre-restore-canary (will be deleted by restore).
    2. Stop the API Server by moving /etc/kubernetes/manifests/kube-apiserver.yaml to /tmp/.
    3. Restore /var/backups/etcd-snapshot-manual.db into new directory /var/lib/etcd-from-backup.
    4. Update /etc/kubernetes/manifests/etcd.yaml to use /var/lib/etcd-from-backup for hostPath volume mounts.
    5. Move kube-apiserver.yaml back to /etc/kubernetes/manifests/.
    6. Verify cluster state is restored and pre-restore-canary is gone.
  • Constraints: Do not overwrite existing /var/lib/etcd directory directly.
  • Hints: Modify both volumeMounts and volumes in etcd.yaml.
  • Expected Result: API server restarts, etcd mounts new directory, canary deployment no longer exists.
  • Verification:
    kubectl get deployment pre-restore-canary
# Expected: Error from server (NotFound)
kubectl get nodes
# Expected: Ready
  • Full Solution:
    kubectl create deployment pre-restore-canary --image=nginx:alpine

# 1. Stop static pods
sudo mv /etc/kubernetes/manifests/kube-apiserver.yaml /tmp/
sudo mv /etc/kubernetes/manifests/etcd.yaml /tmp/

# 2. Restore to new dir
sudo ETCDCTL_API=3 etcdctl \
  --data-dir=/var/lib/etcd-from-backup \
  snapshot restore /var/backups/etcd-snapshot-manual.db

# 3. Update etcd manifest
sudo sed -i 's|/var/lib/etcd|/var/lib/etcd-from-backup|g' /tmp/etcd.yaml

# 4. Restore manifests
sudo mv /tmp/etcd.yaml /etc/kubernetes/manifests/
sudo mv /tmp/kube-apiserver.yaml /etc/kubernetes/manifests/

# 5. Wait for API Server
sleep 15
kubectl get nodes
  • Common Mistakes: Overwriting /var/lib/etcd without stopping the static pod, causing write collisions.
  • Troubleshooting: Use crictl ps -a | grep etcd to check if the restored etcd container launched successfully.
  • Cleanup Instructions:
    sudo rm -rf /var/lib/etcd-from-backup
# (Optionally revert etcd.yaml back to /var/lib/etcd)

LAB 08: Deploying Applications via Helm with Values Overrides

  • Difficulty: Intermediate
  • Estimated Duration: 10 minutes
  • Domain: Cluster Architecture, Installation & Configuration (25%)
  • Learning Objective: Install and configure an application using Helm CLI, pass custom parameters via --set, and verify release lifecycle.
  • Prerequisites: Helm v3 installed on candidate workstation.
  • Cluster Environment: Standard Kubernetes cluster.
  • Initial State: Namespace monitoring-tools does not exist.
  • Task Description:
    1. Add the Prometheus community repo: https://prometheus-community.github.io/helm-charts.
    2. Install a release named node-metrics using chart prometheus-community/prometheus-node-exporter.
    3. Deploy into namespace monitoring-tools (create if needed).
    4. Set the hostNetwork configuration to true and service port to 9100.
    5. List the installed releases and verify status is deployed.
  • Constraints: Use Helm CLI only; do not write raw deployment manifests.
  • Hints: Use --namespace <ns> --create-namespace.
  • Expected Result: Helm release node-metrics is deployed and running DaemonSet pods on every node.
  • Verification:
    helm list -n monitoring-tools
kubectl get daemonset -n monitoring-tools
  • Full Solution:
    helm repo add prometheus-community https://prometheus-community.github.io/helm-charts
helm repo update
helm install node-metrics prometheus-community/prometheus-node-exporter \
  --namespace monitoring-tools \
  --create-namespace \
  --set service.port=9100
  • Common Mistakes: Typing the repo name incorrectly or forgetting helm repo update.
  • Troubleshooting: Run helm status node-metrics -n monitoring-tools to view deployment notes and error states.
  • Cleanup Instructions:
    helm uninstall node-metrics -n monitoring-tools
kubectl delete namespace monitoring-tools

LAB 09: Declarative Multi-Environment Overlays with Kustomize

  • Difficulty: Intermediate
  • Estimated Duration: 12 minutes
  • Domain: Cluster Architecture, Installation & Configuration (25%)
  • Learning Objective: Structure a base directory and production overlay using Kustomize, and apply it directly using kubectl apply -k.
  • Prerequisites: kubectl with native Kustomize support.
  • Cluster Environment: Any test cluster.
  • Initial State: Clean working directory /tmp/kustomize-lab.
  • Task Description:
    1. Create directory structure /tmp/kustomize-lab/base and /tmp/kustomize-lab/overlays/prod.
    2. In base, author a Deployment app-server running nginx:1.27 with 1 replica and a kustomization.yaml.
    3. In overlays/prod, author a kustomization.yaml that:
      • Pulls from ../../base.
      • Adds name prefix prod-.
      • Sets common labels env: production and tier: gold.
      • Patches replica count to 3.
    4. Apply the production overlay using kubectl apply -k.
  • Constraints: Do not modify files in base when creating the production overlay.
  • Hints: Use kubectl kustomize to preview the rendered YAML before applying.
  • Expected Result: A Deployment named prod-app-server with 3 replicas is created.
  • Verification:
    kubectl get deployment prod-app-server -o jsonpath='{.spec.replicas}'
# Expected: 3
kubectl get pods -l env=production,tier=gold
  • Full Solution:
    mkdir -p /tmp/kustomize-lab/base /tmp/kustomize-lab/overlays/prod

# Base
cat <<EOF > /tmp/kustomize-lab/base/deployment.yaml
apiVersion: apps/v1
kind: Deployment
metadata:
  name: app-server
spec:
  replicas: 1
  selector:
    matchLabels:
      app: app-server
  template:
    metadata:
      labels:
        app: app-server
    spec:
      containers:
      - name: nginx
        image: nginx:1.27
EOF

cat <<EOF > /tmp/kustomize-lab/base/kustomization.yaml
resources:
- deployment.yaml
EOF

# Overlay
cat <<EOF > /tmp/kustomize-lab/overlays/prod/replica-patch.yaml
apiVersion: apps/v1
kind: Deployment
metadata:
  name: app-server
spec:
  replicas: 3
EOF

cat <<EOF > /tmp/kustomize-lab/overlays/prod/kustomization.yaml
resources:
- ../../base
namePrefix: prod-
commonLabels:
  env: production
  tier: gold
patches:
- path: replica-patch.yaml
EOF

kubectl apply -k /tmp/kustomize-lab/overlays/prod
  • Common Mistakes: Misspelling resources as resource in kustomization.yaml.
  • Troubleshooting: Run kubectl kustomize /tmp/kustomize-lab/overlays/prod to view exact schema validation errors.
  • Cleanup Instructions:
    kubectl delete -k /tmp/kustomize-lab/overlays/prod
rm -rf /tmp/kustomize-lab

LAB 10: Custom Resource Definition (CRD) Inspection and Instantiation

  • Difficulty: Intermediate
  • Estimated Duration: 10 minutes
  • Domain: Cluster Architecture, Installation & Configuration (25%)
  • Learning Objective: Install a Custom Resource Definition, inspect its schema with kubectl explain, and instantiate a Custom Resource.
  • Prerequisites: Cluster administrator privileges.
  • Cluster Environment: Any test cluster.
  • Initial State: No CRD for Database exists.
  • Task Description:
    1. Author and apply a CRD databases.example.com with group example.com, version v1, kind Database, and scope Namespaced. Include a string spec field engine and integer field replicas.
    2. Use kubectl explain database.spec to verify the schema.
    3. Create a Custom Resource of kind Database named orders-db with engine: postgres and replicas: 2.
  • Constraints: Schema must be validated OpenAPI v3.
  • Hints: Root API group for CRD definitions is apiextensions.k8s.io/v1.
  • Expected Result: kubectl get databases lists orders-db.
  • Verification:
    kubectl get crd databases.example.com
kubectl get databases orders-db -o yaml
  • Full Solution:
    apiVersion: apiextensions.k8s.io/v1
kind: CustomResourceDefinition
metadata:
  name: databases.example.com
spec:
  group: example.com
  versions:
  - name: v1
    served: true
    storage: true
    schema:
      openAPIV3Schema:
        type: object
        properties:
          spec:
            type: object
            properties:
              engine:
                type: string
              replicas:
                type: integer
  scope: Namespaced
  names:
    plural: databases
    singular: database
    kind: Database
    shortNames:
    - db
---
apiVersion: example.com/v1
kind: Database
metadata:
  name: orders-db
spec:
  engine: postgres
  replicas: 2
  • Common Mistakes: Omitting plural in names, which causes CRD validation rejection.
  • Troubleshooting: If kubectl explain fails, check if the CRD version has served: true.
  • Cleanup Instructions:
    kubectl delete crd databases.example.com

LAB 11: ServiceAccount Token Projection & Expiration Tuning

  • Difficulty: Intermediate
  • Estimated Duration: 8 minutes
  • Domain: Cluster Architecture, Installation & Configuration (25%)
  • Learning Objective: Configure a Pod with projected time-bound ServiceAccount tokens and custom audience claims.
  • Prerequisites: Cluster running v1.24+.
  • Cluster Environment: Standard Kubernetes cluster.
  • Initial State: ServiceAccount vault-auth-sa exists.
  • Task Description:
    1. Create a ServiceAccount named vault-auth-sa in default.
    2. Create a Pod vault-client running alpine that mounts a projected ServiceAccount token at /var/run/secrets/tokens/vault-token.
    3. Configure token expiration to 7200 seconds and audience to vault.hashicorp.internal.
    4. Inspect the token from inside the running Pod container.
  • Constraints: Do not use legacy static Secret tokens.
  • Hints: Use spec.volumes[*].projected.sources[*].serviceAccountToken.
  • Expected Result: The token file exists inside the container and can be read.
  • Verification:
    kubectl exec vault-client -- ls -la /var/run/secrets/tokens/vault-token
  • Full Solution:
    kubectl create serviceaccount vault-auth-sa

    apiVersion: v1
kind: Pod
metadata:
  name: vault-client
spec:
  serviceAccountName: vault-auth-sa
  containers:
  - name: client
    image: alpine
    command: ["sleep", "3600"]
    volumeMounts:
    - mountPath: /var/run/secrets/tokens
      name: vault-token-vol
  volumes:
  - name: vault-token-vol
    projected:
      sources:
      - serviceAccountToken:
          path: vault-token
          expirationSeconds: 7200
          audience: vault.hashicorp.internal
  • Common Mistakes: Omitting expirationSeconds or setting it below 600 seconds (minimum is 10 minutes).
  • Troubleshooting: If container fails with mount error, check Pod events with kubectl describe pod vault-client.
  • Cleanup Instructions:
    kubectl delete pod vault-client
kubectl delete serviceaccount vault-auth-sa

LAB 12: Certificate Expiration Audit and Manual Renewal

  • Difficulty: Intermediate
  • Estimated Duration: 10 minutes
  • Domain: Cluster Architecture, Installation & Configuration (25%)
  • Learning Objective: Inspect expiration dates of all control plane TLS certificates and execute targeted renewals using kubeadm.
  • Prerequisites: SSH access to control plane node with sudo privileges.
  • Cluster Environment: kubeadm single or multi-master cluster.
  • Initial State: Cluster certificates in /etc/kubernetes/pki/.
  • Task Description:
    1. Inspect the expiration of all certificates on the control plane node.
    2. Identify the expiration date of apiserver-kubelet-client.crt.
    3. Manually renew strictly the apiserver-kubelet-client certificate without renewing all certs.
    4. Verify the renewed expiration date has moved 1 year into the future.
  • Constraints: Do not break API server connectivity.
  • Hints: Use kubeadm certs check-expiration and kubeadm certs renew <cert-name>.
  • Expected Result: apiserver-kubelet-client certificate shows renewed 1-year validity.
  • Verification:
    sudo kubeadm certs check-expiration | grep apiserver-kubelet-client
  • Full Solution:
    # Check expiration
sudo kubeadm certs check-expiration

# Renew single cert
sudo kubeadm certs renew apiserver-kubelet-client

# Verify
sudo kubeadm certs check-expiration | grep apiserver-kubelet-client
  • Common Mistakes: Running kubeadm certs renew all when the task explicitly asked for a single certificate renewal.
  • Troubleshooting: If the certificate does not update, ensure the command was executed with sudo.
  • Cleanup Instructions: None.

LAB 13: High Availability Control Plane Sizing and Quorum Calculation

  • Difficulty: Intermediate
  • Estimated Duration: 8 minutes
  • Domain: Cluster Architecture, Installation & Configuration (25%)
  • Learning Objective: Calculate exact etcd quorum boundaries, fault tolerances, and control plane load balancer requirements for production HA clusters.
  • Prerequisites: Conceptual understanding of Raft consensus.
  • Cluster Environment: Paper / CLI scenario.
  • Initial State: Cluster sizing inquiry.
  • Task Description:
    1. Create a script or manifest documenting the sizing requirements for an HA cluster designed to survive the simultaneous failure of 2 etcd nodes.
    2. Determine minimum etcd node count, quorum number, and failure tolerance.
    3. Author a ConfigMap named ha-cluster-specs in kube-system containing keys: etcd_nodes, quorum_needed, tolerated_failures.
  • Constraints: Quorum formula $\lfloor N/2 \rfloor + 1$ must be strictly adhered to.
  • Hints: To survive 2 failures, $N=5$, because Quorum $= \lfloor 5/2 \rfloor + 1 = 3$. ($5 - 2 = 3$).
  • Expected Result: ConfigMap reflects accurate distributed consensus topology.
  • Verification:
    kubectl get configmap ha-cluster-specs -n kube-system -o yaml
  • Full Solution:
    kubectl create configmap ha-cluster-specs -n kube-system \
  --from-literal=etcd_nodes=5 \
  --from-literal=quorum_needed=3 \
  --from-literal=tolerated_failures=2
  • Common Mistakes: Assuming a 4-node etcd cluster provides more fault tolerance than a 3-node cluster (both tolerate only 1 failure, but 4-node has higher risk of split-brain).
  • Troubleshooting: Recheck Raft odd-number quorum rules.
  • Cleanup Instructions:
    kubectl delete configmap ha-cluster-specs -n kube-system

LAB 14: Containerd Socket & Cgroup Driver Alignment

  • Difficulty: Intermediate
  • Estimated Duration: 10 minutes
  • Domain: Cluster Architecture, Installation & Configuration (25%)
  • Learning Objective: Verify and enforce SystemdCgroup = true in /etc/containerd/config.toml to prevent kubelet cgroup panic.
  • Prerequisites: SSH access to a worker node.
  • Cluster Environment: Node running containerd.
  • Initial State: Default or unverified containerd configuration.
  • Task Description:
    1. Inspect /etc/containerd/config.toml on worker-1.
    2. Ensure SystemdCgroup is set to true under runc.options.
    3. Restart containerd and kubelet.
    4. Verify using crictl info that the runtime endpoint is active and reports healthy.
  • Constraints: Do not corrupt the TOML syntax.
  • Hints: If /etc/containerd/config.toml is empty or missing, generate defaults with containerd config default.
  • Expected Result: containerd and kubelet running cleanly with systemd cgroups.
  • Verification:
    sudo crictl info | jq .status.conditions
sudo systemctl is-active kubelet
# Expected: active
  • Full Solution:
    sudo mkdir -p /etc/containerd
containerd config default | sudo tee /etc/containerd/config.toml
sudo sed -i 's/SystemdCgroup = false/SystemdCgroup = true/g' /etc/containerd/config.toml
sudo systemctl restart containerd
sudo systemctl restart kubelet
  • Common Mistakes: Editing the wrong [plugins."io.containerd.grpc.v1.cri"...] block.
  • Troubleshooting: If containerd fails to restart, run sudo containerd config dump to find the exact line with the TOML syntax error.
  • Cleanup Instructions: None.

LAB 15: Static Pod Provisioning on Specific Worker Node

  • Difficulty: Intermediate
  • Estimated Duration: 10 minutes
  • Domain: Cluster Architecture, Installation & Configuration (25%)
  • Learning Objective: Provision a static Pod on a worker node by directly placing a manifest in the kubelet's static pod path.
  • Prerequisites: SSH access to worker-1.
  • Cluster Environment: Multi-node cluster.
  • Initial State: Kubelet running on worker-1.
  • Task Description:
    1. Inspect /var/lib/kubelet/config.yaml on worker-1 to locate staticPodPath.
    2. If staticPodPath is not defined, add staticPodPath: /etc/kubernetes/manifests and restart kubelet.
    3. Place a Pod manifest worker-monitor.yaml in /etc/kubernetes/manifests/ running busybox:1.36 sleeping 3600.
    4. On the control plane, verify the mirror pod worker-monitor-worker-1 appears in kubectl get pods.
  • Constraints: Do not use kubectl create or kubectl apply to launch the Pod.
  • Hints: Static pods are named <pod-name>-<node-name>.
  • Expected Result: Mirror pod is visible to the API server in Running status.
  • Verification:
    kubectl get pods -A | grep worker-monitor
  • Full Solution:
    # On worker-1:
sudo mkdir -p /etc/kubernetes/manifests
# Verify /var/lib/kubelet/config.yaml has staticPodPath: /etc/kubernetes/manifests
cat <<EOF | sudo tee /etc/kubernetes/manifests/worker-monitor.yaml
apiVersion: v1
kind: Pod
metadata:
  name: worker-monitor
spec:
  containers:
  - name: monitor
    image: busybox:1.36
    command: ["sleep", "3600"]
EOF

# If needed, restart kubelet:
sudo systemctl restart kubelet

# On Control Plane:
kubectl get pods | grep worker-monitor
  • Common Mistakes: Attempting to delete the mirror pod via kubectl delete pod; it will immediately be recreated by the worker's kubelet until the file is deleted from disk!
  • Troubleshooting: If mirror pod does not appear, check journalctl -u kubelet -e on worker-1.
  • Cleanup Instructions:
    # On worker-1:
sudo rm -f /etc/kubernetes/manifests/worker-monitor.yaml

VOLUME II: THE CKA PRACTICAL WORKBOOK

DOMAIN 2: WORKLOADS & SCHEDULING (LABS 16–30)

LAB 16: Zero-Downtime Deployment Rolling Updates and Revisions

  • Difficulty: Beginner
  • Estimated Duration: 8 minutes
  • Domain: Workloads & Scheduling (15%)
  • Learning Objective: Orchestrate an application rolling update, inspect rollout history, and execute a surgical rollback to a specific historical revision.
  • Prerequisites: Running cluster.
  • Cluster Environment: Standard Kubernetes cluster.
  • Initial State: Clean namespace web-workloads.
  • Task Description:
    1. Create namespace web-workloads.
    2. Deploy nginx-deployment with 3 replicas running image nginx:1.25 in web-workloads.
    3. Update image to nginx:1.26 using kubectl set image, recording the rollout command.
    4. Update image to a non-existent tag nginx:1.999-broken to simulate a broken release.
    5. Check rollout status and observe the failure.
    6. Inspect revision history and roll back to the stable revision running nginx:1.26.
  • Constraints: Keep at least 2 replicas available at all times during rollout.
  • Hints: Use kubectl rollout history and kubectl rollout undo --to-revision=<N>.
  • Expected Result: Deployment stabilizes at 3 replicas running nginx:1.26.
  • Verification:
    kubectl get deployment nginx-deployment -n web-workloads -o jsonpath='{.spec.template.spec.containers[0].image}'
# Expected: nginx:1.26
  • Full Solution:
    kubectl create namespace web-workloads
kubectl create deployment nginx-deployment --image=nginx:1.25 --replicas=3 -n web-workloads
kubectl set image deployment/nginx-deployment nginx=nginx:1.26 -n web-workloads
kubectl rollout status deployment/nginx-deployment -n web-workloads
kubectl set image deployment/nginx-deployment nginx=nginx:1.999-broken -n web-workloads
# Observe failure
kubectl rollout undo deployment/nginx-deployment --to-revision=2 -n web-workloads
  • Common Mistakes: Rolling back without checking revision numbers, reverting to the wrong version.
  • Troubleshooting: Run kubectl describe deployment nginx-deployment -n web-workloads to view condition progress.
  • Cleanup Instructions:
    kubectl delete namespace web-workloads

LAB 17: StatefulSet Deployment with Sequential Pod Management

  • Difficulty: Intermediate
  • Estimated Duration: 12 minutes
  • Domain: Workloads & Scheduling (15%)
  • Learning Objective: Deploy a StatefulSet paired with a headless Service and observe ordered creation and stable network hostnames.
  • Prerequisites: Cluster with default StorageClass.
  • Cluster Environment: Any Kubernetes cluster.
  • Initial State: Namespace stateful-apps.
  • Task Description:
    1. Create namespace stateful-apps.
    2. Create a headless Service db-cluster-svc with clusterIP: None exposing port 27017 for selector app: mongo.
    3. Create a StatefulSet mongo with 3 replicas, serviceName db-cluster-svc, and image mongo:6.0.
    4. Verify pods launch sequentially: mongo-0, then mongo-1, then mongo-2.
    5. Scale the StatefulSet to 4 replicas and verify ordered scale-up.
  • Constraints: Must use headless Service for network identity.
  • Hints: In a StatefulSet, mongo-1 will not start until mongo-0 is in Running and ready.
  • Expected Result: 4 sequential pods running with predictable names.
  • Verification:
    kubectl get pods -n stateful-apps -l app=mongo
  • Full Solution:
    apiVersion: v1
kind: Namespace
metadata:
  name: stateful-apps
---
apiVersion: v1
kind: Service
metadata:
  name: db-cluster-svc
  namespace: stateful-apps
spec:
  clusterIP: None
  selector:
    app: mongo
  ports:
  - port: 27017
    name: mongo
---
apiVersion: apps/v1
kind: StatefulSet
metadata:
  name: mongo
  namespace: stateful-apps
spec:
  serviceName: db-cluster-svc
  replicas: 3
  selector:
    matchLabels:
      app: mongo
  template:
    metadata:
      labels:
        app: mongo
    spec:
      containers:
      - name: mongo
        image: mongo:6.0
        ports:
        - containerPort: 27017

    kubectl apply -f statefulset.yaml
kubectl scale statefulset mongo --replicas=4 -n stateful-apps
  • Common Mistakes: Omitting serviceName in the StatefulSet spec.
  • Troubleshooting: If pods do not advance to the next index, inspect the previous pod's readiness probe.
  • Cleanup Instructions:
    kubectl delete namespace stateful-apps

LAB 18: DaemonSet Node Coverage and Host Resource Access

  • Difficulty: Intermediate
  • Estimated Duration: 10 minutes
  • Domain: Workloads & Scheduling (15%)
  • Learning Objective: Deploy a DaemonSet targeting all worker nodes while tolerating control plane taints to collect host system metrics.
  • Prerequisites: Multi-node cluster with tainted control plane.
  • Cluster Environment: kubeadm or multi-node kind.
  • Initial State: Namespace monitoring.
  • Task Description:
    1. Create namespace monitoring.
    2. Create a DaemonSet named host-monitor in namespace monitoring.
    3. Run container image busybox:1.36 executing sleep 86400.
    4. Add a toleration so the DaemonSet pod also schedules on the control plane node: key: node-role.kubernetes.io/control-plane, effect: NoSchedule.
    5. Verify that the number of running pods exactly matches the total node count (workers + control plane).
  • Constraints: Must use DaemonSet, not Deployment.
  • Hints: Check node count with kubectl get nodes --no-headers | wc -l.
  • Expected Result: Exactly 1 pod runs on every node in the cluster.
  • Verification:
    kubectl get ds host-monitor -n monitoring
# Desired should equal Current and Ready
  • Full Solution:
    apiVersion: apps/v1
kind: DaemonSet
metadata:
  name: host-monitor
  namespace: monitoring
spec:
  selector:
    matchLabels:
      app: host-monitor
  template:
    metadata:
      labels:
        app: host-monitor
    spec:
      tolerations:
      - key: node-role.kubernetes.io/control-plane
        operator: Exists
        effect: NoSchedule
      containers:
      - name: monitor
        image: busybox:1.36
        command: ["sleep", "86400"]

    kubectl create namespace monitoring
kubectl apply -f daemonset.yaml
  • Common Mistakes: Omitting the control plane toleration, resulting in pods only scheduling on worker nodes.
  • Troubleshooting: Run kubectl get pods -n monitoring -o wide to verify each node holds one pod.
  • Cleanup Instructions:
    kubectl delete namespace monitoring

LAB 19: Batch Jobs with Parallelism and Completion Quotas

  • Difficulty: Beginner
  • Estimated Duration: 8 minutes
  • Domain: Workloads & Scheduling (15%)
  • Learning Objective: Configure a Kubernetes Job that requires 6 total completions with up to 2 pods running concurrently.
  • Prerequisites: Working cluster.
  • Cluster Environment: Any cluster.
  • Initial State: Namespace batch-jobs.
  • Task Description:
    1. Create namespace batch-jobs.
    2. Create a Job named data-processor in namespace batch-jobs.
    3. Image: busybox:1.36. Command: sh -c "echo processing partition; sleep 3".
    4. Set completions: 6 and parallelism: 2.
    5. Set backoffLimit: 3.
    6. Monitor the Job until status reaches Completed.
  • Constraints: Ensure restartPolicy: OnFailure or Never.
  • Hints: Use kubectl create job data-processor --image=busybox:1.36 --dry-run=client -o yaml and edit completions and parallelism.
  • Expected Result: Job finishes with 6/6 completions.
  • Verification:
    kubectl get job data-processor -n batch-jobs
# COMPLETIONS: 6/6
  • Full Solution:
    apiVersion: batch/v1
kind: Job
metadata:
  name: data-processor
  namespace: batch-jobs
spec:
  completions: 6
  parallelism: 2
  backoffLimit: 3
  template:
    spec:
      restartPolicy: OnFailure
      containers:
      - name: processor
        image: busybox:1.36
        command: ["/bin/sh", "-c", "echo processing partition; sleep 3"]

    kubectl create namespace batch-jobs
kubectl apply -f job.yaml
kubectl get jobs -n batch-jobs -w
  • Common Mistakes: Setting restartPolicy: Always, which is illegal in Job Pod templates.
  • Troubleshooting: Run kubectl describe job data-processor -n batch-jobs to view active pods and completion timestamps.
  • Cleanup Instructions:
    kubectl delete namespace batch-jobs

LAB 20: CronJob with Concurrency Policies

  • Difficulty: Intermediate
  • Estimated Duration: 10 minutes
  • Domain: Workloads & Scheduling (15%)
  • Learning Objective: Schedule a recurring task using CronJob and enforce concurrencyPolicy: Forbid to prevent overlapping runs.
  • Prerequisites: Working cluster.
  • Cluster Environment: Any cluster.
  • Initial State: Namespace cron-jobs.
  • Task Description:
    1. Create namespace cron-jobs.
    2. Create a CronJob log-cleaner running every minute (* * * * *).
    3. Image: busybox:1.36. Command: sh -c "echo Cleaning logs; sleep 70".
    4. Set concurrencyPolicy: Forbid.
    5. Set successfulJobsHistoryLimit: 2 and failedJobsHistoryLimit: 1.
    6. Observe that because the job sleeps for 70 seconds, the 1-minute schedule skips the second iteration rather than running concurrent pods.
  • Constraints: Must use batch/v1.
  • Hints: concurrencyPolicy options are Allow, Forbid, and Replace.
  • Expected Result: At most 1 pod executes at any single time.
  • Verification:
    kubectl get cronjob log-cleaner -n cron-jobs
kubectl get pods -n cron-jobs
  • Full Solution:
    apiVersion: batch/v1
kind: CronJob
metadata:
  name: log-cleaner
  namespace: cron-jobs
spec:
  schedule: "* * * * *"
  concurrencyPolicy: Forbid
  successfulJobsHistoryLimit: 2
  failedJobsHistoryLimit: 1
  jobTemplate:
    spec:
      template:
        spec:
          restartPolicy: OnFailure
          containers:
          - name: cleaner
            image: busybox:1.36
            command: ["/bin/sh", "-c", "echo Cleaning logs; sleep 70"]

    kubectl create namespace cron-jobs
kubectl apply -f cronjob.yaml
  • Common Mistakes: Using deprecated batch/v1beta1 API group.
  • Troubleshooting: Inspect events with kubectl describe cronjob log-cleaner -n cron-jobs.
  • Cleanup Instructions:
    kubectl delete namespace cron-jobs

LAB 21: Native Sidecar Containers with restartPolicy: Always

  • Difficulty: Advanced
  • Estimated Duration: 12 minutes
  • Domain: Workloads & Scheduling (15%)
  • Learning Objective: Implement Kubernetes v1.29+ native sidecar containers in initContainers with restartPolicy: Always.
  • Prerequisites: Kubernetes v1.29+ cluster.
  • Cluster Environment: Modern cluster.
  • Initial State: Namespace sidecar-demo.
  • Task Description:
    1. Create namespace sidecar-demo.
    2. Author a Pod secure-app with:
      • An initContainer auth-proxy running busybox:1.36 with command: ["sleep", "3600"] and restartPolicy: Always.
      • A main application container web running nginx:1.27.
    3. Verify that auth-proxy starts first, remains running, and does not block web from starting.
    4. Verify that kubectl get pod secure-app shows 2/2 containers running.
  • Constraints: Must use native sidecar syntax in initContainers.
  • Hints: Native sidecars are defined inside spec.initContainers with restartPolicy: Always.
  • Expected Result: Pod shows READY: 2/2 and STATUS: Running.
  • Verification:
    kubectl get pod secure-app -n sidecar-demo
# Expected: READY: 2/2
  • Full Solution:
    apiVersion: v1
kind: Pod
metadata:
  name: secure-app
  namespace: sidecar-demo
spec:
  initContainers:
  - name: auth-proxy
    image: busybox:1.36
    command: ["sleep", "3600"]
    restartPolicy: Always
  containers:
  - name: web
    image: nginx:1.27

    kubectl create namespace sidecar-demo
kubectl apply -f sidecar-pod.yaml
  • Common Mistakes: Placing restartPolicy: Always on a standard initContainer in older clusters (<v1.28) without the SidecarContainers feature gate.
  • Troubleshooting: Run kubectl describe pod secure-app -n sidecar-demo to inspect initialization sequence.
  • Cleanup Instructions:
    kubectl delete namespace sidecar-demo

LAB 22: ConfigMap Volume Mounts with Automatic SubPath Updates

  • Difficulty: Intermediate
  • Estimated Duration: 10 minutes
  • Domain: Workloads & Scheduling (15%)
  • Learning Objective: Mount configuration files from a ConfigMap into a running container without overwriting other files in the destination directory.
  • Prerequisites: Working cluster.
  • Cluster Environment: Any cluster.
  • Initial State: Namespace config-lab.
  • Task Description:
    1. Create namespace config-lab.
    2. Create a ConfigMap custom-nginx-conf containing key custom.conf with text server_tokens off;.
    3. Deploy Pod nginx-secured running nginx:1.27.
    4. Mount custom.conf from the ConfigMap into /etc/nginx/conf.d/custom.conf using subPath so default nginx config files in that directory are preserved.
    5. Verify the file contents inside the container using kubectl exec.
  • Constraints: Do not overwrite /etc/nginx/conf.d/default.conf.
  • Hints: Use volumeMounts[*].subPath: custom.conf.
  • Expected Result: /etc/nginx/conf.d/ contains both default.conf and custom.conf.
  • Verification:
    kubectl exec -it nginx-secured -n config-lab -- ls -la /etc/nginx/conf.d/
kubectl exec -it nginx-secured -n config-lab -- cat /etc/nginx/conf.d/custom.conf
  • Full Solution:
    kubectl create namespace config-lab
kubectl create configmap custom-nginx-conf --from-literal=custom.conf="server_tokens off;" -n config-lab

    apiVersion: v1
kind: Pod
metadata:
  name: nginx-secured
  namespace: config-lab
spec:
  containers:
  - name: nginx
    image: nginx:1.27
    volumeMounts:
    - name: conf-vol
      mountPath: /etc/nginx/conf.d/custom.conf
      subPath: custom.conf
  volumes:
  - name: conf-vol
    configMap:
      name: custom-nginx-conf

    kubectl apply -f nginx-conf-pod.yaml
  • Common Mistakes: Mounting the volume directly at /etc/nginx/conf.d/ without subPath, which wipes out all existing files in that directory.
  • Troubleshooting: If pod fails to start with RunContainerError, check if the ConfigMap key matches subPath.
  • Cleanup Instructions:
    kubectl delete namespace config-lab

LAB 23: Secret Management and Secure Environment Injection

  • Difficulty: Beginner
  • Estimated Duration: 8 minutes
  • Domain: Workloads & Scheduling (15%)
  • Learning Objective: Create a generic Kubernetes Secret and inject its keys into an application container as environment variables.
  • Prerequisites: Working cluster.
  • Cluster Environment: Any cluster.
  • Initial State: Namespace security-lab.
  • Task Description:
    1. Create namespace security-lab.
    2. Create a Secret named database-creds containing:
      • DB_USER=app_user
      • DB_PASS=SuperSecurePass123!
    3. Deploy Pod db-client running busybox:1.36 sleeping 3600.
    4. Inject all key-value pairs from database-creds as environment variables using envFrom.
    5. Verify the environment variables exist inside the container.
  • Constraints: Do not hardcode secret values into the Pod manifest.
  • Hints: Use spec.containers[*].envFrom[*].secretRef.
  • Expected Result: env inside the pod displays DB_USER and DB_PASS.
  • Verification:
    kubectl exec db-client -n security-lab -- env | grep DB_
  • Full Solution:
    kubectl create namespace security-lab
kubectl create secret generic database-creds \
  --from-literal=DB_USER=app_user \
  --from-literal=DB_PASS='SuperSecurePass123!' \
  -n security-lab

    apiVersion: v1
kind: Pod
metadata:
  name: db-client
  namespace: security-lab
spec:
  containers:
  - name: client
    image: busybox:1.36
    command: ["sleep", "3600"]
    envFrom:
    - secretRef:
        name: database-creds

    kubectl apply -f db-client-pod.yaml
  • Common Mistakes: Using env with single keys when envFrom can import all keys in one declaration.
  • Troubleshooting: Run kubectl describe pod db-client -n security-lab if pod fails to launch.
  • Cleanup Instructions:
    kubectl delete namespace security-lab

LAB 24: Pod Quality of Service (QoS) Classes

  • Difficulty: Intermediate
  • Estimated Duration: 10 minutes
  • Domain: Workloads & Scheduling (15%)
  • Learning Objective: Author three separate Pod specifications to explicitly produce the three Kubernetes QoS classes: Guaranteed, Burstable, and BestEffort.
  • Prerequisites: Working cluster.
  • Cluster Environment: Any cluster.
  • Initial State: Namespace qos-lab.
  • Task Description:
    1. Create namespace qos-lab.
    2. Create Pod pod-guaranteed: Requests and Limits must be identical for both CPU and Memory (e.g. 100m / 128Mi).
    3. Create Pod pod-burstable: Request 100m CPU / 128Mi RAM, Limit 200m CPU / 256Mi RAM.
    4. Create Pod pod-besteffort: No requests and no limits defined.
    5. Inspect status.qosClass on all three pods.
  • Constraints: Do not use default namespace LimitRanges that might inject requests.
  • Hints: Guaranteed requires both CPU and RAM requests to equal limits on every container.
  • Expected Result: kubectl get pods shows Guaranteed, Burstable, and BestEffort respectively.
  • Verification:
    kubectl get pods -n qos-lab -o custom-columns=NAME:.metadata.name,QOS:.status.qosClass
  • Full Solution:
    kubectl create namespace qos-lab

    apiVersion: v1
kind: Pod
metadata:
  name: pod-guaranteed
  namespace: qos-lab
spec:
  containers:
  - name: c1
    image: busybox:1.36
    command: ["sleep", "3600"]
    resources:
      requests:
        cpu: 100m
        memory: 128Mi
      limits:
        cpu: 100m
        memory: 128Mi
---
apiVersion: v1
kind: Pod
metadata:
  name: pod-burstable
  namespace: qos-lab
spec:
  containers:
  - name: c1
    image: busybox:1.36
    command: ["sleep", "3600"]
    resources:
      requests:
        cpu: 100m
        memory: 128Mi
      limits:
        cpu: 200m
        memory: 256Mi
---
apiVersion: v1
kind: Pod
metadata:
  name: pod-besteffort
  namespace: qos-lab
spec:
  containers:
  - name: c1
    image: busybox:1.36
    command: ["sleep", "3600"]

    kubectl apply -f qos-pods.yaml
  • Common Mistakes: Defining requests and limits for CPU but omitting memory limits in the Guaranteed pod.
  • Troubleshooting: If status.qosClass is Burstable when aiming for Guaranteed, verify memory limits are explicitly set.
  • Cleanup Instructions:
    kubectl delete namespace qos-lab

LAB 25: Multi-Tier Health Checking (Startup, Readiness, Liveness)

  • Difficulty: Intermediate
  • Estimated Duration: 12 minutes
  • Domain: Workloads & Scheduling (15%)
  • Learning Objective: Configure a resilient Pod with startup, readiness, and liveness probes to prevent premature restarts and bad traffic routing.
  • Prerequisites: Working cluster.
  • Cluster Environment: Any cluster.
  • Initial State: Namespace probe-lab.
  • Task Description:
    1. Create namespace probe-lab.
    2. Deploy Pod robust-web running image nginx:1.27.
    3. Add a startupProbe checking HTTP path / on port 80 with failureThreshold: 30 and periodSeconds: 2.
    4. Add a readinessProbe checking HTTP path / on port 80 with initialDelaySeconds: 5 and periodSeconds: 5.
    5. Add a livenessProbe checking HTTP path / on port 80 with initialDelaySeconds: 10 and periodSeconds: 15.
    6. Expose the Pod with a ClusterIP Service and verify the endpoint is populated only after readiness passes.
  • Constraints: Do not use probe paths that return HTTP 404 or 500.
  • Hints: Startup probe protects slow apps; liveness probe detects deadlock.
  • Expected Result: Pod reaches 1/1 Running, readiness probe succeeds, endpoint exists.
  • Verification:
    kubectl describe pod robust-web -n probe-lab | grep -i probe
kubectl get endpoints robust-web-svc -n probe-lab
  • Full Solution:
    apiVersion: v1
kind: Pod
metadata:
  name: robust-web
  namespace: probe-lab
  labels:
    app: robust-web
spec:
  containers:
  - name: nginx
    image: nginx:1.27
    ports:
    - containerPort: 80
    startupProbe:
      httpGet:
        path: /
        port: 80
      failureThreshold: 30
      periodSeconds: 2
    readinessProbe:
      httpGet:
        path: /
        port: 80
      initialDelaySeconds: 5
      periodSeconds: 5
    livenessProbe:
      httpGet:
        path: /
        port: 80
      initialDelaySeconds: 10
      periodSeconds: 15
---
apiVersion: v1
kind: Service
metadata:
  name: robust-web-svc
  namespace: probe-lab
spec:
  selector:
    app: robust-web
  ports:
  - port: 80
    targetPort: 80

    kubectl create namespace probe-lab
kubectl apply -f robust-web.yaml
  • Common Mistakes: Setting port number incorrectly or testing a non-existent path like /healthz on vanilla NGINX.
  • Troubleshooting: Run kubectl describe pod robust-web -n probe-lab and look for Probe Failed events.
  • Cleanup Instructions:
    kubectl delete namespace probe-lab

LAB 26: Node Affinity with Required and Preferred Matching

  • Difficulty: Intermediate
  • Estimated Duration: 10 minutes
  • Domain: Workloads & Scheduling (15%)
  • Learning Objective: Author advanced Node Affinity rules combining hard (required) requirements with soft (preferred) weighted scoring.
  • Prerequisites: Multi-node cluster.
  • Cluster Environment: Multi-worker cluster.
  • Initial State: Label worker-1 with disktype=nvme and region=us-central. Label worker-2 with disktype=hdd and region=us-central.
  • Task Description:
    1. Label worker nodes as described.
    2. Deploy a Pod analytics-engine running alpine sleeping 3600.
    3. Configure Node Affinity:
      • requiredDuringSchedulingIgnoredDuringExecution: Must be in region=us-central.
      • preferredDuringSchedulingIgnoredDuringExecution: Prefer disktype=nvme with weight 100.
    4. Verify the Pod schedules onto worker-1 due to the preferred score.
  • Constraints: If worker-1 is deleted or cordoned, the Pod must still be capable of landing on worker-2.
  • Hints: Use nodeSelectorTerms for required and preferences for preferred.
  • Expected Result: Pod lands on worker-1.
  • Verification:
    kubectl get pod analytics-engine -o wide
# Node should be worker-1
  • Full Solution:
    kubectl label nodes worker-1 disktype=nvme region=us-central --overwrite
kubectl label nodes worker-2 disktype=hdd region=us-central --overwrite

    apiVersion: v1
kind: Pod
metadata:
  name: analytics-engine
spec:
  affinity:
    nodeAffinity:
      requiredDuringSchedulingIgnoredDuringExecution:
        nodeSelectorTerms:
        - matchExpressions:
          - key: region
            operator: In
            values:
            - us-central
      preferredDuringSchedulingIgnoredDuringExecution:
      - weight: 100
        preference:
          matchExpressions:
          - key: disktype
            operator: In
            values:
            - nvme
  containers:
  - name: engine
    image: alpine
    command: ["sleep", "3600"]

    kubectl apply -f affinity-pod.yaml
  • Common Mistakes: Misspelling requiredDuringSchedulingIgnoredDuringExecution.
  • Troubleshooting: Run kubectl describe pod analytics-engine if scheduling fails.
  • Cleanup Instructions:
    kubectl delete pod analytics-engine
kubectl label nodes worker-1 disktype- region-
kubectl label nodes worker-2 disktype- region-

LAB 27: Taints, Tolerations, and Node Eviction (NoExecute)

  • Difficulty: Intermediate
  • Estimated Duration: 12 minutes
  • Domain: Workloads & Scheduling (15%)
  • Learning Objective: Observe the eviction behavior of NoExecute taints versus NoSchedule, and use tolerationSeconds for graceful drain windows.
  • Prerequisites: Multi-node cluster.
  • Cluster Environment: Multi-worker cluster.
  • Initial State: Running Deployment stateless-app with 3 replicas on worker-1.
  • Task Description:
    1. Taint worker-1 with outage=maintenance:NoExecute.
    2. Observe that all non-tolerating pods on worker-1 are immediately evicted and recreated on other nodes.
    3. Deploy a Pod tolerant-pod that tolerates outage=maintenance:NoExecute with tolerationSeconds: 120.
    4. Verify tolerant-pod runs on worker-1 and remains alive for 2 minutes before eviction.
  • Constraints: Understand the difference between NoSchedule (prevents new pods) and NoExecute (kills running pods).
  • Hints: Trailing minus sign removes a taint: kubectl taint nodes <node> <key>:<effect>-.
  • Expected Result: NoExecute clears un-tolerating pods from the node.
  • Verification:
    kubectl get pods -o wide | grep worker-1
  • Full Solution:
    kubectl taint nodes worker-1 outage=maintenance:NoExecute

    apiVersion: v1
kind: Pod
metadata:
  name: tolerant-pod
spec:
  nodeName: worker-1
  tolerations:
  - key: "outage"
    operator: "Equal"
    value: "maintenance"
    effect: "NoExecute"
    tolerationSeconds: 120
  containers:
  - name: app
    image: alpine
    command: ["sleep", "3600"]

    kubectl apply -f tolerant-pod.yaml
# Remove taint after verification
kubectl taint nodes worker-1 outage=maintenance:NoExecute-
  • Common Mistakes: Applying NoSchedule and expecting running pods to be evicted (only NoExecute evicts).
  • Troubleshooting: Describe the node to verify active taints: kubectl describe node worker-1 | grep Taints.
  • Cleanup Instructions:
    kubectl delete pod tolerant-pod
kubectl taint nodes worker-1 outage- || true

LAB 28: Pod Anti-Affinity Across Topology Failure Domains

  • Difficulty: Intermediate
  • Estimated Duration: 10 minutes
  • Domain: Workloads & Scheduling (15%)
  • Learning Objective: Guarantee high availability by authoring strict podAntiAffinity rules spreading replicas across distinct physical host nodes.
  • Prerequisites: Multi-node cluster (at least 2 worker nodes).
  • Cluster Environment: Multi-worker cluster.
  • Initial State: Namespace ha-demo.
  • Task Description:
    1. Create namespace ha-demo.
    2. Deploy a Deployment redundant-api with 2 replicas in ha-demo.
    3. Image: nginx:1.27.
    4. Configure podAntiAffinity with requiredDuringSchedulingIgnoredDuringExecution matching app: redundant-api on topologyKey: kubernetes.io/hostname.
    5. Verify that each replica is scheduled on a completely different worker node.
    6. Scale the Deployment to 4 replicas (more than available worker nodes) and observe that surplus pods remain Pending.
  • Constraints: Must use hard requiredDuringSchedulingIgnoredDuringExecution.
  • Hints: The topologyKey kubernetes.io/hostname is standard for per-node spreading.
  • Expected Result: Exactly 1 pod per node; surplus pods show Pending.
  • Verification:
    kubectl get pods -n ha-demo -o wide
  • Full Solution:
    apiVersion: apps/v1
kind: Deployment
metadata:
  name: redundant-api
  namespace: ha-demo
spec:
  replicas: 2
  selector:
    matchLabels:
      app: redundant-api
  template:
    metadata:
      labels:
        app: redundant-api
    spec:
      affinity:
        podAntiAffinity:
          requiredDuringSchedulingIgnoredDuringExecution:
          - labelSelector:
              matchExpressions:
              - key: app
                operator: In
                values:
                - redundant-api
            topologyKey: "kubernetes.io/hostname"
      containers:
      - name: nginx
        image: nginx:1.27

    kubectl create namespace ha-demo
kubectl apply -f anti-affinity.yaml
kubectl scale deployment redundant-api --replicas=4 -n ha-demo
  • Common Mistakes: Omitting the matchLabels in the podAntiAffinity.labelSelector.
  • Troubleshooting: Run kubectl describe pod <pending-pod> -n ha-demo to see FailedScheduling: 0/2 nodes are available: 2 node(s) had untolerated taint or pod anti-affinity rules.
  • Cleanup Instructions:
    kubectl delete namespace ha-demo

LAB 29: Horizontal Pod Autoscaler (HPA) Tuning with Metrics Server

  • Difficulty: Intermediate
  • Estimated Duration: 12 minutes
  • Domain: Workloads & Scheduling (15%)
  • Learning Objective: Deploy and configure an HPA resource using autoscaling/v2 to autoscale a workload between 2 and 8 replicas based on CPU load.
  • Prerequisites: Metrics Server installed (kubectl top pods works).
  • Cluster Environment: Standard Kubernetes cluster with Metrics Server.
  • Initial State: Namespace scaling-lab.
  • Task Description:
    1. Create namespace scaling-lab.
    2. Deploy cpu-stress Deployment with 2 replicas of nginx:1.27, setting resources.requests.cpu: 50m and resources.limits.cpu: 100m.
    3. Expose Deployment with a Service cpu-stress-svc.
    4. Create an HPA cpu-stress-hpa targeting 60% CPU utilization with minReplicas 2 and maxReplicas 8.
    5. Inspect the HPA status and verify metrics are reading.
  • Constraints: Deployment must have resource requests declared or HPA will report <unknown>.
  • Hints: Use kubectl autoscale deployment cpu-stress --cpu-percent=60 --min=2 --max=8 -n scaling-lab.
  • Expected Result: HPA shows active target metrics (e.g. 0%/60%).
  • Verification:
    kubectl get hpa cpu-stress-hpa -n scaling-lab
  • Full Solution:
    apiVersion: apps/v1
kind: Deployment
metadata:
  name: cpu-stress
  namespace: scaling-lab
spec:
  replicas: 2
  selector:
    matchLabels:
      app: cpu-stress
  template:
    metadata:
      labels:
        app: cpu-stress
    spec:
      containers:
      - name: nginx
        image: nginx:1.27
        resources:
          requests:
            cpu: 50m
          limits:
            cpu: 100m
---
apiVersion: autoscaling/v2
kind: HorizontalPodAutoscaler
metadata:
  name: cpu-stress-hpa
  namespace: scaling-lab
spec:
  scaleTargetRef:
    apiVersion: apps/v1
    kind: Deployment
    name: cpu-stress
  minReplicas: 2
  maxReplicas: 8
  metrics:
  - type: Resource
    resource:
      name: cpu
      target:
        type: Utilization
        averageUtilization: 60

    kubectl create namespace scaling-lab
kubectl apply -f hpa-lab.yaml
  • Common Mistakes: Forgetting to define resources.requests.cpu on the Deployment containers.
  • Troubleshooting: If HPA reports <unknown>/60%, wait 60 seconds for metrics-server scrape interval, or check kubectl top pods -n scaling-lab.
  • Cleanup Instructions:
    kubectl delete namespace scaling-lab

LAB 30: Namespace ResourceQuotas and Container LimitRanges

  • Difficulty: Intermediate
  • Estimated Duration: 12 minutes
  • Domain: Workloads & Scheduling (15%)
  • Learning Objective: Configure interlocking ResourceQuota and LimitRange policies to enforce namespace resource caps while preventing missing-request admission rejections.
  • Prerequisites: Working cluster.
  • Cluster Environment: Any cluster.
  • Initial State: Namespace dev-sandbox.
  • Task Description:
    1. Create namespace dev-sandbox.
    2. Create a ResourceQuota named compute-quota capping total CPU requests at 1, CPU limits at 2, and maximum Pods at 4.
    3. Create a LimitRange named container-defaults setting default request to 100m CPU / 128Mi RAM and default limit to 200m CPU / 256Mi RAM.
    4. Launch a Pod without specifying requests/limits (kubectl run test-pod --image=nginx:alpine -n dev-sandbox).
    5. Verify the Pod is accepted and has the default limits injected by the LimitRange.
    6. Verify quota usage reflects the new pod.
  • Constraints: Do not allow unconstrained pods in dev-sandbox.
  • Hints: Without the LimitRange, kubectl run fails immediately against a ResourceQuota!
  • Expected Result: test-pod runs, and kubectl describe quota -n dev-sandbox shows 1/4 pods used.
  • Verification:
    kubectl describe quota compute-quota -n dev-sandbox
kubectl get pod test-pod -n dev-sandbox -o jsonpath='{.spec.containers[0].resources}'
  • Full Solution:
    apiVersion: v1
kind: Namespace
metadata:
  name: dev-sandbox
---
apiVersion: v1
kind: ResourceQuota
metadata:
  name: compute-quota
  namespace: dev-sandbox
spec:
  hard:
    requests.cpu: "1"
    limits.cpu: "2"
    pods: "4"
---
apiVersion: v1
kind: LimitRange
metadata:
  name: container-defaults
  namespace: dev-sandbox
spec:
  limits:
  - default:
      cpu: 200m
      memory: 256Mi
    defaultRequest:
      cpu: 100m
      memory: 128Mi
    type: Container

    kubectl apply -f quota-limit.yaml
kubectl run test-pod --image=nginx:alpine -n dev-sandbox
  • Common Mistakes: Applying a ResourceQuota without a LimitRange, causing subsequent developer deployments without explicit resource blocks to fail admission.
  • Troubleshooting: Run kubectl describe quota compute-quota -n dev-sandbox to see available vs used headroom.
  • Cleanup Instructions:
    kubectl delete namespace dev-sandbox

VOLUME II: THE CKA PRACTICAL WORKBOOK

DOMAIN 3: SERVICES & NETWORKING (LABS 31–45)

LAB 31: ClusterIP Service Creation and Endpoint Verification

  • Difficulty: Beginner
  • Estimated Duration: 8 minutes
  • Domain: Services & Networking (20%)
  • Learning Objective: Create a ClusterIP Service to expose a backend Deployment and verify automatic endpoint generation.
  • Prerequisites: Running cluster.
  • Cluster Environment: Standard Kubernetes cluster.
  • Initial State: Clean namespace networking-lab.
  • Task Description:
    1. Create namespace networking-lab.
    2. Deploy a Deployment backend-api with 3 replicas of nginx:1.27 in networking-lab.
    3. Create a ClusterIP Service named backend-svc exposing port 80 targeting containerPort 80.
    4. Inspect the Service endpoints and confirm all 3 Pod IPs appear in the endpoints list.
    5. Test internal connectivity to backend-svc.networking-lab.svc.cluster.local using a curl test pod.
  • Constraints: Service must be of type ClusterIP.
  • Hints: Use kubectl expose deployment backend-api --name=backend-svc --port=80 --target-port=80 -n networking-lab.
  • Expected Result: backend-svc resolves internally and load balances across all 3 backend pods.
  • Verification:
    kubectl get endpoints backend-svc -n networking-lab
kubectl run curl-test --image=curlimages/curl --rm -it --restart=Never -n networking-lab -- http://backend-svc
  • Full Solution:
    kubectl create namespace networking-lab
kubectl create deployment backend-api --image=nginx:1.27 --replicas=3 -n networking-lab
kubectl expose deployment backend-api --name=backend-svc --port=80 --target-port=80 -n networking-lab
  • Common Mistakes: Typo in the selector labels causing empty endpoints (<none>).
  • Troubleshooting: Compare kubectl get svc backend-svc -n networking-lab -o yaml with kubectl get pods -n networking-lab --show-labels.
  • Cleanup Instructions:
    kubectl delete namespace networking-lab

LAB 32: NodePort Service Configuration on Static Ports

  • Difficulty: Beginner
  • Estimated Duration: 8 minutes
  • Domain: Services & Networking (20%)
  • Learning Objective: Expose an application externally on a specific host NodePort within the range 30000–32767.
  • Prerequisites: Multi-node or local cluster with port forwarding.
  • Cluster Environment: Any cluster.
  • Initial State: Deployment web-portal exists in default.
  • Task Description:
    1. Create Deployment web-portal with 2 replicas of nginx:1.27.
    2. Create a NodePort Service named web-portal-external with:
      • port: 80
      • targetPort: 80
      • nodePort: 32080
    3. Query the NodePort on the worker node's IP using curl to verify accessibility.
  • Constraints: nodePort must be explicitly set to 32080.
  • Hints: NodePort range is 30000–32767.
  • Expected Result: HTTP 200 OK received when curling http://<node-ip>:32080.
  • Verification:
    NODE_IP=$(kubectl get nodes -o jsonpath='{.items[0].status.addresses[?(@.type=="InternalIP")].address}')
curl -I http://${NODE_IP}:32080
  • Full Solution:
    apiVersion: apps/v1
kind: Deployment
metadata:
  name: web-portal
spec:
  replicas: 2
  selector:
    matchLabels:
      app: web-portal
  template:
    metadata:
      labels:
        app: web-portal
    spec:
      containers:
      - name: nginx
        image: nginx:1.27
---
apiVersion: v1
kind: Service
metadata:
  name: web-portal-external
spec:
  type: NodePort
  selector:
    app: web-portal
  ports:
  - port: 80
    targetPort: 80
    nodePort: 32080

    kubectl apply -f nodeport-lab.yaml
  • Common Mistakes: Specifying a nodePort below 30000 or above 32767, which fails API validation.
  • Troubleshooting: Check if host firewall (ufw or iptables) is blocking traffic on port 32080.
  • Cleanup Instructions:
    kubectl delete -f nodeport-lab.yaml

LAB 33: Headless Service for Stateful Database Peer Discovery

  • Difficulty: Intermediate
  • Estimated Duration: 10 minutes
  • Domain: Services & Networking (20%)
  • Learning Objective: Create a Headless Service (clusterIP: None) and observe DNS A-record generation for individual StatefulSet Pods.
  • Prerequisites: StatefulSet running.
  • Cluster Environment: Any cluster.
  • Initial State: Namespace cluster-db.
  • Task Description:
    1. Create namespace cluster-db.
    2. Create a headless Service db-discovery with clusterIP: None in cluster-db targeting role: datastore.
    3. Deploy a StatefulSet datastore with 2 replicas using redis:7.2-alpine attached to serviceName: db-discovery.
    4. Launch an interactive test pod with nslookup to verify that db-discovery.cluster-db.svc.cluster.local returns direct IPs for both datastore-0 and datastore-1.
    5. Verify direct lookup for datastore-0.db-discovery.cluster-db.svc.cluster.local.
  • Constraints: spec.clusterIP must be set to None.
  • Hints: Headless services bypass kube-proxy load balancing entirely.
  • Expected Result: DNS returns individual pod IP records.
  • Verification:
    kubectl run dns-test --image=busybox:1.36 --rm -it --restart=Never -n cluster-db -- nslookup db-discovery
  • Full Solution:
    apiVersion: v1
kind: Namespace
metadata:
  name: cluster-db
---
apiVersion: v1
kind: Service
metadata:
  name: db-discovery
  namespace: cluster-db
spec:
  clusterIP: None
  selector:
    role: datastore
  ports:
  - port: 6379
    name: redis
---
apiVersion: apps/v1
kind: StatefulSet
metadata:
  name: datastore
  namespace: cluster-db
spec:
  serviceName: db-discovery
  replicas: 2
  selector:
    matchLabels:
      role: datastore
  template:
    metadata:
      labels:
        role: datastore
    spec:
      containers:
      - name: redis
        image: redis:7.2-alpine

    kubectl apply -f headless-db.yaml
  • Common Mistakes: Missing serviceName in StatefulSet spec, which breaks individual pod DNS records.
  • Troubleshooting: Run kubectl get endpoints db-discovery -n cluster-db to verify registered endpoints.
  • Cleanup Instructions:
    kubectl delete namespace cluster-db

LAB 34: EndpointSlice Inspection and Manual Endpoint Injection

  • Difficulty: Intermediate
  • Estimated Duration: 10 minutes
  • Domain: Services & Networking (20%)
  • Learning Objective: Create a Service without selectors and manually attach an external IP address via Endpoints and EndpointSlices.
  • Prerequisites: Working cluster.
  • Cluster Environment: Any cluster.
  • Initial State: External legacy database at 192.168.1.50:3306.
  • Task Description:
    1. Create a Service named external-mysql with no spec.selector on port 3306 targeting port 3306.
    2. Create an Endpoints object with the exact same name external-mysql pointing to IP 192.168.1.50:3306.
    3. Verify that Kubernetes automatically generates a matching EndpointSlice for the Service.
  • Constraints: Do not use spec.selector on the Service.
  • Hints: When a Service omits selector, Kubernetes does not create endpoints automatically, allowing manual declaration.
  • Expected Result: kubectl describe svc external-mysql lists Endpoints: 192.168.1.50:3306.
  • Verification:
    kubectl get endpoints external-mysql
kubectl get endpointslices -l kubernetes.io/service-name=external-mysql
  • Full Solution:
    apiVersion: v1
kind: Service
metadata:
  name: external-mysql
spec:
  ports:
  - port: 3306
    targetPort: 3306
---
apiVersion: v1
kind: Endpoints
metadata:
  name: external-mysql
subsets:
- addresses:
  - ip: 192.168.1.50
  ports:
  - port: 3306

    kubectl apply -f manual-endpoints.yaml
  • Common Mistakes: Naming the Endpoints object differently than the Service object. They must match exactly!
  • Troubleshooting: Run kubectl describe endpoints external-mysql to check address format.
  • Cleanup Instructions:
    kubectl delete svc external-mysql
kubectl delete endpoints external-mysql

LAB 35: CoreDNS Resolution Triage and Custom Upstream Forwarding

  • Difficulty: Intermediate
  • Estimated Duration: 12 minutes
  • Domain: Services & Networking (20%)
  • Learning Objective: Modify the CoreDNS ConfigMap Corefile to add custom upstream DNS forwarders and verify cache behavior.
  • Prerequisites: Cluster admin access.
  • Cluster Environment: Any cluster.
  • Initial State: Default CoreDNS ConfigMap in kube-system.
  • Task Description:
    1. Backup the existing coredns ConfigMap in kube-system to /tmp/coredns-backup.yaml.
    2. Edit coredns ConfigMap to configure upstream DNS forwarder forward . 8.8.8.8 1.1.1.1.
    3. Restart CoreDNS pods to pick up the updated Corefile.
    4. Launch a test pod and verify resolution of an external domain (e.g., google.com) and internal Service (kubernetes.default).
  • Constraints: Do not corrupt the Corefile syntax.
  • Hints: CoreDNS automatically reloads within 30 seconds, or restart pods with kubectl rollout restart deployment coredns -n kube-system.
  • Expected Result: DNS resolution succeeds for both internal and external queries.
  • Verification:
    kubectl run dns-check --image=busybox:1.36 --rm -it --restart=Never -- nslookup google.com
kubectl run dns-check --image=busybox:1.36 --rm -it --restart=Never -- nslookup kubernetes.default
  • Full Solution:
    kubectl get configmap coredns -n kube-system -o yaml > /tmp/coredns-backup.yaml
# Modify forward line in Corefile to: forward . 8.8.8.8 1.1.1.1
kubectl edit configmap coredns -n kube-system
kubectl rollout restart deployment coredns -n kube-system
kubectl rollout status deployment coredns -n kube-system
  • Common Mistakes: Introducing syntax errors in Corefile curly braces {} causing CoreDNS crash loop.
  • Troubleshooting: Check logs: kubectl logs -n kube-system -l k8s-app=kube-dns.
  • Cleanup Instructions:
    kubectl apply -f /tmp/coredns-backup.yaml
kubectl rollout restart deployment coredns -n kube-system

LAB 36: Ingress Controller Routing with Path-Based Dispatching

  • Difficulty: Intermediate
  • Estimated Duration: 12 minutes
  • Domain: Services & Networking (20%)
  • Learning Objective: Author an Ingress resource with pathType: Prefix dispatching traffic across two independent microservices.
  • Prerequisites: Ingress Controller running.
  • Cluster Environment: Standard cluster with ingress-nginx.
  • Initial State: Namespace ecommerce.
  • Task Description:
    1. Create namespace ecommerce.
    2. Deploy catalog-api (1 replica, image nginx:1.27, port 80) and expose with Service catalog-svc.
    3. Deploy orders-api (1 replica, image httpd:alpine, port 80) and expose with Service orders-svc.
    4. Create an Ingress shop-ingress in ecommerce using ingressClassName: nginx matching host shop.example.local:
      • Path /catalog $\to$ catalog-svc:80
      • Path /orders $\to$ orders-svc:80
    5. Test URL dispatching with curl using the Host header.
  • Constraints: Use pathType: Prefix.
  • Hints: Pass --header "Host: shop.example.local" to curl.
  • Expected Result: Traffic to /catalog reaches catalog-api; traffic to /orders reaches orders-api.
  • Verification:
    kubectl get ingress shop-ingress -n ecommerce
  • Full Solution:
    apiVersion: networking.k8s.io/v1
kind: Ingress
metadata:
  name: shop-ingress
  namespace: ecommerce
  annotations:
    nginx.ingress.kubernetes.io/rewrite-target: /
spec:
  ingressClassName: nginx
  rules:
  - host: shop.example.local
    http:
      paths:
      - path: /catalog
        pathType: Prefix
        backend:
          service:
            name: catalog-svc
            port:
              number: 80
      - path: /orders
        pathType: Prefix
        backend:
          service:
            name: orders-svc
            port:
              number: 80

    kubectl create namespace ecommerce
kubectl create deployment catalog-api --image=nginx:1.27 -n ecommerce
kubectl expose deployment catalog-api --name=catalog-svc --port=80 -n ecommerce
kubectl create deployment orders-api --image=httpd:alpine -n ecommerce
kubectl expose deployment orders-api --name=orders-svc --port=80 -n ecommerce
kubectl apply -f ingress-lab.yaml
  • Common Mistakes: Omitting the rewrite-target annotation when backend containers expect / root paths.
  • Troubleshooting: Run kubectl describe ingress shop-ingress -n ecommerce to verify backend endpoints.
  • Cleanup Instructions:
    kubectl delete namespace ecommerce

LAB 37: Ingress TLS Termination with Cryptographic Secrets

  • Difficulty: Intermediate
  • Estimated Duration: 12 minutes
  • Domain: Services & Networking (20%)
  • Learning Objective: Generate a self-signed TLS certificate pair, create a kubernetes.io/tls Secret, and configure Ingress SSL termination.
  • Prerequisites: OpenSSL installed on workstation.
  • Cluster Environment: Ingress-enabled cluster.
  • Initial State: Service secure-portal-svc running in default.
  • Task Description:
    1. Generate a self-signed TLS cert and private key for domain portal.company.com:
      • tls.crt and tls.key.
    2. Create a TLS Secret named portal-tls-secret from the cert and key.
    3. Create an Ingress resource secure-portal-ingress terminating TLS for portal.company.com using portal-tls-secret.
    4. Verify that HTTPS requests to the Ingress controller succeed.
  • Constraints: Secret must be of type kubernetes.io/tls.
  • Hints: Use kubectl create secret tls portal-tls-secret --cert=tls.crt --key=tls.key.
  • Expected Result: Ingress terminates TLS cleanly on port 443.
  • Verification:
    kubectl get secret portal-tls-secret -o yaml
kubectl describe ingress secure-portal-ingress | grep -A 5 TLS
  • Full Solution:
    # 1. Generate cert
openssl req -x509 -nodes -days 365 -newkey rsa:2048 \
  -keyout /tmp/tls.key -out /tmp/tls.crt \
  -subj "/CN=portal.company.com/O=Company"

# 2. Create Secret
kubectl create secret tls portal-tls-secret --cert=/tmp/tls.crt --key=/tmp/tls.key

# 3. Create Ingress
cat <<EOF | kubectl apply -f -
apiVersion: networking.k8s.io/v1
kind: Ingress
metadata:
  name: secure-portal-ingress
spec:
  ingressClassName: nginx
  tls:
  - hosts:
    - portal.company.com
    secretName: portal-tls-secret
  rules:
  - host: portal.company.com
    http:
      paths:
      - path: /
        pathType: Prefix
        backend:
          service:
