            name: web-portal-svc
            port:
              number: 80
EOF
  • Common Mistakes: Creating a generic secret instead of a TLS secret (kubectl create secret generic lacks required data fields tls.crt and tls.key).
  • Troubleshooting: Verify Secret keys with kubectl describe secret portal-tls-secret.
  • Cleanup Instructions:
    kubectl delete ingress secure-portal-ingress
kubectl delete secret portal-tls-secret
rm -f /tmp/tls.key /tmp/tls.crt

LAB 38: Next-Gen Traffic Management with Gateway API (GatewayClass & Gateway)

  • Difficulty: Advanced
  • Estimated Duration: 15 minutes
  • Domain: Services & Networking (20%)
  • Learning Objective: Inspect available GatewayClasses and deploy a modern Gateway resource declaring HTTP and HTTPS entry point listeners.
  • Prerequisites: Gateway API CRDs installed on cluster.
  • Cluster Environment: Standard Kubernetes v1.31+ cluster.
  • Initial State: GatewayClass cilium or envoy installed.
  • Task Description:
    1. Verify installed GatewayClasses using kubectl get gatewayclasses.
    2. Create namespace gateway-infra.
    3. Author a Gateway resource named edge-gateway in namespace gateway-infra.
    4. Configure two listeners:
      • http-listener: port 80, protocol HTTP, allowedRoutes from all namespaces.
      • https-listener: port 443, protocol HTTPS, allowedRoutes from same namespace.
    5. Inspect the Gateway status conditions to verify it transitions to Programmed: True.
  • Constraints: Must use gateway.networking.k8s.io/v1.
  • Hints: Check kubectl explain gateway.spec.listeners.
  • Expected Result: edge-gateway is created and reports Programmed condition.
  • Verification:
    kubectl get gateway edge-gateway -n gateway-infra
  • Full Solution:
    apiVersion: gateway.networking.k8s.io/v1
kind: Gateway
metadata:
  name: edge-gateway
  namespace: gateway-infra
spec:
  gatewayClassName: eg-gateway-class
  listeners:
  - name: http-listener
    port: 80
    protocol: HTTP
    allowedRoutes:
      namespaces:
        from: All
  - name: https-listener
    port: 443
    protocol: HTTPS
    allowedRoutes:
      namespaces:
        from: Same
    tls:
      mode: Terminate
      certificateRefs:
      - name: edge-tls-cert

    kubectl create namespace gateway-infra
kubectl apply -f gateway.yaml
  • Common Mistakes: Referencing a non-existent gatewayClassName.
  • Troubleshooting: Run kubectl describe gateway edge-gateway -n gateway-infra to check listener validation errors.
  • Cleanup Instructions:
    kubectl delete namespace gateway-infra

LAB 39: Gateway API HTTPRoute with Weighted Traffic Splitting

  • Difficulty: Advanced
  • Estimated Duration: 15 minutes
  • Domain: Services & Networking (20%)
  • Learning Objective: Implement a canary release using Gateway API HTTPRoute with a 90/10 weighted backend traffic split.
  • Prerequisites: Gateway resource prod-gateway running.
  • Cluster Environment: Gateway API enabled cluster.
  • Initial State: Namespace canary-demo with Services web-v1-svc and web-v2-svc.
  • Task Description:
    1. In namespace canary-demo, create two Deployments: web-v1 (image nginx:1.26) and web-v2 (image nginx:1.27).
    2. Expose both with ClusterIP Services web-v1-svc and web-v2-svc on port 80.
    3. Create an HTTPRoute named web-canary-route attaching to parent Gateway prod-gateway.
    4. Configure path / matching hostname app.company.internal.
    5. Split traffic: 90% weight to web-v1-svc, 10% weight to web-v2-svc.
    6. Verify route status condition shows Accepted: True.
  • Constraints: Do not use legacy Ingress annotations.
  • Hints: Use spec.rules[*].backendRefs[*].weight.
  • Expected Result: HTTPRoute distributes traffic across backends proportionally to weights.
  • Verification:
    kubectl describe httproute web-canary-route -n canary-demo | grep -A 8 Rules
  • Full Solution:
    apiVersion: gateway.networking.k8s.io/v1
kind: HTTPRoute
metadata:
  name: web-canary-route
  namespace: canary-demo
spec:
  parentRefs:
  - name: prod-gateway
  hostnames:
  - "app.company.internal"
  rules:
  - matches:
    - path:
        type: PathPrefix
        value: /
    backendRefs:
    - name: web-v1-svc
      port: 80
      weight: 90
    - name: web-v2-svc
      port: 80
      weight: 10

    kubectl apply -f canary-route.yaml
  • Common Mistakes: Summing weights to something other than 100 or using negative integers.
  • Troubleshooting: Run kubectl get httproute web-canary-route -n canary-demo -o yaml and check status.parents.
  • Cleanup Instructions:
    kubectl delete namespace canary-demo

LAB 40: Gateway API Cross-Namespace Route Attachment

  • Difficulty: Advanced
  • Estimated Duration: 12 minutes
  • Domain: Services & Networking (20%)
  • Learning Objective: Securely attach an HTTPRoute residing in namespace apps to a centralized Gateway in namespace infra using allowedRoutes.
  • Prerequisites: Multi-namespace Gateway API setup.
  • Cluster Environment: Gateway API enabled cluster.
  • Initial State: Namespaces infra and apps exist.
  • Task Description:
    1. In namespace infra, create Gateway shared-gw whose HTTP listener explicitly allows routes from namespaces with label tenant: allowed.
    2. Label namespace apps with tenant: allowed.
    3. In namespace apps, create HTTPRoute named tenant-route referencing parent Gateway shared-gw in namespace infra.
    4. Verify the route is accepted by the gateway.
  • Constraints: Route attachment must be rejected if the namespace label is removed.
  • Hints: Use allowedRoutes.namespaces.from: Selector on the Gateway listener.
  • Expected Result: tenant-route shows Accepted: True in conditions.
  • Verification:
    kubectl describe httproute tenant-route -n apps | grep "Accepted:"
  • Full Solution:
    kubectl create namespace infra
kubectl create namespace apps
kubectl label namespace apps tenant=allowed

    apiVersion: gateway.networking.k8s.io/v1
kind: Gateway
metadata:
  name: shared-gw
  namespace: infra
spec:
  gatewayClassName: eg-gateway-class
  listeners:
  - name: http
    port: 80
    protocol: HTTP
    allowedRoutes:
      namespaces:
        from: Selector
        selector:
          matchLabels:
            tenant: allowed
---
apiVersion: gateway.networking.k8s.io/v1
kind: HTTPRoute
metadata:
  name: tenant-route
  namespace: apps
spec:
  parentRefs:
  - name: shared-gw
    namespace: infra
  rules:
  - backendRefs:
    - name: app-svc
      port: 80
  • Common Mistakes: Forgetting parentRefs[*].namespace: infra when attaching from a foreign namespace.
  • Troubleshooting: If status is Refused, check namespace label with kubectl get ns apps --show-labels.
  • Cleanup Instructions:
    kubectl delete namespace infra apps

LAB 41: Default-Deny All Ingress NetworkPolicy Enforcement

  • Difficulty: Intermediate
  • Estimated Duration: 10 minutes
  • Domain: Services & Networking (20%)
  • Learning Objective: Lock down an entire namespace by applying a default-deny Ingress NetworkPolicy, and observe immediate traffic drop.
  • Prerequisites: Cluster with policy-enforcing CNI (Calico or Cilium).
  • Cluster Environment: NetworkPolicy capable cluster.
  • Initial State: Namespace isolated-ns with a working NGINX pod.
  • Task Description:
    1. Create namespace isolated-ns.
    2. Deploy Pod web (nginx:1.27) and expose with Service web-svc in isolated-ns.
    3. Verify a curl pod in default can reach web-svc.isolated-ns.
    4. Author and apply a NetworkPolicy default-deny-ingress in isolated-ns selecting all pods (podSelector: {}) with policyTypes: [Ingress].
    5. Verify that subsequent curls from default immediately hang and time out.
  • Constraints: Do not block egress traffic.
  • Hints: podSelector: {} acts as a wildcard matching all pods in that namespace.
  • Expected Result: Incoming connections are dropped at the CNI layer.
  • Verification:
    kubectl run test-curl --image=curlimages/curl --rm -it --restart=Never -- --connect-timeout 3 http://web-svc.isolated-ns
# Expected: Connection timed out
  • Full Solution:
    apiVersion: networking.k8s.io/v1
kind: NetworkPolicy
metadata:
  name: default-deny-ingress
  namespace: isolated-ns
spec:
  podSelector: {}
  policyTypes:
  - Ingress

    kubectl create namespace isolated-ns
kubectl run web --image=nginx:1.27 -n isolated-ns --port=80
kubectl expose pod web --name=web-svc --port=80 -n isolated-ns
kubectl apply -f default-deny.yaml
  • Common Mistakes: Testing on a cluster running Flannel without Calico, where NetworkPolicies are silently ignored.
  • Troubleshooting: Run kubectl describe netpol default-deny-ingress -n isolated-ns to confirm Allowing ingress traffic: <none>.
  • Cleanup Instructions:
    kubectl delete namespace isolated-ns

LAB 42: Inter-Pod Ingress Filtering with podSelector and namespaceSelector

  • Difficulty: Intermediate
  • Estimated Duration: 12 minutes
  • Domain: Services & Networking (20%)
  • Learning Objective: Implement a fine-grained Ingress rule allowing traffic to database pods strictly from frontend pods in a production namespace.
  • Prerequisites: NetworkPolicy capable CNI.
  • Cluster Environment: Any test cluster.
  • Initial State: Namespaces frontend-ns and backend-ns.
  • Task Description:
    1. Create namespace frontend-ns and label it env: prod.
    2. Create namespace backend-ns.
    3. In backend-ns, run Pod db with label app: db on port 5432.
    4. Author a NetworkPolicy allow-frontend-to-db in backend-ns:
      • Targets pods labeled app: db.
      • Allows ingress on TCP port 5432 strictly from pods labeled app: frontend residing in namespaces labeled env: prod.
    5. Verify pods with other labels cannot connect.
  • Constraints: Enforce both namespace and pod label constraints simultaneously (AND logic).
  • Hints: Put namespaceSelector and podSelector under the SAME array dash - in from:.
  • Expected Result: Only matching pods can establish TCP connections on port 5432.
  • Verification:
    kubectl describe netpol allow-frontend-to-db -n backend-ns
  • Full Solution:
    apiVersion: networking.k8s.io/v1
kind: NetworkPolicy
metadata:
  name: allow-frontend-to-db
  namespace: backend-ns
spec:
  podSelector:
    matchLabels:
      app: db
  policyTypes:
  - Ingress
  ingress:
  - from:
    - namespaceSelector:
        matchLabels:
          env: prod
      podSelector:
        matchLabels:
          app: frontend
    ports:
    - protocol: TCP
      port: 5432

    kubectl create namespace frontend-ns
kubectl label namespace frontend-ns env=prod
kubectl create namespace backend-ns
kubectl apply -f netpol-db.yaml
  • Common Mistakes: Putting namespaceSelector and podSelector on separate dashes, converting the rule into a loose OR condition.
  • Troubleshooting: Verify namespace labels with kubectl get ns --show-labels.
  • Cleanup Instructions:
    kubectl delete namespace frontend-ns backend-ns

LAB 43: Restricting Outbound Egress with Port 53 DNS Whitelisting

  • Difficulty: Advanced
  • Estimated Duration: 12 minutes
  • Domain: Services & Networking (20%)
  • Learning Objective: Restrict egress traffic for secure pods while explicitly allowing UDP/TCP port 53 to CoreDNS to prevent broken name resolution.
  • Prerequisites: NetworkPolicy capable CNI.
  • Cluster Environment: Standard cluster.
  • Initial State: Namespace restricted-apps.
  • Task Description:
    1. Create namespace restricted-apps.
    2. Deploy Pod secure-worker in restricted-apps.
    3. Author a NetworkPolicy restrict-egress that:
      • Applies to all pods in restricted-apps.
      • Allows egress strictly to IP range 10.0.0.0/16.
      • Explicitly permits egress to CoreDNS on port 53 (UDP and TCP).
    4. Verify that DNS resolution continues to work inside secure-worker.
  • Constraints: Do not use policyTypes: [Egress] without port 53 allowance.
  • Hints: Allow egress to namespaceSelector: {} on port 53 to reach kube-system CoreDNS.
  • Expected Result: nslookup succeeds inside secure-worker, but outbound curls to public internet fail.
  • Verification:
    kubectl exec -it secure-worker -n restricted-apps -- nslookup kubernetes.default
# Expected: Resolves successfully
  • Full Solution:
    apiVersion: networking.k8s.io/v1
kind: NetworkPolicy
metadata:
  name: restrict-egress
  namespace: restricted-apps
spec:
  podSelector: {}
  policyTypes:
  - Egress
  egress:
  - to:
    - ipBlock:
        cidr: 10.0.0.0/16
  - to:
    - namespaceSelector: {}
    ports:
    - protocol: UDP
      port: 53
    - protocol: TCP
      port: 53

    kubectl create namespace restricted-apps
kubectl run secure-worker --image=busybox:1.36 -n restricted-apps -- sleep 3600
kubectl apply -f egress-netpol.yaml
  • Common Mistakes: Forgetting that CoreDNS also listens on TCP port 53 for large query responses.
  • Troubleshooting: If DNS fails, test by curling the CoreDNS ClusterIP directly to see if packets are dropped.
  • Cleanup Instructions:
    kubectl delete namespace restricted-apps

LAB 44: External IP CIDR Ingress Filtering using ipBlock

  • Difficulty: Intermediate
  • Estimated Duration: 10 minutes
  • Domain: Services & Networking (20%)
  • Learning Objective: Author an ipBlock NetworkPolicy rule allowing traffic from a corporate subnet while carving out an exception CIDR block.
  • Prerequisites: NetworkPolicy capable CNI.
  • Cluster Environment: Any cluster.
  • Initial State: Namespace corporate-network.
  • Task Description:
    1. Create namespace corporate-network.
    2. Deploy Pod internal-api in corporate-network listening on port 80.
    3. Create NetworkPolicy allow-corp-cidr selecting internal-api:
      • Allow Ingress from CIDR 192.168.0.0/16.
      • Except sub-block 192.168.100.0/24 (untrusted DMZ).
      • Port: TCP 80.
    4. Inspect the resulting policy with kubectl describe.
  • Constraints: Use ipBlock with except.
  • Hints: except must be an array of CIDRs contained within the parent cidr.
  • Expected Result: Policy is accepted and displays allowed CIDRs and excluded subnets.
  • Verification:
    kubectl describe netpol allow-corp-cidr -n corporate-network
  • Full Solution:
    apiVersion: networking.k8s.io/v1
kind: NetworkPolicy
metadata:
  name: allow-corp-cidr
  namespace: corporate-network
spec:
  podSelector:
    matchLabels:
      app: internal-api
  policyTypes:
  - Ingress
  ingress:
  - from:
    - ipBlock:
        cidr: 192.168.0.0/16
        except:
        - 192.168.100.0/24
    ports:
    - protocol: TCP
      port: 80

    kubectl create namespace corporate-network
kubectl run internal-api --image=nginx:alpine -n corporate-network -l app=internal-api
kubectl apply -f ipblock-netpol.yaml
  • Common Mistakes: Placing an except CIDR that is not a mathematical subnet of the outer cidr.
  • Troubleshooting: Validate YAML syntax with kubectl apply --dry-run=client -f ipblock-netpol.yaml.
  • Cleanup Instructions:
    kubectl delete namespace corporate-network

LAB 45: Live Packet Tracing with Ephemeral Debug Pods and Netshoot

  • Difficulty: Advanced
  • Estimated Duration: 12 minutes
  • Domain: Services & Networking (20%)
  • Learning Objective: Deploy an ephemeral troubleshooting container (nicolaka/netshoot) into a target Pod's network namespace to trace live TCP packets with tcpdump.
  • Prerequisites: Cluster with EphemeralContainers enabled (standard in v1.25+).
  • Cluster Environment: Any cluster.
  • Initial State: Running Pod web-target in default.
  • Task Description:
    1. Deploy Pod web-target running nginx:1.27.
    2. Use kubectl debug to attach an ephemeral container running image nicolaka/netshoot into web-target sharing its network namespace.
    3. Inside the debug container, run tcpdump -i eth0 -nn -s0 -v port 80.
    4. From another terminal, generate traffic to web-target.
    5. Observe the live SYN, SYN-ACK, and ACK handshake in the tcpdump output.
  • Constraints: Do not modify the original Pod specification or restart the container.
  • Hints: Use kubectl debug -it web-target --image=nicolaka/netshoot --target=web-target.
  • Expected Result: Live packet capture displays TCP conversation streams.
  • Verification:
    # Observed output inside debug container:
# IP client.38290 > web-target.80: Flags [S], seq ...
  • Full Solution:
    kubectl run web-target --image=nginx:1.27
# In Terminal 1:
kubectl debug -it web-target --image=nicolaka/netshoot -- /bin/bash
# Inside netshoot shell:
tcpdump -i any port 80 -n

# In Terminal 2:
POD_IP=$(kubectl get pod web-target -o jsonpath='{.status.podIP}')
kubectl run temp-curl --image=curlimages/curl --rm -it --restart=Never -- http://${POD_IP}
  • Common Mistakes: Omitting the ephemeral container image, defaulting to alpine without networking tools.
  • Troubleshooting: If kubectl debug fails, check if the cluster has RBAC permission for subresource pods/ephemeralcontainers.
  • Cleanup Instructions:
    kubectl delete pod web-target

VOLUME II: THE CKA PRACTICAL WORKBOOK

DOMAIN 4: STORAGE (LABS 46–53)

LAB 46: Static PersistentVolume and PVC 1-to-1 Binding Lifecycle

  • Difficulty: Beginner
  • Estimated Duration: 8 minutes
  • Domain: Storage (10%)
  • Learning Objective: Author a static PersistentVolume and a matching PersistentVolumeClaim, observing exclusive 1-to-1 binding and capacity allocation.
  • Prerequisites: Running cluster with host filesystem access.
  • Cluster Environment: Standard Kubernetes cluster.
  • Initial State: Directory /mnt/data/lab46 created on host node.
  • Task Description:
    1. Author a PersistentVolume named pv-static-10g:
      • Capacity: 10Gi
      • AccessMode: ReadWriteOnce
      • ReclaimPolicy: Retain
      • StorageClassName: manual-storage
      • Type: hostPath at path /mnt/data/lab46
    2. Author a PersistentVolumeClaim named pvc-claim-5g in default:
      • Requested Storage: 5Gi
      • AccessMode: ReadWriteOnce
      • StorageClassName: manual-storage
    3. Verify that pvc-claim-5g binds to pv-static-10g.
    4. Note that the remaining 5Gi on the PV is locked and unavailable to other claims.
  • Constraints: Do not use default storage classes.
  • Hints: A PVC can bind to a PV of equal or greater capacity.
  • Expected Result: Both PV and PVC show status Bound.
  • Verification:
    kubectl get pv pv-static-10g
kubectl get pvc pvc-claim-5g
  • Full Solution:
    apiVersion: v1
kind: PersistentVolume
metadata:
  name: pv-static-10g
spec:
  capacity:
    storage: 10Gi
  accessModes:
    - ReadWriteOnce
  persistentVolumeReclaimPolicy: Retain
  storageClassName: manual-storage
  hostPath:
    path: /mnt/data/lab46
---
apiVersion: v1
kind: PersistentVolumeClaim
metadata:
  name: pvc-claim-5g
spec:
  accessModes:
    - ReadWriteOnce
  storageClassName: manual-storage
  resources:
    requests:
      storage: 5Gi

    kubectl apply -f static-pv-pvc.yaml
  • Common Mistakes: Mismatching storageClassName between PV and PVC, causing PVC to remain in Pending.
  • Troubleshooting: Run kubectl describe pvc pvc-claim-5g to inspect binding events.
  • Cleanup Instructions:
    kubectl delete pvc pvc-claim-5g
kubectl delete pv pv-static-10g

LAB 47: Dynamic Volume Provisioning with StorageClasses

  • Difficulty: Intermediate
  • Estimated Duration: 10 minutes
  • Domain: Storage (10%)
  • Learning Objective: Configure a custom StorageClass with dynamic provisioning and observe automated PV creation upon PVC submission.
  • Prerequisites: Cluster with dynamic provisioner (e.g. rancher.io/local-path or cloud provisioner).
  • Cluster Environment: Standard cluster.
  • Initial State: No StorageClass named fast-dynamic.
  • Task Description:
    1. Create a StorageClass named fast-dynamic utilizing provisioner rancher.io/local-path (or target cluster CSI provisioner).
    2. Set reclaimPolicy: Delete and volumeBindingMode: Immediate.
    3. Create a PVC dynamic-claim in namespace default requesting 2Gi on fast-dynamic.
    4. Verify that Kubernetes dynamically generates a new PersistentVolume without manual administrator intervention.
    5. Deploy a Pod data-writer mounting the claim at /data and write a test file.
  • Constraints: Must use dynamic provisioning.
  • Hints: Look at existing StorageClasses with kubectl get sc to find the active provisioner string.
  • Expected Result: PVC immediately binds to an auto-created PV named pvc-<uuid>.
  • Verification:
    kubectl get pvc dynamic-claim
kubectl get pv | grep dynamic-claim
  • Full Solution:
    apiVersion: storage.k8s.io/v1
kind: StorageClass
metadata:
  name: fast-dynamic
provisioner: rancher.io/local-path
reclaimPolicy: Delete
volumeBindingMode: Immediate
---
apiVersion: v1
kind: PersistentVolumeClaim
metadata:
  name: dynamic-claim
spec:
  storageClassName: fast-dynamic
  accessModes:
    - ReadWriteOnce
  resources:
    requests:
      storage: 2Gi
---
apiVersion: v1
kind: Pod
metadata:
  name: data-writer
spec:
  containers:
  - name: writer
    image: alpine
    command: ["/bin/sh", "-c", "echo 'storage verified' > /data/test.txt; sleep 3600"]
    volumeMounts:
    - name: storage
      mountPath: /data
  volumes:
  - name: storage
    persistentVolumeClaim:
      claimName: dynamic-claim

    kubectl apply -f dynamic-storage.yaml
  • Common Mistakes: Typo in provisioner name causing provisioner lookup failures.
  • Troubleshooting: Run kubectl describe pvc dynamic-claim to check ExternalProvisioning events.
  • Cleanup Instructions:
    kubectl delete -f dynamic-storage.yaml

LAB 48: Delayed Volume Binding with WaitForFirstConsumer

  • Difficulty: Intermediate
  • Estimated Duration: 12 minutes
  • Domain: Storage (10%)
  • Learning Objective: Configure a StorageClass with volumeBindingMode: WaitForFirstConsumer to ensure volumes are provisioned in the exact availability zone of the scheduled Pod.
  • Prerequisites: Multi-node cluster.
  • Cluster Environment: Multi-worker cluster.
  • Initial State: StorageClass topology-aware-sc.
  • Task Description:
    1. Author a StorageClass delayed-sc with volumeBindingMode: WaitForFirstConsumer.
    2. Create PVC delayed-pvc requesting 1Gi on delayed-sc.
    3. Observe that delayed-pvc remains in Pending with message waiting for first consumer to be created before binding.
    4. Create Pod delayed-consumer targeting node worker-2 with nodeName: worker-2 that mounts delayed-pvc.
    5. Verify that as soon as the Pod is created, the volume binds immediately on worker-2.
  • Constraints: Understand why Pending status is normal before Pod scheduling.
  • Hints: The volume binding controller waits for the scheduler to populate spec.nodeName.
  • Expected Result: PVC transitions from Pending to Bound once consumer Pod is scheduled.
  • Verification:
    kubectl get pvc delayed-pvc
# Should show Bound only after Pod is applied
  • Full Solution:
    apiVersion: storage.k8s.io/v1
kind: StorageClass
metadata:
  name: delayed-sc
provisioner: rancher.io/local-path
volumeBindingMode: WaitForFirstConsumer
---
apiVersion: v1
kind: PersistentVolumeClaim
metadata:
  name: delayed-pvc
spec:
  storageClassName: delayed-sc
  accessModes:
    - ReadWriteOnce
  resources:
    requests:
      storage: 1Gi
---
apiVersion: v1
kind: Pod
metadata:
  name: delayed-consumer
spec:
  containers:
  - name: app
    image: alpine
    command: ["sleep", "3600"]
    volumeMounts:
    - name: data
      mountPath: /data
  volumes:
  - name: data
    persistentVolumeClaim:
      claimName: delayed-pvc

    kubectl apply -f delayed-sc-pvc.yaml
# Verify PVC is Pending
kubectl apply -f delayed-pod.yaml
# Verify PVC is Bound
  • Common Mistakes: Panicking when the PVC shows Pending before any Pod has referenced it.
  • Troubleshooting: Run kubectl describe pvc delayed-pvc to confirm message WaitForFirstConsumer.
  • Cleanup Instructions:
    kubectl delete pod delayed-consumer
kubectl delete pvc delayed-pvc
kubectl delete sc delayed-sc

LAB 49: Reclaim Policy Enforcement (Retain vs Delete) and Released Recovery

  • Difficulty: Advanced
  • Estimated Duration: 15 minutes
  • Domain: Storage (10%)
  • Learning Objective: Recover data from a Released PersistentVolume by manually stripping spec.claimRef and re-binding it to a new claim.
  • Prerequisites: Working cluster.
  • Cluster Environment: Any cluster.
  • Initial State: PV pv-reclaim-test with persistentVolumeReclaimPolicy: Retain.
  • Task Description:
    1. Create a PV pv-reclaim-test with capacity 2Gi and reclaimPolicy: Retain.
    2. Bind it with PVC pvc-original.
    3. Write data to the volume from a temporary pod.
    4. Delete pvc-original.
    5. Observe that pv-reclaim-test enters Released status and refuses to bind to a new claim pvc-new.
    6. Manually edit pv-reclaim-test to delete spec.claimRef.
    7. Verify status changes to Available and pvc-new successfully binds to it with original data intact.
  • Constraints: Do not delete or recreate the PersistentVolume object.
  • Hints: Use kubectl edit pv pv-reclaim-test and remove the claimRef block.
  • Expected Result: PV transitions Bound $\to$ Released $\to$ Available $\to$ Bound.
  • Verification:
    kubectl get pv pv-reclaim-test
  • Full Solution:
    apiVersion: v1
kind: PersistentVolume
metadata:
  name: pv-reclaim-test
spec:
  capacity:
    storage: 2Gi
  accessModes:
    - ReadWriteOnce
  persistentVolumeReclaimPolicy: Retain
  storageClassName: manual-reclaim
  hostPath:
    path: /tmp/reclaim-data
---
apiVersion: v1
kind: PersistentVolumeClaim
metadata:
  name: pvc-original
spec:
  storageClassName: manual-reclaim
  accessModes:
    - ReadWriteOnce
  resources:
    requests:
      storage: 2Gi

    kubectl apply -f pv-reclaim.yaml
# Delete PVC
kubectl delete pvc pvc-original
# PV is now Released
kubectl patch pv pv-reclaim-test -p '{"spec":{"claimRef": null}}'
# PV is now Available!
  • Common Mistakes: Trying to re-bind without removing claimRef, causing new PVC to hang in Pending.
  • Troubleshooting: Inspect kubectl get pv pv-reclaim-test -o yaml to verify claimRef is completely absent.
  • Cleanup Instructions:
    kubectl delete pv pv-reclaim-test

LAB 50: Single-Pod Data Locking with ReadWriteOncePod (RWOP)

  • Difficulty: Intermediate
  • Estimated Duration: 10 minutes
  • Domain: Storage (10%)
  • Learning Objective: Enforce strict single-pod data access using modern access mode ReadWriteOncePod introduced in Kubernetes v1.29+.
  • Prerequisites: Kubernetes v1.29+ cluster with CSI supporting RWOP.
  • Cluster Environment: Modern cluster.
  • Initial State: Namespace rwop-demo.
  • Task Description:
    1. Create namespace rwop-demo.
    2. Author a PVC exclusive-pvc requesting 1Gi with accessMode ReadWriteOncePod.
    3. Deploy Pod first-pod mounting exclusive-pvc.
    4. Attempt to deploy a second Pod second-pod mounting the exact same exclusive-pvc on the same node.
    5. Observe that second-pod blocks in Pending / ContainerCreating with a volume exclusivity conflict error.
  • Constraints: Access mode must be strictly ReadWriteOncePod.
  • Hints: In standard ReadWriteOnce, multiple pods on the same node can mount the volume. ReadWriteOncePod forbids this!
  • Expected Result: Second pod cannot mount the volume while the first pod is active.
  • Verification:
    kubectl describe pod second-pod -n rwop-demo | grep -i failedmount
  • Full Solution:
    apiVersion: v1
kind: PersistentVolumeClaim
metadata:
  name: exclusive-pvc
  namespace: rwop-demo
spec:
  accessModes:
    - ReadWriteOncePod
  resources:
    requests:
      storage: 1Gi
---
apiVersion: v1
kind: Pod
metadata:
  name: first-pod
  namespace: rwop-demo
spec:
  containers:
  - name: c1
    image: alpine
    command: ["sleep", "3600"]
    volumeMounts:
    - name: vol
      mountPath: /data
  volumes:
  - name: vol
    persistentVolumeClaim:
      claimName: exclusive-pvc
---
apiVersion: v1
kind: Pod
metadata:
  name: second-pod
  namespace: rwop-demo
spec:
  containers:
  - name: c2
    image: alpine
    command: ["sleep", "3600"]
    volumeMounts:
    - name: vol
      mountPath: /data
  volumes:
  - name: vol
    persistentVolumeClaim:
      claimName: exclusive-pvc

    kubectl create namespace rwop-demo
kubectl apply -f rwop-demo.yaml
  • Common Mistakes: Confusing ReadWriteOnce with ReadWriteOncePod.
  • Troubleshooting: Run kubectl get events -n rwop-demo to see volume lock rejection.
  • Cleanup Instructions:
    kubectl delete namespace rwop-demo

LAB 51: Dynamic PVC Online Volume Expansion

  • Difficulty: Intermediate
  • Estimated Duration: 10 minutes
  • Domain: Storage (10%)
  • Learning Objective: Perform an online volume expansion of an active PersistentVolumeClaim from 1Gi to 3Gi without restarting application Pods.
  • Prerequisites: StorageClass with allowVolumeExpansion: true.
  • Cluster Environment: Dynamic storage cluster.
  • Initial State: PVC expandable-pvc currently bound at 1Gi.
  • Task Description:
    1. Verify the StorageClass allows volume expansion (allowVolumeExpansion: true).
    2. Deploy Pod app-resizing mounting expandable-pvc at /data.
    3. Edit expandable-pvc using kubectl patch to increase requested storage to 3Gi.
    4. Verify the PVC status reflects 3Gi and the container filesystem expands online.
  • Constraints: Do not delete or terminate app-resizing.
  • Hints: You can only expand storage size; shrinking storage is strictly prohibited by Kubernetes.
  • Expected Result: Filesystem expands seamlessly without downtime.
  • Verification:
    kubectl get pvc expandable-pvc
# Capacity should show 3Gi
kubectl exec -it app-resizing -- df -h /data
  • Full Solution:
    kubectl patch pvc expandable-pvc -p '{"spec":{"resources":{"requests":{"storage":"3Gi"}}}}'
  • Common Mistakes: Attempting to reduce volume size (e.g. from 3Gi to 1Gi), which is rejected with 422 Unprocessable Entity.
  • Troubleshooting: If capacity does not change, check if the StorageClass has allowVolumeExpansion: true.
  • Cleanup Instructions:
    kubectl delete pod app-resizing
kubectl delete pvc expandable-pvc

LAB 52: Multi-Pod Shared Read-Write Storage (ReadWriteMany)

  • Difficulty: Intermediate
  • Estimated Duration: 12 minutes
  • Domain: Storage (10%)
  • Learning Objective: Configure a shared volume with ReadWriteMany access mode and verify concurrent read-write access from pods on different nodes.
  • Prerequisites: Clustered/shared storage provisioner (NFS or local-path shared).
  • Cluster Environment: Multi-worker cluster.
  • Initial State: Namespace shared-storage.
  • Task Description:
    1. Create namespace shared-storage.
    2. Create PVC shared-pvc with accessModes: [ReadWriteMany] requesting 2Gi.
    3. Deploy Pod writer-pod on worker-1 mounting shared-pvc and writing timestamps to /shared/log.txt.
    4. Deploy Pod reader-pod on worker-2 mounting shared-pvc and tailing /shared/log.txt.
    5. Verify reader-pod sees live updates written by writer-pod.
  • Constraints: Must use ReadWriteMany.
  • Hints: Block storage (AWS EBS, GCP PD) does NOT support RWX. File storage (NFS, EFS, CephFS) is required.
  • Expected Result: Concurrent multi-node read/write succeeds.
  • Verification:
    kubectl logs reader-pod -n shared-storage
  • Full Solution:
    apiVersion: v1
kind: PersistentVolumeClaim
metadata:
  name: shared-pvc
  namespace: shared-storage
spec:
  accessModes:
    - ReadWriteMany
  resources:
    requests:
      storage: 2Gi
---
apiVersion: v1
kind: Pod
metadata:
  name: writer-pod
  namespace: shared-storage
spec:
  nodeName: worker-1
  containers:
  - name: writer
    image: alpine
    command: ["/bin/sh", "-c", "while true; do date >> /shared/log.txt; sleep 2; done"]
    volumeMounts:
    - name: shared-vol
      mountPath: /shared
  volumes:
  - name: shared-vol
    persistentVolumeClaim:
      claimName: shared-pvc
---
apiVersion: v1
kind: Pod
metadata:
  name: reader-pod
  namespace: shared-storage
spec:
  nodeName: worker-2
  containers:
  - name: reader
    image: alpine
    command: ["/bin/sh", "-c", "sleep 5; cat /shared/log.txt; sleep 3600"]
    volumeMounts:
    - name: shared-vol
      mountPath: /shared
  volumes:
  - name: shared-vol
    persistentVolumeClaim:
      claimName: shared-pvc

    kubectl create namespace shared-storage
kubectl apply -f shared-storage.yaml
  • Common Mistakes: Requesting ReadWriteMany against an EBS/GCP PD CSI driver that only supports ReadWriteOnce.
  • Troubleshooting: Check kubectl describe pvc shared-pvc -n shared-storage for unsupported access mode errors.
  • Cleanup Instructions:
    kubectl delete namespace shared-storage

LAB 53: CSI Driver Inspection and VolumeAttachment Triage

  • Difficulty: Intermediate
  • Estimated Duration: 10 minutes
  • Domain: Storage (10%)
  • Learning Objective: Inspect Container Storage Interface (CSI) node registration, volume drivers, and VolumeAttachment API objects.
  • Prerequisites: CSI-enabled cluster.
  • Cluster Environment: Standard Kubernetes cluster.
  • Initial State: Running cluster with active workloads.
  • Task Description:
    1. Inspect all registered CSI drivers using kubectl get csidrivers.
    2. Inspect CSI node registrations across all nodes using kubectl get csinodes.
    3. Inspect active VolumeAttachment objects across the cluster.
    4. Identify which node has attached volume pvc-xxxx.
  • Constraints: Understand how the CSI Controller attaches volumes before the CSI Node mounts them.
  • Hints: VolumeAttachment objects are cluster-scoped.
  • Expected Result: Full mapping of PVs to physical nodes via VolumeAttachment.
  • Verification:
    kubectl get csidrivers
kubectl get csinodes -o wide
kubectl get volumeattachments
  • Full Solution:
    kubectl get csidrivers -o custom-columns=NAME:.metadata.name,ATTACH-REQUIRED:.spec.attachRequired,POD-INFO:.spec.podInfoOnMount
kubectl get csinodes
kubectl get volumeattachment -o custom-columns=NAME:.metadata.name,NODE:.spec.nodeName,PV:.spec.source.persistentVolumeName,ATTACHED:.status.attached
  • Common Mistakes: Looking for VolumeAttachment in a specific namespace (it is non-namespaced).
  • Troubleshooting: If a volume attachment hangs, describe the VolumeAttachment object to view CSI driver attach errors.
  • Cleanup Instructions: None.

DOMAIN 5: TROUBLESHOOTING (LABS 54–66)

LAB 54: Pod Stuck in Pending (Resource Depletion and Taints)

  • Difficulty: Beginner
  • Estimated Duration: 8 minutes
  • Domain: Troubleshooting (30%)
  • Learning Objective: Diagnose why a Pod remains in Pending status by inspecting scheduler events and correcting unschedulable constraints.
  • Prerequisites: Running cluster.
  • Cluster Environment: Standard Kubernetes cluster.
  • Initial State: Pod batch-calc created in default stuck in Pending.
  • Task Description:
    1. Inspect the cluster to locate a pod named batch-calc stuck in Pending.
    2. Inspect the pod's events to identify why the scheduler failed to place it.
    3. You will discover that the container requested cpu: "64", which exceeds cluster capacity.
    4. Fix the manifest to request cpu: "100m", re-apply, and verify the Pod enters Running status.
  • Constraints: Do not delete or cordon worker nodes.
  • Hints: Look at the bottom of kubectl describe pod batch-calc.
  • Expected Result: Pod transitions from Pending to Running.
  • Verification:
    kubectl get pod batch-calc
# STATUS: Running
  • Full Solution:
    # 1. Diagnose
kubectl describe pod batch-calc
# Event: 0/3 nodes are available: 3 Insufficient cpu.

# 2. Extract and edit
kubectl get pod batch-calc -o yaml > /tmp/batch-calc.yaml
sed -i 's/cpu: "64"/cpu: 100m/g' /tmp/batch-calc.yaml

# 3. Replace
kubectl replace --force -f /tmp/batch-calc.yaml
  • Common Mistakes: Trying to edit requests.cpu on a running pod with kubectl edit without --force (requests are immutable on running pods in standard clusters).
  • Troubleshooting: Run kubectl get events --field-selector involvedObject.name=batch-calc.
  • Cleanup Instructions:
    kubectl delete pod batch-calc

LAB 55: Diagnosing and Fixing CrashLoopBackOff

  • Difficulty: Intermediate
  • Estimated Duration: 10 minutes
  • Domain: Troubleshooting (30%)
  • Learning Objective: Triage a container that boots and immediately crashes using kubectl logs --previous and repair application startup arguments.
  • Prerequisites: Running cluster.
  • Cluster Environment: Any cluster.
  • Initial State: Pod log-forwarder in namespace logging in CrashLoopBackOff.
  • Task Description:
    1. Inspect Pod log-forwarder in namespace logging.
    2. Read the crash logs using kubectl logs log-forwarder -n logging --previous.
    3. Identify that the command failed with error: cat: can't open '/etc/config/app.conf': No such file or directory.
    4. Create a ConfigMap named forwarder-config containing key app.conf with text log_level=debug.
    5. Mount this ConfigMap at /etc/config in the Pod manifest and re-deploy.
    6. Verify the Pod reaches Running status with zero restarts.
  • Constraints: Do not alter the container entrypoint command.
  • Hints: Use --previous to see logs from the crashed container before the restart backoff.
  • Expected Result: Pod stabilizes at Running.
  • Verification:
    kubectl get pod log-forwarder -n logging
  • Full Solution:
    kubectl create namespace logging --dry-run=client -o yaml | kubectl apply -f -

# Check previous log
kubectl logs log-forwarder -n logging --previous

# Create missing config
kubectl create configmap forwarder-config --from-literal=app.conf="log_level=debug" -n logging

# Update Pod manifest
cat <<EOF | kubectl apply -f -
apiVersion: v1
kind: Pod
metadata:
  name: log-forwarder
  namespace: logging
spec:
  containers:
  - name: forwarder
    image: busybox:1.36
    command: ["/bin/sh", "-c", "cat /etc/config/app.conf && sleep 3600"]
    volumeMounts:
    - name: cfg
      mountPath: /etc/config
  volumes:
  - name: cfg
    configMap:
      name: forwarder-config
EOF
  • Common Mistakes: Running kubectl logs without --previous, which often displays an empty log if the container is currently in the backoff sleep cycle.
  • Troubleshooting: Inspect exit code in kubectl describe pod log-forwarder -n logging.
  • Cleanup Instructions:
    kubectl delete namespace logging

LAB 56: Fixing ImagePullBackOff and Private Registry Secrets

  • Difficulty: Beginner
  • Estimated Duration: 8 minutes
  • Domain: Troubleshooting (30%)
  • Learning Objective: Identify image name typographical errors and configure imagePullSecrets for private container registries.
  • Prerequisites: Running cluster.
  • Cluster Environment: Any cluster.
  • Initial State: Pod secure-app showing ImagePullBackOff.
  • Task Description:
    1. Inspect Pod secure-app in default.
    2. Run kubectl describe pod secure-app to identify the image error.
    3. Notice the typo in the image name: nginxx:1.27 instead of nginx:1.27.
    4. Update the pod image to nginx:1.27.
    5. Verify the pod transitions from ImagePullBackOff $\to$ ContainerCreating $\to$ Running.
  • Constraints: Do not recreate the pod if you can patch it cleanly, or replace it rapidly.
  • Hints: In a Pod, spec.containers[0].image can be edited, or recreate with --force.
  • Expected Result: Pod pulls successfully and runs.
  • Verification:
    kubectl get pod secure-app
  • Full Solution:
    kubectl describe pod secure-app | grep -A 3 Events
kubectl set image pod secure-app secure-app=nginx:1.27
  • Common Mistakes: Confusing ImagePullBackOff (image not found) with CrashLoopBackOff (image downloaded, but binary failed).
  • Troubleshooting: Inspect exact event string in kubectl describe pod secure-app.
  • Cleanup Instructions:
    kubectl delete pod secure-app

LAB 57: Out-Of-Memory (OOMKilled Exit Code 137) Limit Tuning

  • Difficulty: Intermediate
  • Estimated Duration: 10 minutes
  • Domain: Troubleshooting (30%)
  • Learning Objective: Diagnose an application terminated with Exit Code 137 (OOMKilled) and calibrate memory limits to accommodate application heap spikes.
  • Prerequisites: Running cluster.
  • Cluster Environment: Any cluster.
  • Initial State: Deployment cache-worker whose pods restart every 30 seconds with Exit Code 137.
  • Task Description:
    1. Inspect the pods of Deployment cache-worker.
    2. Notice the restart count incrementing and Last State: Terminated, Reason: OOMKilled.
    3. Inspect container memory limits: currently set to 32Mi.
    4. Update the Deployment manifest to increase memory limits to 256Mi and memory requests to 128Mi.
    5. Monitor the new rollout and verify the pods remain running with 0 restarts.
  • Constraints: Understand why the Linux kernel OOM killer terminates processes exceeding limits.memory.
  • Hints: Exit Code 137 $= 128 + 9$ (SIGKILL).
  • Expected Result: Deployment pods stabilize at Running with zero subsequent restarts.
  • Verification:
    kubectl get pods -l app=cache-worker
  • Full Solution:
    kubectl describe pod -l app=cache-worker | grep -E "Reason|Exit Code"
kubectl set resources deployment cache-worker --limits=memory=256Mi --requests=memory=128Mi
kubectl rollout status deployment cache-worker
  • Common Mistakes: Increasing CPU limits instead of memory limits (CPU limits cause throttling; only memory causes OOMKilled).
  • Troubleshooting: Check node kernel dmesg for kernel OOM killer logs: dmesg -T | grep -i oom.
  • Cleanup Instructions:
    kubectl delete deployment cache-worker

LAB 58: Triage and Recovery of a NotReady Worker Node

  • Difficulty: Advanced
  • Estimated Duration: 15 minutes
  • Domain: Troubleshooting (30%)
  • Learning Objective: SSH into a failed worker node, inspect systemd service failures, repair a dead kubelet daemon, and verify node restoration.
  • Prerequisites: Multi-node cluster with SSH access to worker-1.
  • Cluster Environment: kubeadm multi-node cluster.
  • Initial State: worker-1 shows STATUS: NotReady.
  • Task Description:
    1. On the control plane, run kubectl get nodes and observe worker-1 is NotReady.
    2. SSH into worker-1.
    3. Inspect the status of the kubelet service: sudo systemctl status kubelet.
    4. Notice the service is inactive (dead).
    5. Attempt to start the service: sudo systemctl start kubelet.
    6. Inspect sudo journalctl -u kubelet -e to find why it fails: unable to load client CA file: stat /etc/kubernetes/pki/ca.crt: no such file or directory.
    7. Discover someone accidentally renamed /etc/kubernetes/pki/ca.crt to /etc/kubernetes/pki/ca.crt.bak.
    8. Restore the CA certificate file, start and enable kubelet.
    9. Exit to control plane and verify worker-1 transitions back to Ready.
  • Constraints: Do not reboot the host.
  • Hints: Kubelet requires the cluster CA certificate to validate the API server.
  • Expected Result: worker-1 returns to Ready status.
  • Verification:
    kubectl get nodes worker-1
# STATUS: Ready
  • Full Solution:
    # On worker-1:
sudo systemctl status kubelet
sudo journalctl -u kubelet -n 30 --no-pager
# Identify missing ca.crt
sudo mv /etc/kubernetes/pki/ca.crt.bak /etc/kubernetes/pki/ca.crt
sudo systemctl restart kubelet
sudo systemctl is-active kubelet
# Output: active
exit

# On Control Plane:
kubectl get nodes worker-1
  • Common Mistakes: Failing to inspect journalctl -u kubelet and guessing random configurations.
  • Troubleshooting: Verify node conditions: kubectl describe node worker-1 | grep Ready.
  • Cleanup Instructions: None.

LAB 59: Broken Kubelet Cgroup Driver Recovery

  • Difficulty: Advanced
  • Estimated Duration: 12 minutes
  • Domain: Troubleshooting (30%)
  • Learning Objective: Resolve a fatal cgroup driver mismatch between containerd and kubelet causing node boot loops.
  • Prerequisites: SSH access to a worker node.
  • Cluster Environment: Node running containerd.
  • Initial State: Kubelet failing with misconfigured cgroup driver.
  • Task Description:
    1. On worker node worker-2, kubelet fails to start.
    2. Inspect sudo journalctl -u kubelet -e.
    3. Locate error: failed to run Kubelet: misconfiguration: cgroup driver: "cgroupfs" is different from docker/containerd cgroup driver "systemd".
    4. Edit /var/lib/kubelet/config.yaml.
    5. Update cgroupDriver: cgroupfs to cgroupDriver: systemd.
    6. Restart kubelet and verify it starts cleanly without errors.
  • Constraints: Enforce systemd cgroup driver everywhere.
  • Hints: Kubelet's configuration is managed via /var/lib/kubelet/config.yaml.
  • Expected Result: Kubelet service enters active (running).
  • Verification:
    sudo systemctl status kubelet
# Active: active (running)
  • Full Solution:
    # On worker-2:
sudo sed -i 's/cgroupDriver: cgroupfs/cgroupDriver: systemd/g' /var/lib/kubelet/config.yaml
sudo systemctl restart kubelet
sudo systemctl status kubelet --no-pager
  • Common Mistakes: Modifying flags in /etc/default/kubelet that have been deprecated in modern Kubernetes. Edit /var/lib/kubelet/config.yaml directly.
  • Troubleshooting: If kubelet still fails, verify /etc/containerd/config.toml has SystemdCgroup = true.
  • Cleanup Instructions: None.

LAB 60: Broken Control Plane Static Pod Manifest Repair

  • Difficulty: Advanced
  • Estimated Duration: 15 minutes
  • Domain: Troubleshooting (30%)
  • Learning Objective: Fix an invalid CLI flag inside /etc/kubernetes/manifests/kube-scheduler.yaml that prevents the Kubernetes scheduler from starting.
  • Prerequisites: SSH access to control plane node with sudo privileges.
  • Cluster Environment: kubeadm control plane node.
  • Initial State: All newly created pods stay in Pending forever; kube-scheduler static pod is not running.
  • Task Description:
    1. Notice that all pods across the cluster remain Pending even though nodes have ample CPU and RAM.
    2. Check control plane pods: kubectl get pods -n kube-system. Notice kube-scheduler is missing or crashed.
    3. SSH into the control plane node.
    4. Inspect /etc/kubernetes/manifests/kube-scheduler.yaml.
    5. Run sudo crictl ps -a | grep scheduler and check logs: sudo crictl logs <id>.
    6. Discover someone introduced a bogus flag: --port=10251 or --unknown-flag=true.
    7. Remove the invalid flag from /etc/kubernetes/manifests/kube-scheduler.yaml.
    8. Observe kubelet automatically detect the file change and relaunch the scheduler container within 10 seconds.
    9. Verify pending pods are immediately scheduled and transition to Running.
  • Constraints: Do not use kubectl apply on static pod manifests.
  • Hints: Kubelet watches /etc/kubernetes/manifests/ with inotify. Saving the file automatically triggers container recreation.
  • Expected Result: kube-scheduler starts, and pending pods schedule immediately.
  • Verification:
    kubectl get pods -n kube-system | grep scheduler
kubectl get pods
  • Full Solution:
    # On Control Plane:
sudo crictl ps -a --name=kube-scheduler
# Inspect logs of exited container
sudo crictl logs $(sudo crictl ps -a --name=kube-scheduler -q | head -n 1)

# Edit manifest and remove invalid line
sudo nano /etc/kubernetes/manifests/kube-scheduler.yaml

# Wait 10 seconds and verify
kubectl get pods -n kube-system -l component=kube-scheduler
  • Common Mistakes: Trying to delete the static pod with kubectl delete pod; kubelet will simply recreate the broken container again until the YAML on disk is fixed!
  • Troubleshooting: Check journalctl -u kubelet if the static pod is not detected.
  • Cleanup Instructions: None.

LAB 61: Expired Cluster Certificate Recovery via kubeadm certs renew

  • Difficulty: Advanced
  • Estimated Duration: 12 minutes
  • Domain: Troubleshooting (30%)
  • Learning Objective: Recover an unresponsive cluster whose control plane TLS certificates have expired.
  • Prerequisites: SSH access to control plane node.
  • Cluster Environment: kubeadm cluster.
  • Initial State: kubectl returns Unable to connect to the server: x509: certificate has expired.
  • Task Description:
    1. Run kubectl get nodes and observe the x509 certificate expired error.
    2. SSH into the control plane node.
    3. Check certificate status: sudo kubeadm certs check-expiration.
    4. Observe that admin.conf, apiserver, etc. are expired.
    5. Renew all certificates: sudo kubeadm certs renew all.
    6. Copy the renewed admin credentials to $HOME/.kube/config: sudo cp -i /etc/kubernetes/admin.conf $HOME/.kube/config && sudo chown $(id -u):$(id -g) $HOME/.kube/config.
    7. Restart control plane static pods by moving manifests out to /tmp/ and back in.
    8. Verify kubectl get nodes functions cleanly.
  • Constraints: Renew all certificates using official kubeadm tooling.
  • Hints: Remember to update $HOME/.kube/config with the new client cert embedded in /etc/kubernetes/admin.conf!
  • Expected Result: Cluster responds cleanly to all kubectl commands.
  • Verification:
    kubectl get nodes
sudo kubeadm certs check-expiration
  • Full Solution:
    # 1. Renew
sudo kubeadm certs renew all

# 2. Update user kubeconfig
mkdir -p $HOME/.kube
sudo cp -f /etc/kubernetes/admin.conf $HOME/.kube/config
sudo chown $(id -u):$(id -g) $HOME/.kube/config

# 3. Restart static pods
sudo mv /etc/kubernetes/manifests/*.yaml /tmp/
sleep 5
sudo mv /tmp/*.yaml /etc/kubernetes/manifests/

# 4. Verify
sleep 15
kubectl get nodes
  • Common Mistakes: Renewing certificates in /etc/kubernetes/pki but forgetting to update $HOME/.kube/config, leaving the user with an expired client cert!
  • Troubleshooting: Run openssl x509 -in /etc/kubernetes/pki/apiserver.crt -noout -dates to verify valid dates.
  • Cleanup Instructions: None.

LAB 62: CoreDNS Loop Crash Recovery

  • Difficulty: Intermediate
  • Estimated Duration: 10 minutes
  • Domain: Troubleshooting (30%)
  • Learning Objective: Diagnose and resolve a CoreDNS infinite loop crash caused by systemd-resolved stub resolvers in host /etc/resolv.conf.
  • Prerequisites: Control plane access.
  • Cluster Environment: Ubuntu nodes with systemd-resolved.
  • Initial State: CoreDNS pods in CrashLoopBackOff.
  • Task Description:
    1. Notice CoreDNS pods in kube-system crash-looping.
    2. Inspect logs: kubectl logs -n kube-system -l k8s-app=kube-dns.
    3. Observe error: plugin/loop: Loop (127.0.0.1:53 -> :53) detected for zone ".".
    4. Inspect the host /etc/resolv.conf and discover nameserver 127.0.0.53.
    5. Update the kubelet configuration /var/lib/kubelet/config.yaml to point resolvConf to /run/systemd/resolve/resolv.conf (which points to real upstream DNS).
    6. Restart kubelet: sudo systemctl restart kubelet.
    7. Delete crash-looping CoreDNS pods and verify the new pods enter Running status without loop errors.
  • Constraints: Do not disable CoreDNS loop detection plugin.
  • Hints: /run/systemd/resolve/resolv.conf contains upstream nameservers instead of the local stub resolver.
  • Expected Result: CoreDNS pods stabilize at Running.
  • Verification:
    kubectl get pods -n kube-system -l k8s-app=kube-dns
# STATUS: Running, RESTARTS: 0
  • Full Solution:
    # Check logs
kubectl logs -n kube-system -l k8s-app=kube-dns

# Configure kubelet to use real upstream resolv.conf
sudo sed -i 's|resolvConf: .*|resolvConf: /run/systemd/resolve/resolv.conf|g' /var/lib/kubelet/config.yaml
sudo systemctl restart kubelet

# Delete old pods to pick up new config
kubectl delete pods -n kube-system -l k8s-app=kube-dns
  • Common Mistakes: Manually removing the loop plugin from the Corefile without fixing the underlying upstream loop, leading to high CPU exhaustion.
  • Troubleshooting: Check host nameserver: cat /run/systemd/resolve/resolv.conf.
  • Cleanup Instructions: None.

LAB 63: Network Service Endpoint Disconnect

  • Difficulty: Beginner
  • Estimated Duration: 8 minutes
  • Domain: Troubleshooting (30%)
  • Learning Objective: Diagnose why a Kubernetes Service returns connection refused or empty responses by identifying selector label mismatches.
  • Prerequisites: Running cluster.
  • Cluster Environment: Any cluster.
  • Initial State: Service auth-api-svc and Deployment auth-api running in default.
  • Task Description:
    1. Service auth-api-svc is failing to route traffic to application pods.
    2. Run kubectl get endpoints auth-api-svc and observe ENDPOINTS: <none>.
    3. Inspect the selector of the Service and compare it with the labels of the Deployment pods.
    4. Notice the Service selector specifies app: auth-service, but the Pod template is labeled app: auth-api.
    5. Fix the Service selector to match app: auth-api.
    6. Verify kubectl get endpoints auth-api-svc now lists all pod IP addresses.
  • Constraints: Do not delete the Deployment.
  • Hints: A Service will only route traffic to Pods whose labels match its spec.selector exactly.
  • Expected Result: Service endpoints are populated and internal traffic flows.
  • Verification:
    kubectl get endpoints auth-api-svc
# Should show IP:Port
  • Full Solution:
    kubectl get svc auth-api-svc -o yaml
kubectl get pods --show-labels

# Update selector
kubectl patch svc auth-api-svc -p '{"spec":{"selector":{"app":"auth-api"}}}'

# Verify
kubectl get endpoints auth-api-svc
  • Common Mistakes: Editing the Deployment pod labels instead of the Service selector, which breaks other label-dependent controllers.
  • Troubleshooting: Inspect selector with kubectl describe svc auth-api-svc.
  • Cleanup Instructions:
    kubectl delete svc auth-api-svc

LAB 64: Broken NetworkPolicy Triage (Blocked Cross-Namespace Traffic)

  • Difficulty: Intermediate
  • Estimated Duration: 10 minutes
  • Domain: Troubleshooting (30%)
  • Learning Objective: Discover and fix a restrictive NetworkPolicy that is silently dropping cross-namespace API calls.
  • Prerequisites: NetworkPolicy capable cluster.
  • Cluster Environment: Any cluster with Calico/Cilium.
  • Initial State: Namespaces frontend and backend. Frontend cannot connect to backend-svc.
  • Task Description:
    1. Pod in namespace frontend attempts to curl http://backend-svc.backend:8080 but times out.
    2. Inspect the backend namespace for active NetworkPolicies: kubectl get netpol -n backend.
    3. Inspect policy lockdown-backend: notice it allows Ingress only from namespaces labeled tier: web.
    4. Check labels on namespace frontend: kubectl get ns frontend --show-labels. Notice the label is missing.
    5. Label namespace frontend with tier: web.
    6. Verify connectivity is immediately restored.
  • Constraints: Do not delete or disable the NetworkPolicy.
  • Hints: NetworkPolicies selecting namespaces match against labels on the Namespace object, not the Pod object!
  • Expected Result: Connections from frontend to backend succeed.
  • Verification:
    kubectl run curl-test --image=curlimages/curl --rm -it --restart=Never -n frontend -- http://backend-svc.backend:8080
# Expected: HTTP 200 OK
  • Full Solution:
    kubectl describe netpol lockdown-backend -n backend
kubectl label namespace frontend tier=web --overwrite
  • Common Mistakes: Labeling the Pod in frontend instead of labeling the Namespace resource.
  • Troubleshooting: Run kubectl get netpol -A to ensure no competing default-deny egress policies exist in frontend.
  • Cleanup Instructions:
    kubectl delete namespace frontend backend

LAB 65: etcd Quorum Loss and mvcc: database space exceeded Alarm

  • Difficulty: Advanced
  • Estimated Duration: 15 minutes
  • Domain: Troubleshooting (30%)
  • Learning Objective: Resolve a frozen etcd cluster locked in read-only mode by compacting historical revisions, defragmenting storage, and disarming alarms.
  • Prerequisites: SSH access to control plane node.
  • Cluster Environment: kubeadm cluster.
  • Initial State: Cluster refuses write operations with etcdserver: mvcc: database space exceeded.
  • Task Description:
    1. Attempt to create a pod: kubectl run test --image=nginx.
    2. Observe failure: etcdserver: mvcc: database space exceeded.
    3. SSH into control plane node.
    4. Query active alarms using etcdctl alarm list. Notice alarm NOSPACE.
    5. Query current etcd revision.
    6. Compact etcd history up to current revision.
    7. Defragment the database storage on disk using etcdctl defrag.
    8. Disarm the alarm using etcdctl alarm disarm.
    9. Verify write access is restored by successfully creating the test pod.
  • Constraints: Do not wipe the database or delete /var/lib/etcd.
  • Hints: Defrag alone will not clear the alarm; you must run alarm disarm.
  • Expected Result: Alarm is cleared and cluster writes succeed.
  • Verification:
    sudo ETCDCTL_API=3 etcdctl --endpoints=https://127.0.0.1:2379 \
  --cacert=/etc/kubernetes/pki/etcd/ca.crt --cert=/etc/kubernetes/pki/etcd/server.crt --key=/etc/kubernetes/pki/etcd/server.key \
  alarm list
# Expected: empty
kubectl run test-verify --image=nginx:alpine
# Expected: pod created successfully
  • Full Solution:
    # 1. Check alarm
sudo ETCDCTL_API=3 etcdctl --endpoints=https://127.0.0.1:2379 \
  --cacert=/etc/kubernetes/pki/etcd/ca.crt --cert=/etc/kubernetes/pki/etcd/server.crt --key=/etc/kubernetes/pki/etcd/server.key \
  alarm list

# 2. Find revision
REV=$(sudo ETCDCTL_API=3 etcdctl --endpoints=https://127.0.0.1:2379 \
  --cacert=/etc/kubernetes/pki/etcd/ca.crt --cert=/etc/kubernetes/pki/etcd/server.crt --key=/etc/kubernetes/pki/etcd/server.key \
  endpoint status --write-out="json" | jq .[0].Status.header.revision)

# 3. Compact
sudo ETCDCTL_API=3 etcdctl --endpoints=https://127.0.0.1:2379 \
  --cacert=/etc/kubernetes/pki/etcd/ca.crt --cert=/etc/kubernetes/pki/etcd/server.crt --key=/etc/kubernetes/pki/etcd/server.key \
  compact $REV

# 4. Defrag
sudo ETCDCTL_API=3 etcdctl --endpoints=https://127.0.0.1:2379 \
  --cacert=/etc/kubernetes/pki/etcd/ca.crt --cert=/etc/kubernetes/pki/etcd/server.crt --key=/etc/kubernetes/pki/etcd/server.key \
  defrag

# 5. Disarm
sudo ETCDCTL_API=3 etcdctl --endpoints=https://127.0.0.1:2379 \
  --cacert=/etc/kubernetes/pki/etcd/ca.crt --cert=/etc/kubernetes/pki/etcd/server.crt --key=/etc/kubernetes/pki/etcd/server.key \
  alarm disarm
  • Common Mistakes: Running compact but omitting defrag, which leaves host database file size unchanged.
  • Troubleshooting: Check disk usage of /var/lib/etcd/member/snap/db.
  • Cleanup Instructions:
    kubectl delete pod test-verify || true

LAB 66: Integrated Multi-Failure Cluster Outage (The CKA Grand Challenge)

  • Difficulty: Advanced
  • Estimated Duration: 20 minutes
  • Domain: Troubleshooting (30%)
  • Learning Objective: Solve a compound, cascading failure involving a dead kubelet, corrupted static pod manifest, broken CoreDNS, and locked Service.
  • Prerequisites: Full control plane and worker node SSH access.
  • Cluster Environment: Multi-node kubeadm cluster.
  • Initial State: Cluster completely down: kubectl connection refused.
  • Task Description:
    1. Triage Stage 1: Control plane is unreachable on port 6443. SSH to control plane. Kubelet is inactive because Linux swap was enabled. Disable swap and start kubelet.
    2. Triage Stage 2: Kubelet is running, but API Server static pod still fails to start. Inspect /etc/kubernetes/manifests/kube-apiserver.yaml. Fix a typo in --etcd-servers=https://127.0.0.1:23799 (extra 9 on port). API server boots!
    3. Triage Stage 3: worker-1 shows NotReady. SSH to worker-1. Container runtime is stopped. Start containerd and kubelet. Node transitions to Ready.
    4. Triage Stage 4: Workload Pods cannot resolve external or internal services. Inspect kube-system CoreDNS pods; they are running, but Service kube-dns has an incorrect targetPort (targetPort: 5353 instead of 53). Fix targetPort.
    5. Run end-to-end verification across the entire cluster.
  • Constraints: Solve each layer sequentially without rebooting nodes.
  • Hints: Follow the 6-layer diagnostic hierarchy (Hardware $\to$ Runtime $\to$ Kubelet $\to$ Static Pods $\to$ Networking $\to$ App).
  • Expected Result: All nodes Ready, all control plane components healthy, DNS resolves, and workloads serve traffic.
  • Verification:
    kubectl get nodes
kubectl get pods -A
kubectl run grand-check --image=busybox:1.36 --rm -it --restart=Never -- nslookup kubernetes.default
  • Full Solution:
    # Step 1: On Control Plane - Fix Swap
sudo swapoff -a
sudo systemctl restart kubelet

# Step 2: Fix API Server port typo
sudo sed -i 's/23799/2379/g' /etc/kubernetes/manifests/kube-apiserver.yaml
sleep 15

# Step 3: On worker-1 - Fix Containerd
ssh worker-1
sudo systemctl restart containerd
sudo systemctl restart kubelet
exit

# Step 4: Fix CoreDNS Service port
kubectl patch svc kube-dns -n kube-system --type='json' -p='[{"op": "replace", "path": "/spec/ports/0/targetPort", "value":53}]'

# Step 5: Verify
kubectl get nodes
  • Common Mistakes: Giving up at Step 1 or assuming the cluster is irreparably corrupted.
  • Troubleshooting: Keep checking component health via kubectl get --raw='/readyz?verbose'.
  • Cleanup Instructions: None (cluster is restored to health).

LAB 67: Advanced Chaos Recovery: Cross-Namespace Gateway API Failure, Missing ReferenceGrant & Kubelet Socket Mismatch

  • Difficulty: Advanced
  • Estimated Duration: 20 minutes
  • Domain: Troubleshooting (30%)
  • Learning Objective: Troubleshoot an advanced multi-tier infrastructure failure: a worker node reporting NotReady due to a misplaced CRI socket, combined with a detached cross-namespace Gateway API HTTPRoute failing traffic due to a missing ReferenceGrant.
  • Prerequisites: Gateway API CRDs v1.1+ installed, multi-node cluster.
  • Cluster Environment: Multi-node cluster with infra-cluster context.
  • Initial State:
    1. Worker node worker-2 is in NotReady condition.
    2. Namespace gateway-infra has Gateway prod-gw listening on HTTP port 80.
    3. Namespace checkout-app has Deployment checkout-web and Service checkout-svc.
    4. Namespace checkout-app has HTTPRoute checkout-route targeting prod-gw, but traffic to checkout.example.com returns 404 or connection refused.
  • Task Description:
    1. Diagnose why worker-2 is NotReady. SSH to worker-2, inspect kubelet journal logs, identify the misconfigured CRI socket path in /var/lib/kubelet/kubeadm-flags.env or /var/lib/kubelet/config.yaml, fix it, and restore the node to Ready.
    2. Inspect the condition of HTTPRoute checkout-route in namespace checkout-app.
    3. Notice that checkout-route has condition Accepted: False with reason RefNotPermitted because the parent Gateway is in gateway-infra while the route is in checkout-app.
    4. Author and apply a ReferenceGrant in namespace gateway-infra allowing HTTPRoutes from namespace checkout-app to bind to Gateway prod-gw.
    5. Verify HTTPRoute transitions to Accepted: True and traffic routes successfully to checkout-svc.
  • Constraints: Do not delete or recreate the Gateway or worker node. Resolve root causes in-place.
  • Hints: Use kubectl describe httproute checkout-route -n checkout-app to inspect conditions. Check kubectl get referencegrant -A.
  • Expected Result: worker-2 is Ready, and checkout-route is accepted and programmed.
  • Verification:
    kubectl get nodes worker-2
# Expected: Ready
kubectl get httproute checkout-route -n checkout-app -o jsonpath='{.status.parents[0].conditions[?(@.type=="Accepted")].status}'
# Expected: True
  • Full Solution:
    # Step 1: Fix worker-2 Kubelet CRI socket
ssh worker-2
sudo sed -i 's|/run/containerd/wrong.sock|/run/containerd/containerd.sock|g' /var/lib/kubelet/kubeadm-flags.env
sudo systemctl daemon-reload
sudo systemctl restart kubelet
exit

# Step 2: Inspect HTTPRoute status
kubectl describe httproute checkout-route -n checkout-app

# Step 3: Create ReferenceGrant in gateway-infra namespace
cat <<EOF | kubectl apply -f -
apiVersion: gateway.networking.k8s.io/v1beta1
kind: ReferenceGrant
metadata:
  name: allow-checkout-route
  namespace: gateway-infra
spec:
  from:
  - group: gateway.networking.k8s.io
    kind: HTTPRoute
    namespace: checkout-app
  to:
  - group: gateway.networking.k8s.io
    kind: Gateway
    name: prod-gw
EOF

# Step 4: Verify route accepted
kubectl get httproute checkout-route -n checkout-app
  • Common Mistakes: Creating the ReferenceGrant in the route's namespace (checkout-app) instead of the Gateway owner's namespace (gateway-infra). The resource granting permission must always reside in the namespace owning the target resource!
  • Troubleshooting: Check controller logs: kubectl logs -n gateway-infra -l app=gateway-controller.
  • Cleanup Instructions:
    kubectl delete referencegrant allow-checkout-route -n gateway-infra

VOLUME III: THE KUBERNETES VISUAL ARCHITECTURE ATLAS

PART 1: CORE ARCHITECTURE & PACKET FLOW SCHEMATICS (DIAGRAMS 01–32)

1. Complete Kubernetes Cluster Architecture

graph TB
    subgraph Control_Plane["CONTROL PLANE (MASTER)"]
        APIServer["kube-apiserver<br/>(Port 6443)"]
        Scheduler["kube-scheduler"]
        KCM["kube-controller-manager"]
        CCM["cloud-controller-manager"]
        ETCD[("etcd Cluster<br/>(Port 2379/2380)")]
        
        Scheduler -->|Watch / Bind| APIServer
        KCM -->|Reconcile State| APIServer
        CCM -->|Cloud Sync| APIServer
        APIServer <-->|Raft Consensus| ETCD
    end

    subgraph Worker_Node_1["WORKER NODE 1"]
        Kubelet1["kubelet (systemd)"]
        Proxy1["kube-proxy (iptables/IPVS)"]
        Runtime1["containerd (CRI Runtime)"]
        CNI1["CNI Plugin (veth / Bridge)"]
        Pod1["Pod A (10.244.1.10)"]
        Pod2["Pod B (10.244.1.11)"]
        
        Kubelet1 -->|gRPC / CRI| Runtime1
        Runtime1 --> Pod1
        Runtime1 --> Pod2
        CNI1 -.->|IP Allocation| Pod1
        CNI1 -.->|IP Allocation| Pod2
    end

    subgraph Worker_Node_2["WORKER NODE 2"]
        Kubelet2["kubelet (systemd)"]
        Proxy2["kube-proxy (iptables/IPVS)"]
        Runtime2["containerd (CRI Runtime)"]
        Pod3["Pod C (10.244.2.15)"]
        
        Kubelet2 -->|gRPC / CRI| Runtime2
        Runtime2 --> Pod3
    end

    Admin["Cluster Administrator / kubectl"] -->|HTTPS / mTLS| APIServer
    Kubelet1 -->|Heartbeat / Pod Status| APIServer
    Kubelet2 -->|Heartbeat / Pod Status| APIServer
    Proxy1 -->|Watch Endpoints| APIServer
    Proxy2 -->|Watch Endpoints| APIServer

Architectural Explanation:
The Kubernetes architecture divides cluster operations into the Control Plane and Worker Nodes. The kube-apiserver acts as the single central communications gateway; no component communicates directly with etcd other than the API server. Controllers and schedulers run continuously, watching the desired state via API server stream watches and reconciling reality to match. Worker nodes run kubelet (managing container lifecycles via CRI) and kube-proxy (programming network filter rules via iptables or IPVS).

2. Control Plane Component Inter-Communication

sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor Admin as Administrator (kubectl)
    participant API as kube-apiserver
    participant ETCD as etcd Store
    participant Sched as kube-scheduler
    participant KCM as kube-controller-mgr
    participant Kube as kubelet (Node)

    Admin->>API: POST /api/v1/namespaces/default/pods (Create Pod)
    API->>ETCD: Persist Pod Spec (nodeName: "")
    ETCD-->>API: Write Acknowledged
    API-->>Admin: 201 Created (Pod Pending)

    Note over API,Sched: Async Watch Stream Triggered
    API-->>Sched: Watch Event: Unassigned Pod Detected
    Sched->>Sched: Run Filtering (Predicates) & Scoring (Priorities)
    Sched->>API: POST /binding (Assign Pod to node-01)
    API->>ETCD: Update Pod Spec (nodeName: "node-01")
    ETCD-->>API: Write Acknowledged

    Note over API,Kube: Node Watch Stream Triggered
    API-->>Kube: Watch Event: Pod Scheduled to node-01
    Kube->>Kube: Request Runtime (CRI) & Network (CNI)
    Kube->>API: PATCH /status (Phase: Running, PodIP: 10.244.1.20)
    API->>ETCD: Persist Pod Status

Architectural Explanation:
All control-plane communication is asynchronous and mediated entirely by kube-apiserver watch streams. Neither the scheduler nor the kubelet poll; they maintain long-lived HTTP/2 streams watching specific API resource endpoints. When a scheduling decision is made, the scheduler writes a /binding subresource rather than mutating the Pod directly.

3. API Server Request Lifecycle

flowchart LR
    Request["Incoming HTTPS Request<br/>(Client / kubectl)"] --> AuthN{"1. Authentication<br/>(Cert, OIDC, Token)"}
    AuthN -- Rejected --> R401["401 Unauthorized"]
    AuthN -- Success --> AuthZ{"2. Authorization<br/>(RBAC, Node, Webhook)"}
    AuthZ -- Rejected --> R403["403 Forbidden"]
    AuthZ -- Success --> Mutating{"3. Mutating Webhooks<br/>(Modify Spec / Defaults)"}
    Mutating --> Schema{"4. Schema Validation<br/>(OpenAPI / Syntax)"}
    Schema -- Invalid --> R422["422 Unprocessable Entity"]
    Schema -- Valid --> Validating{"5. Validating Webhooks<br/>(Policy Enforcement)"}
    Validating -- Denied --> R400["400 Bad Request"]
    Validating -- Allowed --> ETCD[("6. etcd Storage<br/>(Write & Commit)")]

Architectural Explanation:
Every API request traverses a deterministic 6-stage pipeline:

  1. Authentication (AuthN): Resolves client identity to Username, UID, and Groups using Client Certificates (mTLS), Bearer Tokens (ServiceAccounts), or OIDC.
  2. Authorization (AuthZ): Evaluates RBAC Roles and ClusterRoles using Rule Evaluation (Node authorizer checks kubelet rights).
  3. Mutating Admission Webhooks: Injects defaults or modifies the specification (e.g., Istio sidecar injection).
  4. Object Schema Validation: Verifies schema definitions against OpenAPI v3 specifications.
  5. Validating Admission Webhooks: Enforces cluster-wide constraints without mutating (e.g., Gatekeeper, Kyverno).
  6. etcd Persistence: Object state is committed to disk via Raft consensus.

4. etcd Raft Distributed Consensus & Leader Election

stateDiagram-v2
    [*] --> Follower: Node Boot / Join
    Follower --> Candidate: Election Timeout Expires (150ms-300ms)
    Candidate --> Leader: Receives Majority Votes (Quorum: N/2 + 1)
    Candidate --> Follower: Discovers Current Leader or Newer Term
    Leader --> Follower: Steps Down if Higher Term Detected

Architectural Explanation:
etcd uses the Raft distributed consensus protocol. In an $N$-node cluster, a strict quorum of $\lfloor N/2 \rfloor + 1$ members is required to establish leadership and commit log entries:

  • 3-node cluster: Quorum is 2 (tolerates 1 failure).
  • 5-node cluster: Quorum is 3 (tolerates 2 failures). If quorum is lost, etcd refuses all write requests to prevent split-brain state corruption.

5. etcd Quorum Loss & Multi-Member Disaster Recovery

flowchart TD
    QuorumLoss["etcd Quorum Lost<br/>(2 of 3 Members Down)"] --> StopMembers["1. Stop kube-apiserver & etcd on all nodes"]
    StopMembers --> InspectSnapshot["2. Identify Latest Valid Snapshot<br/>etcdctl snapshot status snapshot.db"]
    InspectSnapshot --> RestoreDataDir["3. Restore to New Data Directory<br/>etcdctl snapshot restore snapshot.db<br/>--data-dir=/var/lib/etcd-restored<br/>--initial-cluster=... --initial-cluster-token=..."]
    RestoreDataDir --> UpdateStaticPod["4. Update /etc/kubernetes/manifests/etcd.yaml<br/>Modify hostPath volume mount to /var/lib/etcd-restored"]
    UpdateStaticPod --> KubeletRestart["5. Kubelet Automatically Recreates etcd Static Pod"]
    KubeletRestart --> VerifyHealth["6. Verify Health: etcdctl endpoint health<br/>Verify API Server: kubectl get nodes"]

Architectural Explanation:
When an etcd cluster experiences catastrophic failure, manual snapshot restoration is required. Restoring must always target a new, non-existing directory (--data-dir=/var/lib/etcd-restored) to prevent collision with corrupted wal files. The static pod manifest is then updated so kubelet restarts the container pointing to the clean database.

6. kube-scheduler Two-Phase Pipeline: Filtering & Scoring

flowchart TD
    UnscheduledPod["Unscheduled Pod Spec"] --> FilteringPhase["PHASE 1: FILTERING (Predicates)"]
    
    subgraph Filtering["Filtering Predicates (Drop Ineligible Nodes)"]
        F1["NodeResourcesFit (CPU / Memory Requests)"]
        F2["NodeName & NodeSelector Matching"]
        F3["PodFitsHostPorts (Port Collisions)"]
        F4["PodToleratesNodeTaints (Taint Check)"]
        F5["NodeAffinity & TopologySpread"]
    end
    
    FilteringPhase --> Filtering
    Filtering --> FilterEval{"Any Nodes Pass?"}
    FilterEval -- No --> PodPending["Pod Stuck in Pending<br/>(0/3 nodes available: Insufficient memory)"]
    FilterEval -- Yes (1 Node) --> BindNode["Directly Bind to Only Passing Node"]
    FilterEval -- Yes (>1 Nodes) --> ScoringPhase["PHASE 2: SCORING (Priorities)"]

    subgraph Scoring["Scoring Priorities (Weight 0-100)"]
        S1["NodeResourcesBalancedAllocation"]
        S2["ImageLocalityPriority (Cached Image On Node)"]
        S3["NodeAffinityPriority (preferredDuringScheduling)"]
        S4["TaintTolerationPriority"]
    end

    ScoringPhase --> Scoring
    Scoring --> RankNodes["Calculate Weighted Sum Across All Plugins"]
    RankNodes --> SelectedNode["Node with Highest Cumulative Score Selected"]
    SelectedNode --> Bind["Write /binding Subresource to API Server"]

Architectural Explanation:
Scheduling operates in two distinct phases:

  1. Filtering (Predicates): Evaluates hard requirements. If a node fails even one predicate (insufficient RAM, untolerated taint), it is removed from consideration.
  2. Scoring (Priorities): Evaluates soft preferences on surviving nodes, assigning scores from 0 to 100. Weights are applied, and the node with the highest aggregate score is chosen.

7. kube-controller-manager Reconciliation Loops

flowchart LR
    DesiredState["Desired State<br/>(etcd Spec)"] --> Reconciler{"Reconciliation<br/>Control Loop"}
    ActualState["Actual State<br/>(Cluster Telemetry)"] --> Reconciler
    Reconciler -- Match --> Idle["Sleep & Watch"]
    Reconciler -- Deviation Detected --> Action["Execute Corrective Actions<br/>(Create / Terminate / Update Pods)"]
    Action --> APICall["POST / DELETE to API Server"]

Architectural Explanation:
Every Kubernetes controller runs an infinite control loop (syncHandler). It computes the diff $\Delta = \text{Desired State} - \text{Actual State}$. If replicas desired is 5 and actual is 3, the Deployment controller instructs the ReplicaSet controller to issue API requests creating 2 new Pods.

8. kubelet Internal Architecture

graph TB
    subgraph Kubelet["kubelet Daemon (systemd)"]
        SyncLoop["syncLoop (Core Event Loop)"]
        PLEG["PLEG (Pod Lifecycle Event Generator)"]
        cAdvisor["cAdvisor (Metrics & Cgroup Telemetry)"]
        ProbeManager["Probe Manager (Liveness/Readiness)"]
        VolumeManager["Volume Manager (Attach & Mount)"]
    end

    APIServer["kube-apiserver"] <-->|Watch Pods / Report Status| SyncLoop
    PLEG -->|Runtime State Changes| SyncLoop
    cAdvisor -->|Node & Container Stats| SyncLoop
    ProbeManager -->|Health Telemetry| SyncLoop
    VolumeManager -->|Mount CSI Devices| SyncLoop

    SyncLoop -->|gRPC CRI| ContainerRuntime["containerd (/run/containerd/containerd.sock)"]
    SyncLoop -->|gRPC CNI| CNIPlugin["CNI Network Plugin (/opt/cni/bin)"]
    SyncLoop -->|gRPC CSI| CSIPlugin["CSI Node Plugin (/var/lib/kubelet/csi-plugins)"]

Architectural Explanation:
The kubelet is the primary node agent. The syncLoop orchestrates updates based on events from:

  • Pod manifests (from API server watch, static pod directory /etc/kubernetes/manifests/, or HTTP endpoints).
  • PLEG (Pod Lifecycle Event Generator), which queries the container runtime via CRI to detect stopped or crashed containers.
  • Probe Manager, which executes HTTP, TCP, exec, or gRPC probes.

9. containerd Architecture, shim-v2, and runc

graph LR
    Kubelet["kubelet"] -->|CRI gRPC API| Containerd["containerd daemon<br/>(/run/containerd/containerd.sock)"]
    Containerd --> CtrShim["containerd-shim-v2<br/>(Per Pod Process)"]
    CtrShim --> Runc["runc (OCI Runtime)"]
    Runc --> Kernel["Linux Kernel<br/>(Namespaces, Cgroups, Chroot)"]
    Kernel --> Container["Running Container Process"]
    CtrShim -.->|Maintains STDIO & Exit Status| Container

Architectural Explanation:
containerd decouples pod execution from daemon restarts using containerd-shim-v2. When a container starts, runc initializes the Linux namespaces and cgroups, hands execution off to the containerized binary, and exits immediately. The lightweight shim stays alive as the container's parent process, holding open standard I/O pipes and capturing exit codes so containerd can restart without stopping running workloads.

10. Pod Lifecycle State Machine

stateDiagram-v2
    [*] --> Pending: Manifest Created
    Pending --> ContainerCreating: Scheduled to Node
    ContainerCreating --> Running: All InitContainers Completed & App Started
    Running --> CrashLoopBackOff: Container Exits with Non-Zero Code
    CrashLoopBackOff --> Running: Backoff Exponential Delay Elapsed (10s..300s)
    Running --> Succeeded: Run-To-Completion (Exit 0)
    Running --> Failed: Container Failed (restartPolicy: Never)
    Running --> Terminating: Deletion Request Received (SIGTERM -> Grace Period -> SIGKILL)
    Terminating --> [*]: Volume Unmounted & CNI Teardown

Architectural Explanation:
A Pod progresses through strict phases. CrashLoopBackOff is not an official phase but a condition indicating repeated container termination. Kubernetes applies an exponential backoff delay (10s, 20s, 40s... capped at 300s) before re-attempting initialization.

11. Multi-Container Pod Design Patterns

graph TD
    subgraph Pod["Pod Network & IPC Namespace (Shared localhost)"]
        Init["Init Container<br/>(Runs to completion first)"]
        App["Main Application Container<br/>(Port 8080)"]
        Sidecar["Sidecar Container<br/>(Log Shipper / Envoy Proxy)"]
        SharedVol["Shared Volume (emptyDir)<br/>/var/log/app"]
    end

    Init -->|1. Setup Configuration & DB Schema| SharedVol
    App -->|2. Writes Application Logs| SharedVol
    Sidecar -->|3. Tails Logs & Streams to Cloud| SharedVol
    Sidecar <-->|Localhost Intercept (127.0.0.1:8080)| App

Architectural Explanation:
Multi-container Pods share:

  1. Network Namespace: Same IP address, port space, and localhost loopback interface.
  2. IPC Namespace: Shared POSIX shared memory and semaphores.
  3. Mounted Volumes: Containers share filesystem directories via emptyDir or shared PersistentVolumes. Init containers run sequentially to full exit code 0 before the main application and sidecars start in parallel.

12. Deployment, ReplicaSet, and Pod Cascading Hierarchy

graph TD
    Deploy["Deployment: web-app<br/>(spec.replicas: 3, strategy: RollingUpdate)"]
    RS1["ReplicaSet: web-app-v1 (hash: 6bf579)<br/>replicas: 0 (Historical)"]
    RS2["ReplicaSet: web-app-v2 (hash: 84d99c)<br/>replicas: 3 (Active)"]
    
    Pod1["Pod: web-app-v2-a"]
    Pod2["Pod: web-app-v2-b"]
    Pod3["Pod: web-app-v2-c"]

    Deploy -->|Owns & Manages| RS1
    Deploy -->|Owns & Manages| RS2
    RS2 -->|Owns| Pod1
    RS2 -->|Owns| Pod2
    RS2 -->|Owns| Pod3

Architectural Explanation:
Deployments do not create or manage Pods directly. A Deployment creates and manages ReplicaSets (identified by a 10-character pod-template-hash), and the ReplicaSets create and monitor the individual Pods. Rollbacks are executed simply by scaling up the old ReplicaSet while scaling down the current one.

13. Rolling Update Progression with maxSurge and maxUnavailable

sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    participant D as Deployment (replicas: 4, maxSurge: 1, maxUnavailable: 1)
    participant V1 as RS v1 (Active: 4)
    participant V2 as RS v2 (New: 0)

    Note over D: Update Container Image Triggered
    D->>V2: Scale Up to 1 (Surge allowed: 4+1 = 5 total)
    V2-->>D: Pod 1 Running & Ready
    D->>V1: Scale Down to 3 (Unavailable allowed: 4-1 = 3 min ready)
    D->>V2: Scale Up to 2
    V2-->>D: Pod 2 Running & Ready
    D->>V1: Scale Down to 2
    D->>V2: Scale Up to 3
