    V2-->>D: Pod 3 Running & Ready
    D->>V1: Scale Down to 1
    D->>V2: Scale Up to 4
    V2-->>D: Pod 4 Running & Ready
    D->>V1: Scale Down to 0
    Note over D: Rollout Fully Completed

Architectural Explanation:
Rolling updates preserve availability using two constraints:

  • maxSurge: Maximum number of Pods that can be scheduled above the desired replica count.
  • maxUnavailable: Maximum number of Pods that can be unavailable during the update. During an update, new Pods must pass readiness probes before older Pods are scheduled for termination.

14. StatefulSet Stable Network Identity & Ordered Scaling

graph TB
    subgraph StatefulSet["StatefulSet: db (replicas: 3)"]
        Pod0["db-0 (Ordinal 0)<br/>PV: data-db-0 (10Gi)"]
        Pod1["db-1 (Ordinal 1)<br/>PV: data-db-1 (10Gi)"]
        Pod2["db-2 (Ordinal 2)<br/>PV: data-db-2 (10Gi)"]
    end

    Headless["Headless Service: db-service (clusterIP: None)"]
    
    Headless -.->|DNS: db-0.db-service.default.svc.cluster.local| Pod0
    Headless -.->|DNS: db-1.db-service.default.svc.cluster.local| Pod1
    Headless -.->|DNS: db-2.db-service.default.svc.cluster.local| Pod2

Architectural Explanation:
StatefulSets provide:

  1. Deterministic Ordinals: Pods are named $(statefulset-name)-$(ordinal) from 0 to $N-1$.
  2. Sequential Operations: Pod $i$ must be Running and Ready before Pod $i+1$ is initialized. Scale-down occurs in reverse order.
  3. Persistent Volume Attachment: Each replica receives an independent PersistentVolume via volumeClaimTemplates that persists across pod deletion.
  4. Stable Network Identity: Paired with a Headless Service (clusterIP: None), each Pod receives a predictable DNS A-record.

15. DaemonSet Node Scheduling & Taint Tolerations

graph TD
    subgraph Nodes["Cluster Nodes"]
        N1["Control Plane<br/>Taint: node-role.kubernetes.io/control-plane:NoSchedule"]
        N2["Worker Node 1<br/>No Taints"]
        N3["Worker Node 2 (Edge)<br/>Taint: dedicated=edge:NoSchedule"]
    end

    DS["DaemonSet: fluentd-logging"]
    
    DS -->|Tolerates control-plane taint| P1["fluentd Pod on Control Plane"]
    DS -->|Standard Scheduling| P2["fluentd Pod on Worker 1"]
    DS -.->|Blocked without edge toleration| N3
    P1 --> N1
    P2 --> N2

Architectural Explanation:
A DaemonSet ensures that a copy of a Pod runs on all (or selected) nodes. By default, DaemonSet pods automatically include tolerations for standard node taints (node.kubernetes.io/not-ready, unreachable, disk-pressure). To run on control-plane nodes or specialized nodes with custom taints, explicit tolerations must be defined in the DaemonSet pod template.

16. Job & CronJob Execution Pipeline

flowchart TD
    CronJob["CronJob: report-generator<br/>schedule: '0 2 * * *' (Daily 2 AM)"] --> TimeTrigger{"2:00 AM Reached"}
    TimeTrigger --> CreateJob["Creates Job: report-generator-28491820"]
    CreateJob --> JobController["Job Controller Reconciler"]
    JobController --> LaunchPod["Launches Pod (restartPolicy: OnFailure)"]
    LaunchPod --> RunContainer["Executes Batch Workload"]
    RunContainer -- Exit 0 --> JobComplete["Job Status: Completed (Succeeded = 1)"]
    RunContainer -- Exit 1 --> CheckBackoff{"backoffLimit (e.g. 6) Exceeded?"}
    CheckBackoff -- No --> Reattempt["Exponential Backoff & Relaunch Pod"]
    CheckBackoff -- Yes --> JobFailed["Job Status: Failed"]

Architectural Explanation:
CronJobs manage time-based execution by instantiating Jobs. Jobs guarantee batch run-to-completion. Unlike Deployments, Pods managed by a Job must declare restartPolicy: OnFailure or Never. If a container fails, the Job controller retries until backoffLimit is reached.

17. ConfigMap and Secret Injection Mechanics

graph LR
    subgraph Storage["etcd Storage"]
        CM["ConfigMap: app-config<br/>(DATABASE_URL, LOG_LEVEL)"]
        SEC["Secret: app-secret<br/>(API_KEY, DB_PASS)"]
    end

    subgraph Pod["Target Pod"]
        Env["Environment Variables<br/>(env / envFrom)"]
        VolumeMount["Volume Mount<br/>/etc/config/app.properties<br/>/etc/secrets/token"]
    end

    CM -->|Injected at Pod Creation| Env
    SEC -->|Injected at Pod Creation| Env
    CM -->|Mounted as Files| VolumeMount
    SEC -->|Mounted as tmpfs Files| VolumeMount

Architectural Explanation:
ConfigMaps and Secrets can be consumed via:

  1. Environment Variables: Values are evaluated at container creation. Modifying the ConfigMap does not update running environment variables without a pod restart.
  2. Volume Mounts: Injected as files. Kubernetes atomic directory swapping ensures files are dynamically updated without restarting the pod (eventual consistency within kubelet sync periods). Secrets are stored in tmpfs (RAM) on the node to prevent disk persistence.

18. Resource Requests, Limits, and Kernel CFS Quotas

flowchart TD
    PodSpec["Pod Spec:<br/>resources.requests.cpu: 500m<br/>resources.limits.cpu: 1000m<br/>resources.requests.memory: 256Mi<br/>resources.limits.memory: 512Mi"] --> Translation["Kubelet Translates to Linux Cgroup Parameters"]
    
    Translation --> CPUReq["cpu.shares = 512 (Relative Priority)"]
    Translation --> CPULim["cpu.cfs_quota_us = 100000<br/>cpu.cfs_period_us = 100000<br/>(Hard CFS Throttling)"]
    Translation --> MemReq["memory.min / low (OOM Score Adjustment)"]
    Translation --> MemLim["memory.max = 536870912 Bytes (Hard Limit)"]

    CPULim --> CPUExceed{"App Exceeds 1000m CPU?"}
    CPUExceed -- Yes --> Throttled["Kernel Throttles CPU Execution Cycles<br/>(No Pod Kill)"]

    MemLim --> MemExceed{"App Exceeds 512Mi RAM?"}
    MemExceed -- Yes --> OOMKill["Linux Kernel OOM Killer Invoked<br/>Exit Code 137 (OOMKilled)"]

Architectural Explanation:

  • CPU Requests: Used by kube-scheduler for placement and translated to kernel cpu.shares.
  • CPU Limits: Enforced via Completely Fair Scheduler (CFS) bandwidth quotas. Exceeding limits results in CPU cycle throttling, not termination.
  • Memory Requests: Determines QoS class (Guaranteed, Burstable, BestEffort) and OOM-score adjustment.
  • Memory Limits: Enforced as a hard memory ceiling. Exceeding memory limits triggers an instantaneous kernel OOMKilled (Exit Code 137).

19. Liveness, Readiness, and Startup Probe Execution Lifecycle

flowchart TD
    Start["Container Started"] --> StartupCheck{"Startup Probe<br/>Defined?"}
    
    StartupCheck -- Yes --> RunStartup["Execute Startup Probe"]
    RunStartup -- Failing --> CheckStartupLimit{"failureThreshold<br/>Exceeded?"}
    CheckStartupLimit -- No --> RunStartup
    CheckStartupLimit -- Yes --> RestartStartup["Restart Container"]
    
    StartupCheck -- No / Succeeded --> ParallelChecks["Run Liveness & Readiness Concurrently"]
    
    subgraph ActiveChecks["Active Health Checking"]
        Readiness["Readiness Probe<br/>(Is app ready to receive traffic?)"]
        Liveness["Liveness Probe<br/>(Is app deadlocked / broken?)"]
    end

    ParallelChecks --> Readiness
    ParallelChecks --> Liveness

    Readiness -- Pass --> AddEndpoints["Add Pod IP to Service EndpointSlice<br/>(Traffic Flows)"]
    Readiness -- Fail --> RemoveEndpoints["Remove Pod IP from EndpointSlice<br/>(No Traffic Sent to Pod)"]

    Liveness -- Pass --> MaintainRunning["Container Remains Running"]
    Liveness -- Fail --> KubeletRestart["Kubelet Kills Container & Triggers Restart"]

Architectural Explanation:

  1. Startup Probe: Disables liveness and readiness checks during slow initialization. If it fails, the container is killed.
  2. Readiness Probe: Controls service routing. A failed readiness probe removes the Pod from Service EndpointSlices without killing the container.
  3. Liveness Probe: Detects deadlocks and internal application halts. A failed liveness probe triggers an immediate container restart by the kubelet.

20. Node Affinity, Anti-Affinity, and Topology Spread Constraints

graph TD
    Pod["Scheduled Pod Spec"]
    
    subgraph HardReq["Hard Requirements (RequiredDuringSchedulingIgnoredDuringExecution)"]
        NodeAffinity["Node Affinity:<br/>topology.kubernetes.io/zone In [us-east-1a, us-east-1b]"]
        PodAntiAffinity["Pod Anti-Affinity:<br/>MatchLabel: app=web (Do not co-locate with another web pod)"]
    end

    subgraph Topology["Topology Spread Constraints"]
        Spread["topologySpreadConstraints:<br/>maxSkew: 1<br/>topologyKey: topology.kubernetes.io/zone<br/>whenUnsatisfiable: DoNotSchedule"]
    end

    Pod --> HardReq
    Pod --> Topology
    HardReq --> Filter["Filtering Phase: Drops non-matching nodes"]
    Topology --> Balance["Distributes replicas evenly across availability zones"]

Architectural Explanation:

  • requiredDuringSchedulingIgnoredDuringExecution: Strict rule. If no node matches, the Pod remains Pending.
  • preferredDuringSchedulingIgnoredDuringExecution: Soft rule used during the scoring phase.
  • topologySpreadConstraints: Ensures high availability by preventing pods from concentrating in a single failure domain (zone, rack, or host), strictly bounding skew by maxSkew.

21. Taints, Tolerations, and Node Cordon/Drain Eviction

sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor Admin as Administrator
    participant API as kube-apiserver
    participant Node as Worker Node (node-02)
    participant Pods as Workload Pods

    Admin->>API: kubectl cordon node-02
    API->>Node: Mark unschedulable (node.spec.unschedulable = true)
    Note over Node: No new pods will be scheduled on node-02

    Admin->>API: kubectl drain node-02 --ignore-daemonsets --delete-emptydir-data
    API->>Pods: Send Eviction API request to each Pod on node-02
    Note over API: Respects PodDisruptionBudgets (PDB)
    Pods->>Pods: Receive SIGTERM & Graceful Shutdown (30s)
    API->>Node: Delete Pods from node-02
    Note over API: Deployment Controller reschedules replacement Pods on other nodes

    Admin->>Node: Perform OS Patching / Kubelet Upgrade / Reboot
    Admin->>API: kubectl uncordon node-02
    API->>Node: Mark schedulable (node.spec.unschedulable = false)

Architectural Explanation:

  • Taints: Applied to nodes (key=value:Effect). Effects include NoSchedule, PreferNoSchedule, and NoExecute.
  • Tolerations: Declared on Pods to allow scheduling on tainted nodes.
  • Cordoning: Marks a node unschedulable without disturbing running pods.
  • Draining: Safely evicts running pods via the Eviction API (honoring PDBs) so the host can undergo maintenance.

22. Pod Priority, PriorityClasses, and Preemption

flowchart TD
    HighPod["Incoming High-Priority Pod<br/>(priorityClassName: production-critical, Value: 1000000)"] --> Sched["kube-scheduler Evaluates Nodes"]
    Sched --> FitCheck{"Can Pod fit on any node<br/>without changes?"}
    FitCheck -- Yes --> ScheduleStandard["Schedule Normally"]
    FitCheck -- No --> Preemption["PREEMPTION ALGORITHM TRIGGERED"]

    Preemption --> ScanNodes["Scan nodes for lower-priority pods"]
    ScanNodes --> SelectVictims["Select lowest-priority victim pods<br/>(priorityClassName: batch-low, Value: 100)"]
    SelectVictims --> EvictVictim["Send Eviction / Delete to Victim Pods"]
    EvictVictim --> WaitGrace["Wait for termination grace period"]
    WaitGrace --> BindHigh["Bind High-Priority Pod to Freed Node Capacity"]

Architectural Explanation:
When high-priority workloads cannot be scheduled due to insufficient resources, the scheduler's preemption logic evicts lower-priority pods. The scheduler sets nominatedNodeName on the high-priority pod while waiting for the victims to terminate cleanly.

23. Horizontal Pod Autoscaler (HPA) Metrics Flow

sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    participant App as Pods (App Replicas)
    participant cAd as cAdvisor (Node Kubelet)
    participant MS as metrics-server
    participant HPA as HPA Controller
    participant Dep as Deployment Controller

    App->>cAd: Execute Workload (CPU/Memory Consumption)
    cAd->>cAd: Aggregate Cgroup Telemetry
    MS->>cAd: Scrape Summary API (/stats/summary) every 15s
    HPA->>MS: Query Metrics API (metrics.k8s.io/v1beta1) every 15s
    Note over HPA: Compute Formula:<br/>desiredReplicas = ceil[currentReplicas * (currentMetric / targetMetric)]
    HPA->>Dep: Update Scale Subresource (e.g. Scale 2 -> 5 Replicas)
    Dep->>App: Spawn Additional Pods

Architectural Explanation:
The HPA controller queries the metrics.k8s.io API provided by metrics-server. It computes the target scale using the formula: $$\text{desiredReplicas} = \left\lceil \text{currentReplicas} \times \left( \frac{\text{currentMetricValue}}{\text{targetMetricValue}} \right) \right\rceil$$ Stabilization windows prevent rapid thrashing (flapping) during metric spikes.

24. ResourceQuota and LimitRange Admission Controller

flowchart LR
    CreateReq["kubectl create Pod Spec<br/>(cpu: 500m, memory: 1Gi)"] --> LR{"LimitRange Admission Check"}
    
    subgraph LimitRangeRules["LimitRange (Per Pod/Container Defaults & Bounds)"]
        LR1["Inject default requests/limits if missing"]
        LR2["Enforce min/max allowed per container"]
    end

    LR --> LimitRangeRules
    LimitRangeRules -- Violates Bounds --> RejectLR["403 Forbidden: LimitRange Exceeded"]
    LimitRangeRules -- Passes --> RQ{"ResourceQuota Admission Check"}

    subgraph ResourceQuotaRules["ResourceQuota (Namespace Aggregate Capacity)"]
        RQ1["Sum current allocated requests + new request"]
        RQ2["Compare against hard quota ceiling"]
    end

    RQ --> ResourceQuotaRules
    ResourceQuotaRules -- Exceeds Aggregate --> RejectRQ["403 Forbidden: Exceeded Quota"]
    ResourceQuotaRules -- Under Ceiling --> Allow["Commit Pod to etcd"]

Architectural Explanation:

  • LimitRange: Enforces constraints on individual Pods/containers within a namespace (min/max bounds and automatic default injection).
  • ResourceQuota: Enforces aggregate limits on total resources (CPU, Memory, Pod count, PVC count) consumed across the entire namespace.

25. Kubernetes Networking: Intra-Node vs. Inter-Node Packet Flow

flowchart TD
    subgraph Node1["WORKER NODE 1 (192.168.1.10)"]
        PodA["Pod A<br/>10.244.1.5"] <-->|veth pair| Bridge1["cbr0 / CNI Bridge"]
        PodB["Pod B<br/>10.244.1.6"] <-->|veth pair| Bridge1
        Bridge1 <-->|Host Route| VXLAN1["VXLAN Tunnel Interface (flannel.1 / calico)"]
    end

    subgraph Node2["WORKER NODE 2 (192.168.1.20)"]
        PodC["Pod C<br/>10.244.2.8"] <-->|veth pair| Bridge2["cbr0 / CNI Bridge"]
        Bridge2 <-->|Host Route| VXLAN2["VXLAN Tunnel Interface (flannel.1 / calico)"]
    end

    PodA -.->|Intra-Node: Local Bridge Switching| PodB
    VXLAN1 == Encapsulated UDP Packet (Port 4789 / 8472) ==> VXLAN2
    VXLAN2 -->|Decapsulate| PodC

Architectural Explanation:

  1. Intra-Node: Traffic between Pod A and Pod B stays entirely on the local Linux bridge (cbr0 or veth pair). No encapsulation or external routing occurs.
  2. Inter-Node: Traffic destined for Pod C (different subnet 10.244.2.0/24) is routed to the overlay interface (e.g., flannel.1), encapsulated inside an outer UDP packet, transmitted across the physical network, and decapsulated by the destination node's CNI interface.

26. Kubernetes Service Routing: iptables vs. IPVS

graph TD
    Client["Client Pod (10.244.1.5)"] -->|Connects to Service ClusterIP 10.96.0.100:80| Kernel["Linux Kernel Netfilter"]

    subgraph Mode["kube-proxy Mode"]
        IPTables["iptables Mode (Sequential Chains)<br/>PREROUTING -> KUBE-SERVICES -> KUBE-SVC-XXX<br/>Random probability load balancing: 1/n"]
        IPVS["IPVS Mode (Kernel Hash Tables)<br/>Netlink IPVS Virtual Server (10.96.0.100:80)<br/>Algorithms: Round-Robin, Least-Connection, Source-Hash"]
    end

    Kernel --> Mode
    Mode --> DNAT["DNAT: Translate ClusterIP to Endpoint Pod IP (10.244.2.15:8080)"]
    DNAT --> TargetPod["Target Pod (10.244.2.15)"]

Architectural Explanation:
Services do not run as processes; they are virtual IPs programmed by kube-proxy.

  • iptables: Uses sequentially evaluated packet filter chains. Rules grow $O(N)$ with service counts, introducing latency at scale.
  • IPVS: Uses kernel hash tables offering $O(1)$ lookups, supporting large-scale enterprise deployments with granular load-balancing algorithms.

27. EndpointSlice Architecture

graph TD
    Svc["Service: api-service<br/>(ClusterIP: 10.96.10.50)"]
    
    subgraph Traditional["Legacy Endpoints (Single Monolithic Object)"]
        EP["Endpoints: api-service<br/>Contains 5,000 Pod IPs<br/>(1.5MB etcd object updated on ANY pod change)"]
    end

    subgraph Modern["EndpointSlices (Scalable Partitioning)"]
        EPS1["EndpointSlice: api-service-1<br/>Endpoints 1 to 100"]
        EPS2["EndpointSlice: api-service-2<br/>Endpoints 101 to 200"]
        EPS3["EndpointSlice: api-service-N<br/>Endpoints N to N+100"]
    end

    Svc --> Traditional
    Svc --> Modern

Architectural Explanation:
Legacy Endpoints stored all backing pod IPs in a single API resource. For a service with 2,000 replicas, a single pod scaling event forced the API server and all kube-proxy instances to re-transmit the entire multi-megabyte object. EndpointSlices partition endpoints into chunks of 100, dramatically reducing control-plane bandwidth and CPU load.

28. Cluster DNS Resolution: CoreDNS

sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    participant Pod as Application Pod
    participant CoreDNS as CoreDNS (10.96.0.10)
    participant Upstream as External Upstream DNS (8.8.8.8)

    Note over Pod: Read /etc/resolv.conf<br/>nameserver 10.96.0.10<br/>search default.svc.cluster.local svc.cluster.local cluster.local
    Pod->>CoreDNS: Query: backend-svc (Appends default.svc.cluster.local)
    CoreDNS->>CoreDNS: Match in-memory Service cache
    CoreDNS-->>Pod: Return Service ClusterIP (10.96.40.22)

    Pod->>CoreDNS: Query: external.example.com
    CoreDNS->>CoreDNS: Does not match cluster.local zone
    CoreDNS->>Upstream: Forward DNS Query to 8.8.8.8
    Upstream-->>CoreDNS: Return Public IP (93.184.216.34)
    CoreDNS-->>Pod: Return Public IP

Architectural Explanation:
Every Pod inherits /etc/resolv.conf configured with nameserver 10.96.0.10 (CoreDNS Service IP) and search paths. Queries for bare names (backend-svc) sequentially append search domains until a match is resolved by CoreDNS. External domains are forwarded upstream to host resolvers.

29. Ingress Controller Architecture

graph TD
    Client["External Client (HTTPS:443)"] --> LB["External Load Balancer"]
    LB --> NodePort["NodePort 30443 (Worker Nodes)"]
    NodePort --> IngressPod["Ingress Controller Pod (nginx / traefik)"]
    
    subgraph Rules["Ingress Resource (networking.k8s.io/v1)"]
        R1["Host: api.example.com /path: /v1 -> Service api-svc:8080"]
        R2["Host: web.example.com /path: /   -> Service web-svc:80"]
    end

    IngressPod --> Rules
    Rules --> PodA["api-svc Pod (10.244.1.15)"]
    Rules --> PodB["web-svc Pod (10.244.2.22)"]

Architectural Explanation:
An Ingress Controller is a specialized reverse proxy running inside the cluster. It bypasses kube-proxy ClusterIP routing for performance, querying EndpointSlices directly to stream traffic straight to container endpoints with TLS termination and path rewrites.

30. Gateway API Architecture & Role Separation

graph TD
    subgraph InfraProvider["Infrastructure Provider"]
        GC["GatewayClass: standard-gateway<br/>(controller: acme.io/gateway-controller)"]
    end

    subgraph ClusterAdmin["Cluster Administrator"]
        GW["Gateway: prod-gateway<br/>(Listeners: HTTPS:443, Host: *.example.com)"]
    end

    subgraph AppDeveloper["Application Developer (Namespaces)"]
        HR1["HTTPRoute: payments-route<br/>(ParentRef: prod-gateway, Path: /payments)"]
        HR2["HTTPRoute: orders-route<br/>(ParentRef: prod-gateway, Path: /orders)"]
        Svc1["Service: payments-svc"]
        Svc2["Service: orders-svc"]
    end

    GC -->|Defines Controller| GW
    GW -->|Attached By| HR1
    GW -->|Attached By| HR2
    HR1 --> Svc1
    HR2 --> Svc2

Architectural Explanation:
Gateway API succeeds Ingress by splitting routing into three decoupled personas:

  1. Infrastructure Provider (GatewayClass): Defines the underlying load-balancing implementation.
  2. Cluster Administrator (Gateway): Declares network ports, IP allocations, and TLS certificates.
  3. Application Developer (HTTPRoute): Defines routing rules, header matches, and canary weights without needing cluster-admin privileges.

31. NetworkPolicy Traffic Filtering Matrix

graph LR
    subgraph External["Traffic Sources"]
        World["Internet / Outside Cluster"]
        OtherNS["Pods in Other Namespaces"]
        SameNS["Pods in Same Namespace"]
    end

    subgraph Target["Target Pod (app: secure-db, port: 5432)"]
        NetPol["NetworkPolicy Enforcement<br/>policyTypes: [Ingress, Egress]"]
        DBPod["Postgres Container (5432)"]
    end

    World -- Blocked by Default Deny --> NetPol
    OtherNS -- Allowed if namespaceSelector Matches --> NetPol
    SameNS -- Allowed if podSelector Matches app: backend --> NetPol
    NetPol --> DBPod

Architectural Explanation:

  • By default, all Pod network traffic is non-isolated (open allow-all).
  • The moment a NetworkPolicy selects a Pod, it transitions to default-deny for that policy type (Ingress or Egress).
  • Traffic is whitelisted only when source criteria (podSelector, namespaceSelector, ipBlock) and destination port match explicitly.

32. CSI Architecture and Volume Mount Protocol

sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    participant API as kube-apiserver
    participant Attacher as CSI External Attacher
    participant Provisioner as CSI External Provisioner
    participant Kube as Node Kubelet
    participant Driver as CSI Node Plugin

    API->>Provisioner: Create PVC with StorageClass
    Provisioner->>Provisioner: Provision Remote Storage Disk (e.g. AWS EBS / Ceph)
    Provisioner->>API: Create PersistentVolume (PV) bound to PVC

    API->>Attacher: Pod Scheduled to node-01
    Attacher->>Attacher: Call ControllerPublishVolume (Attach Disk to VM)
    Attacher->>API: Update VolumeAttachment Status (Attached)

    API->>Kube: VolumeAttachment Ready for Mount
    Kube->>Driver: NodeStageVolume (Format filesystem e.g. ext4)
    Kube->>Driver: NodePublishVolume (Bind mount to /var/lib/kubelet/pods/UID/volumes)
    Kube->>Kube: Launch Container with Mounted Filesystem

Architectural Explanation:
Container Storage Interface (CSI) separates storage plugins from core Kubernetes code:

  1. Controller Plugins: Run on control plane (csi-provisioner, csi-attacher) to provision and attach block devices to nodes.
  2. Node Plugins: Run as DaemonSets on worker nodes (csi-node-driver-registrar) to execute local format (NodeStageVolume) and mount (NodePublishVolume) calls.

VOLUME III: THE KUBERNETES VISUAL ARCHITECTURE ATLAS

PART 2: SYSTEMATIC TROUBLESHOOTING DECISION TREES (DIAGRAMS 33–64)

33. Universal 60-Second Node Triage Decision Tree

flowchart TD
    Start["Node Reports Issue / Alert"] --> Step1["1. Run: kubectl get nodes -o wide"]
    Step1 --> CheckStatus{"Is Node Status Ready?"}
    
    CheckStatus -- NotReady --> GoNotReady["Follow Diagram 34: Node NotReady Tree"]
    CheckStatus -- Ready --> Step2["2. Check Node Resource Pressure:<br/>kubectl describe node <name> | grep -A 5 Conditions"]
    
    Step2 --> PressureCheck{"Any Condition True?<br/>MemoryPressure, DiskPressure, PIDPressure"}
    PressureCheck -- Yes --> GoPressure["Follow Diagram 57-59: Resource Pressure Trees"]
    PressureCheck -- No --> Step3["3. Inspect System Logs on Node:<br/>journalctl -u kubelet -e --no-pager"]
    
    Step3 --> KubeletLog{"Any Fatal Errors in Kubelet?"}
    KubeletLog -- Yes --> GoKubelet["Follow Diagram 35: Kubelet Systemd Failure"]
    KubeletLog -- No --> Step4["4. Check Container Runtime:<br/>crictl info && systemctl status containerd"]
    Step4 --> RuntimeCheck{"Runtime Active & Responsive?"}
    RuntimeCheck -- No --> GoRuntime["Follow Diagram 36: containerd Failure"]
    RuntimeCheck -- Yes --> NodeHealthy["Node Infrastructure Verified Healthy"]

Diagnostic Explanation:
When troubleshooting an affected cluster, never jump blindly into pod manifests. Follow this 60-second triage protocol to isolate node-level infrastructure failures from workload application faults in under one minute.

34. Node Status: NotReady Diagnostic and Remediation Algorithm

flowchart TD
    NotReady["Node Reports NotReady in kubectl get nodes"] --> SSH["SSH into Affected Node"]
    SSH --> SvcCheck["systemctl status kubelet"]
    
    SvcCheck --> IsRunning{"Is kubelet running?"}
    IsRunning -- Inactive / Failed --> StartKubelet["systemctl restart kubelet<br/>journalctl -u kubelet -xe"]
    IsRunning -- Active (Running) --> CheckAPIReach{"Can Node reach kube-apiserver?<br/>curl -k https://<control-plane>:6443/version"}
    
    CheckAPIReach -- Connection Refused / Timeout --> NetIssue["Firewall / Network / Security Group Blocking Port 6443"]
    CheckAPIReach -- 200 OK or 401 Unauthorized --> CertCheck{"Are Kubelet Certificates Valid?<br/>openssl x509 -in /var/lib/kubelet/pki/kubelet-client-current.pem -noout -dates"}
    
    CertCheck -- Expired --> RenewCert["Regenerate Certs via kubeadm / kubeconfig bootstrap"]
    CertCheck -- Valid --> CgroupCheck{"Do Kubelet & containerd Cgroup Drivers Match?<br/>SystemdCgroup = true in config.toml"}
    
    CgroupCheck -- Mismatch --> FixCgroup["Align cgroup to systemd & restart daemons"]
    CgroupCheck -- Match --> DiskCheck{"Is Node Root Disk 100% Full?<br/>df -h /"}
    DiskCheck -- Full --> CleanDisk["Prune dead containers & logs: crictl rmi --prune"]
    DiskCheck -- Not Full --> NodeReady["Kubelet Posts NodeStatus -> Node Transitions to Ready"]

Diagnostic Explanation:
A NotReady condition occurs when the node lease fails to renew or heartbeat times out. Root causes fall into:

  1. Dead kubelet systemd service.
  2. Network partition to the API server on port 6443.
  3. Expired TLS client certificates in /var/lib/kubelet/pki/.
  4. Cgroup driver mismatch between kubelet and containerd.
  5. Disk space exhaustion preventing container creation.

35. kubelet Systemd Service Failure & Cgroup Driver Mismatch

flowchart TD
    FailedKubelet["systemctl status kubelet reports failed"] --> Journal["Run: journalctl -u kubelet -n 100 --no-pager"]
    Journal --> Analyze{"Analyze Error Message"}
    
    Analyze -- "cgroup driver mismatch" --> CheckConfig["Inspect /etc/containerd/config.toml<br/>Look for: SystemdCgroup = true"]
    CheckConfig --> EditConfig["Set SystemdCgroup = true<br/>systemctl restart containerd && systemctl restart kubelet"]
    
    Analyze -- "failed to load kubelet config file" --> CheckFile["Inspect /var/lib/kubelet/config.yaml<br/>Verify valid YAML syntax and paths"]
    
    Analyze -- "failed to run Kubelet: misconfiguration: kubelet cgroup" --> SwapCheck{"Is Linux Swap Active?<br/>Run: swapon --show"}
    SwapCheck -- Swap Active --> TurnOffSwap["Run: sudo swapoff -a<br/>Comment swap line in /etc/fstab"]
    
    Analyze -- "certificate signed by unknown authority" --> KubeconfigFix["Verify /etc/kubernetes/kubelet.conf points to correct CA"]

Diagnostic Explanation:
The kubelet will refuse to start if Linux swap memory is enabled (unless configured explicitly with failSwapOn: false) or if the cgroup driver does not match the container runtime (systemd is mandatory in modern Kubernetes).

36. Container Runtime (containerd) Socket and CRI Resolution

flowchart TD
    RuntimeDown["containerd service failing or crictl errors"] --> CheckStatus["systemctl status containerd"]
    CheckStatus --> SockCheck{"Does socket exist?<br/>ls -la /run/containerd/containerd.sock"}
    
    SockCheck -- No --> CheckCrashLog["Inspect: journalctl -u containerd -n 50 --no-pager"]
    CheckCrashLog --> BrokenConfig{"Invalid syntax in /etc/containerd/config.toml?"}
    BrokenConfig -- Yes --> DefaultConfig["Generate clean config:<br/>containerd config default > /etc/containerd/config.toml<br/>Set SystemdCgroup = true<br/>systemctl restart containerd"]
    
    SockCheck -- Yes --> CrictlTest{"Does crictl work?<br/>crictl pods"}
    CrictlTest -- Socket error --> SetEndpoint["Configure /etc/crictl.yaml:<br/>runtime-endpoint: unix:///run/containerd/containerd.sock"]
    CrictlTest -- Success --> Ready["Container runtime fully operational"]

Diagnostic Explanation:
crictl requires an explicit runtime endpoint configuration. If /etc/crictl.yaml is absent, run crictl config --set runtime-endpoint=unix:///run/containerd/containerd.sock. Corrupted config.toml files should be regenerated from containerd config default.

37. Control Plane Static Pod Outage & Log Recovery

flowchart TD
    ControlPlaneDown["Control Plane Not Responding<br/>kubectl fails: Connection Refused on 6443"] --> SSH["SSH to Control Plane Node"]
    SSH --> InspectStatic["Inspect /etc/kubernetes/manifests/<br/>kube-apiserver.yaml, etcd.yaml, kube-scheduler.yaml"]
    
    InspectStatic --> CheckCRI["Inspect Static Containers with crictl:<br/>crictl ps -a --name kube-apiserver"]
    CheckCRI --> ContainerFound{"Is API Server container listed?"}
    
    ContainerFound -- Exited / Crashing --> FetchLogs["Fetch logs directly from container runtime:<br/>crictl logs <container-id>"]
    ContainerFound -- Missing Entirely --> ManifestCheck["Verify manifest syntax & permissions in /etc/kubernetes/manifests/"]
    
    FetchLogs --> ErrorType{"Identify Root Error"}
    ErrorType -- "etcdserver: request timed out / connection refused" --> CheckEtcd["Verify etcd static container is healthy (Port 2379)"]
    ErrorType -- "address already in use" --> PortConflict["Kill orphaned process holding port 6443: fuser -k 6443/tcp"]
    ErrorType -- "certificate has expired" --> CertRenew["Renew certificates: kubeadm certs renew all"]

Diagnostic Explanation:
When the API server is down, kubectl cannot be used. Troubleshooting must be conducted on the control-plane host using crictl ps -a and inspecting raw static pod container logs directly under /var/log/pods/ or via crictl logs.

38. kube-apiserver Port 6443 Connection Refused Resolution

flowchart TD
    ConnRefused["kubectl: The connection to server 192.168.1.10:6443 was refused"] --> Step1["Check kubelet on Master: systemctl is-active kubelet"]
    Step1 -- Inactive --> StartK["systemctl start kubelet"]
    Step1 -- Active --> Step2["Check if apiserver container is running:<br/>crictl ps | grep kube-apiserver"]
    
    Step2 -- Running --> FWCheck["Check Linux Firewall / iptables:<br/>ufw status or iptables -L -n | grep 6443"]
    FWCheck -- Blocked --> AllowPort["Allow TCP Port 6443"]
    
    Step2 -- Not Running --> Step3["Inspect latest apiserver container log:<br/>crictl logs $(crictl ps -a --name kube-apiserver -q | head -n 1)"]
    Step3 --> AnalyzeErr{"Error Cause?"}
    AnalyzeErr -- ETCD unreachable --> FixETCD["Recover etcd container on port 2379"]
    AnalyzeErr -- Bad flag in manifest --> FixYAML["Fix invalid flag in /etc/kubernetes/manifests/kube-apiserver.yaml"]
    AnalyzeErr -- Expired PKI --> FixPKI["kubeadm certs renew all && systemctl restart kubelet"]

Diagnostic Explanation:
"Connection refused" on port 6443 means no process is listening on the port. It is almost always caused by the kube-apiserver static pod crashing on startup due to etcd connection failure or bad arguments in /etc/kubernetes/manifests/kube-apiserver.yaml.

39. etcd Cluster Health, Cert Expiration & Quorum Restoration

flowchart TD
    ETCDAlert["etcd Health Alert / Timeout"] --> EnvSetup["Export Auth Environment Variables:<br/>ETCDCTL_API=3<br/>CACERT=/etc/kubernetes/pki/etcd/ca.crt<br/>CERT=/etc/kubernetes/pki/etcd/server.crt<br/>KEY=/etc/kubernetes/pki/etcd/server.key"]
    
    EnvSetup --> HealthCheck["Run: etcdctl --cacert=$CACERT --cert=$CERT --key=$KEY<br/>--endpoints=https://127.0.0.1:2379 endpoint health"]
    
    HealthCheck --> IsHealthy{"Is endpoint healthy?"}
    IsHealthy -- Yes --> CheckQuorum["Run: etcdctl member list -w table<br/>Verify all members have active peer status"]
    
    IsHealthy -- No (tls: bad certificate) --> CheckCerts["Check cert dates:<br/>kubeadm certs check-expiration"]
    CheckCerts --> RenewAll["kubeadm certs renew etcd-server etcd-peer"]
    
    IsHealthy -- No (connection refused) --> CheckDisk["Check if etcd disk is full (df -h /var/lib/etcd)"]
    CheckDisk -- Out of space --> ExpandDisk["Free disk space or restore from uncorrupted snapshot"]

Diagnostic Explanation:
All etcdctl commands in Kubernetes require mTLS authentication using /etc/kubernetes/pki/etcd/server.crt and server.key. Always verify cluster health before attempting snapshot restores.

40. CoreDNS Resolution Failure & Loop Detection Diagnostic Tree

flowchart TD
    DNSFail["Pod cannot resolve service names (e.g. nslookup kubernetes.default)"] --> Step1["Check CoreDNS Pods:<br/>kubectl get pods -n kube-system -l k8s-app=kube-dns"]
    
    Step1 --> PodState{"CoreDNS Pods Running?"}
    PodState -- CrashLoopBackOff --> CheckLoop{"Are logs reporting 'Loop ... detected'?"}
    CheckLoop -- Yes --> FixLoop["Loop occurs when host /etc/resolv.conf points to 127.0.0.53 loopback.<br/>Update /etc/resolv.conf on host to upstream DNS (8.8.8.8) & restart CoreDNS"]
    
    PodState -- Pending --> CheckSched["Check node taints or missing CNI network plugin"]
    
    PodState -- Running --> Step2["Test DNS Service IP & Endpoints:<br/>kubectl get svc kube-dns -n kube-system<br/>kubectl get endpoints kube-dns -n kube-system"]
    
    Step2 --> EndpointsPresent{"Are endpoints populated?"}
    EndpointsPresent -- No --> CheckKubeProxy["kube-proxy issue or pod selector mismatch"]
    EndpointsPresent -- Yes --> Step3["Test Direct Query:<br/>kubectl run test-dns --rm -it --image=busybox:1.28 -- nslookup 10.96.0.1"]
    Step3 -- Success --> NetPolCheck["Inspect NetworkPolicies in application namespace blocking Port 53 UDP"]

Diagnostic Explanation:
CoreDNS CrashLoopBackOff with Loop detected is a classic Linux systemd-resolved bug where CoreDNS forwards to local loopback and detects infinite recursion. The fix is updating host nameservers and editing the Corefile in ConfigMap/coredns.

41. Pod Stuck in Pending: Scheduling Failure Flowchart

flowchart TD
    PendingPod["Pod Stuck in Pending"] --> Describe["Run: kubectl describe pod <pod-name>"]
    Describe --> ReadEvents["Examine 'Events' Section at Bottom"]
    
    ReadEvents --> Reason{"Evaluate Scheduling Failure Reason"}
    
    Reason -- "0/N nodes available: Insufficient cpu / memory" --> Action1["Lower pod resource requests or scale cluster / node capacity"]
    Reason -- "node(s) had untolerated taint" --> Action2["Add matching toleration to pod spec or remove taint from node"]
    Reason -- "node(s) didn't match Pod's node affinity/selector" --> Action3["Correct labels on node (kubectl label node ...) or adjust pod spec"]
    Reason -- "0/N nodes available: node(s) had volume node-affinity conflict" --> Action4["PVC is bound to PV in a different availability zone than scheduled node"]
    Reason -- "No events recorded" --> Action5["kube-scheduler is dead or control plane disconnected"]

Diagnostic Explanation:
If a Pod is Pending, the kube-scheduler has failed to bind it to a node. The Events table of kubectl describe pod lists the exact predicate that failed on every node.

42. Pod CrashLoopBackOff: Exit Code Analysis

flowchart TD
    Crash["Pod in CrashLoopBackOff"] --> GetCode["Run: kubectl describe pod <pod-name><br/>Inspect Last State: Exit Code"]
    
    GetCode --> ExitCode{"Analyze Exit Code"}
    
    ExitCode -- "Exit Code 0" --> Reason0["Container completed work and exited normally.<br/>Fix: Add long-running process or command (e.g. sleep infinity)"]
    ExitCode -- "Exit Code 1" --> Reason1["Application general error / syntax error.<br/>Check: kubectl logs <pod-name> --previous"]
    ExitCode -- "Exit Code 137" --> Reason137["OOMKilled (Out of Memory - 128 + 9 SIGKILL).<br/>Fix: Increase resources.limits.memory in pod spec"]
    ExitCode -- "Exit Code 139" --> Reason139["Segmentation Fault (SIGSEGV). Corrupted binary or library."]
    ExitCode -- "Exit Code 143" --> Reason143["Graceful Termination (128 + 15 SIGTERM). Stopped by orchestrator."]
    ExitCode -- "Exit Code 255" --> Reason255["Entrypoint or CMD command does not exist in container image."]

Diagnostic Explanation:
Exit codes convey the precise termination cause:

  • Exit Code 0: Task finished (Jobs use this; long-running services crash without a persistent foreground daemon).
  • Exit Code 137: Memory limit exceeded (OOMKilled) or external kill -9.
  • Always run kubectl logs <pod-name> --previous to see what printed immediately before the crash.

43. ImagePullBackOff & Private Registry Credential Failure

flowchart TD
    ImageErr["Pod in ImagePullBackOff / ErrImagePull"] --> Describe["Run: kubectl describe pod <pod-name>"]
    Describe --> CheckMsg{"Analyze Failed Event Message"}
    
    CheckMsg -- "manifest for <image>:<tag> not found" --> FixTag["Image tag does not exist. Correct tag in pod spec."]
    CheckMsg -- "repository does not exist or may require 'docker login'" --> FixAuth["Private Registry Authentication Required"]
    CheckMsg -- "dial tcp: lookup registry... i/o timeout" --> FixDNS["Worker node cannot resolve DNS or internet gateway down"]
    
    FixAuth --> CreateSecret["Create Docker Registry Secret:<br/>kubectl create secret docker-registry reg-cred<br/>--docker-server=... --docker-username=... --docker-password=..."]
    CreateSecret --> AttachSecret["Add imagePullSecrets to Pod Spec:<br/>spec.imagePullSecrets:<br/>  - name: reg-cred"]
    AttachSecret --> PodPulls["Kubelet Authenticates & Pulls Image"]

Diagnostic Explanation:
ImagePullBackOff indicates that the container image cannot be downloaded. Ensure the image tag is exact and that imagePullSecrets are defined in the pod manifest or bound to the ServiceAccount.

44. CreateContainerConfigError & Missing ConfigMap/Secret

flowchart TD
    ConfigErr["Pod in CreateContainerConfigError"] --> Describe["Run: kubectl describe pod <pod-name>"]
    Describe --> InspectMsg{"Analyze Event Message"}
    
    InspectMsg -- "configmap '<name>' not found" --> CreateCM["Create missing ConfigMap in the SAME namespace as pod"]
    InspectMsg -- "secret '<name>' not found" --> CreateSec["Create missing Secret in the SAME namespace as pod"]
    InspectMsg -- "key '<key>' not found" --> AddKey["Add missing key-value pair to ConfigMap or Secret"]
    
    CreateCM --> KubeletAutoStarts["Kubelet automatically detects created resource and starts Pod"]
    CreateSec --> KubeletAutoStarts
    AddKey --> KubeletAutoStarts

Diagnostic Explanation:
CreateContainerConfigError occurs before the container process is launched because a referenced configMapKeyRef or secretKeyRef does not exist in the pod's namespace. The pod transitions to Running automatically once the missing resource is created.

45. Service Connectivity Failure: ClusterIP Unreachable

flowchart TD
    SvcFail["Cannot reach Service ClusterIP (curl http://<cluster-ip>:<port>)"] --> Step1["1. Verify Endpoints:<br/>kubectl get endpoints <service-name>"]
    
    Step1 --> HasEndpoints{"Are Pod IPs listed under ENDPOINTS?"}
    HasEndpoints -- None / <none> --> CheckSelector["Compare Service spec.selector with Pod metadata.labels.<br/>Labels MUST match exactly!"]
    CheckSelector --> CheckProbes["Check if backing Pods are failing Readiness probes.<br/>Unready pods are stripped from endpoints!"]
    
    HasEndpoints -- Valid Pod IPs --> Step2["2. Test direct Pod IP connectivity:<br/>curl http://<pod-ip>:<targetPort>"]
    Step2 -- Direct Pod Fails --> CheckApp["App inside container not listening on expected port or bound only to 127.0.0.1"]
    
    Step2 -- Direct Pod Succeeds --> Step3["3. Inspect kube-proxy & iptables:<br/>kubectl get pods -n kube-system -l k8s-app=kube-proxy"]
    Step3 --> ProxyHealthy{"kube-proxy running on all nodes?"}
    ProxyHealthy -- No --> RestartProxy["Restart kube-proxy daemonset"]
    ProxyHealthy -- Yes --> NetPolCheck["Check for NetworkPolicies blocking ingress on target pod"]

Diagnostic Explanation:
A healthy Service requires:

  1. Exact label selector matching pod labels.
  2. Backing pods passing readiness probes.
  3. Target container listening on 0.0.0.0 (not 127.0.0.1).
  4. Active kube-proxy iptables/IPVS rules on every node.

46. NodePort Service Port Refused & Firewall Mismatch

flowchart TD
    NPFail["Cannot reach NodePort (curl http://<node-ip>:<nodePort>)"] --> Step1["Check Service Definition: kubectl get svc <name>"]
    Step1 --> VerifyType{"Is service type NodePort?"}
    VerifyType -- No --> FixType["Set spec.type: NodePort"]
    
    VerifyType -- Yes --> Step2["Identify allocated NodePort (Range 30000-32767)"]
    Step2 --> LocalTest["SSH into Node & test locally:<br/>curl http://127.0.0.1:<nodePort>"]
    
    LocalTest -- Works on Node --> ExternalFW["Local node port works -> External firewall / Cloud Security Group is blocking port"]
    LocalTest -- Connection Refused --> ProxyCheck["Check kube-proxy logs for iptables programming errors"]

Diagnostic Explanation:
If a NodePort works via curl http://127.0.0.1:<port> on the node itself but fails from outside, the host firewall (ufw, firewalld) or cloud security group is blocking the port range 30000–32767.

47. Ingress 404 Not Found & 502/503 Bad Gateway

flowchart TD
    IngressErr["HTTP Error via Ingress"] --> Code{"HTTP Status Code?"}
    
    Code -- 404 Not Found --> CheckHostPath["Verify Ingress Host & Path:<br/>1. Does request Host header match spec.rules[].host?<br/>2. Does path match pathType (Prefix vs Exact)?<br/>3. Verify default backend configuration"]
    
    Code -- 502 Bad Gateway --> CheckTarget["Target backend Service / Pod is refusing connection.<br/>Verify container is listening on targetPort specified in Service"]
    
    Code -- 503 Service Unavailable --> CheckEndP["Service has 0 healthy endpoints.<br/>Check pod readiness probes and replica counts"]

Diagnostic Explanation:

  • 404 Not Found: Ingress controller received the request, but no rule matched the Host or Path.
  • 502 Bad Gateway: Ingress routed traffic to the upstream pod, but the pod refused the TCP connection (port mismatch).
  • 503 Service Unavailable: No backing pods are in Ready state.

48. Gateway API HTTPRoute Detached & Unresolved ParentRef

flowchart TD
    RouteFail["HTTPRoute Not Serving Traffic"] --> InspectRoute["Run: kubectl describe httproute <name>"]
    InspectRoute --> CheckConditions["Check Status -> Conditions"]
    
    CheckConditions --> ParentRefCond{"Accepted: True & Programmed: True?"}
    
    ParentRefCond -- "Accepted: False / ResolvedRefs: False" --> RefIssue{"Why is ParentRef Rejected?"}
    RefIssue -- "Gateway not found" --> CheckGWName["Verify spec.parentRefs[].name matches an existing Gateway"]
    RefIssue -- "Cross-namespace reference not allowed" --> ReferenceGrant["Create ReferenceGrant in Gateway namespace to allow cross-namespace routes"]
    RefIssue -- "SectionName / Port mismatch" --> FixPort["Ensure HTTPRoute parentRef sectionName matches listener name in Gateway"]
    
    ParentRefCond -- "Accepted: True" --> BackendCheck["Verify backendRefs point to valid Services with endpoints"]

Diagnostic Explanation:
Gateway API routes decouple routing from gateways. An HTTPRoute must be accepted by the parent Gateway. If they reside in different namespaces, a ReferenceGrant resource is mandatory for security authorization.

49. NetworkPolicy Dropped Packet Triage

flowchart TD
    NetPolIssue["Pods Cannot Communicate / Timeouts Occur"] --> CheckCNI["1. Verify CNI Supports NetworkPolicies<br/>(Calico, Cilium, Weave support NetPol; Flannel DOES NOT without Canal)"]
    
    CheckCNI -- Flannel Only --> UpgradeCNI["Install Calico or Canal to enforce NetworkPolicies"]
    CheckCNI -- Supported --> Step2["2. Inspect Policies in Namespace:<br/>kubectl get netpol -n <namespace>"]
    
    Step2 --> EvaluateRules["Evaluate podSelector and policyTypes:<br/>Does any NetPol select the target Pod?"]
    EvaluateRules -- Selected --> CheckDefaultDeny["Selected pod is now in Default-Deny mode for that traffic direction"]
    
    CheckDefaultDeny --> VerifyWhitelist["Verify Ingress/Egress Whitelist Rules:<br/>1. podSelector matches client?<br/>2. namespaceSelector matches client namespace?<br/>3. ports[].port matches target port?"]
    VerifyWhitelist -- Mismatch --> FixNetPol["Update NetworkPolicy YAML to permit required traffic"]

Diagnostic Explanation:
NetworkPolicies are silent drop filters. If flannel is used alone, policies are silently ignored without error. When debugging, test reachability with kubectl exec -it <pod> -- nc -zvw3 <ip> <port>.

50. PersistentVolume & Claim Stuck in Pending / Binding Failure

flowchart TD
    PVCFail["PVC Stuck in Pending"] --> DescribePVC["Run: kubectl describe pvc <pvc-name>"]
    DescribePVC --> CheckEvents["Read Events Section"]
    
    CheckEvents --> EventMsg{"Analyze Event Message"}
    
    EventMsg -- "waiting for first consumer to be created" --> NormalBehavior["StorageClass uses volumeBindingMode: WaitForFirstConsumer.<br/>PVC will remain Pending until a Pod using it is scheduled!"]
    
    EventMsg -- "no persistent volumes available" --> StaticIssue["Static Provisioning: No existing PV matches size, accessModes, or storageClassName"]
    StaticIssue --> FixPV["Create PV with capacity >= PVC request and identical accessModes"]
    
    EventMsg -- "provisioning failed" --> DynamicIssue["Dynamic Provisioning: CSI driver error or invalid StorageClass parameters"]

Diagnostic Explanation:
volumeBindingMode: WaitForFirstConsumer is standard practice. It delays volume creation until the Pod is placed on a node, ensuring the storage volume is provisioned in the same availability zone as the node.

51. Volume Mount Failure: Multi-Attach Error & Node Lock

flowchart TD
    AttachFail["Pod in ContainerCreating: Volume Attach Failed"] --> Describe["Run: kubectl describe pod <pod-name>"]
    Describe --> ReadErr{"Multi-Attach error for volume ... Volume is already exclusively attached to one node"}
    
    ReadErr --> Cause["Previous node did not release disk lock during sudden crash / cordon"]
    Cause --> Step1["1. Identify old node holding attachment:<br/>kubectl get volumeattachments"]
    Step1 --> Step2["2. Delete orphaned VolumeAttachment object:<br/>kubectl delete volumeattachment <attachment-id>"]
    Step2 --> Step3["3. Alternatively, force delete terminated pod on old node:<br/>kubectl delete pod <old-pod> --force --grace-period=0"]
    Step3 --> VolumeMounts["Kubelet attaches volume to new node & starts Pod"]

Diagnostic Explanation:
Cloud block storage (EBS, GPD, Cinder) can only attach to one VM at a time (ReadWriteOnce). When a node crashes, the csi-attacher waits for safe detachment before allowing a new host to mount the volume.

52. Dynamic Storage Provisioner Failure & StorageClass Tree

flowchart TD
    DynFail["Dynamic PVC Provisioning Fails"] --> SC["Inspect StorageClass: kubectl get sc <name> -o yaml"]
    SC --> CheckDriver["Check provisioner field (e.g. ebs.csi.aws.com, standard)"]
    
    CheckDriver --> CheckDriverPods["Verify CSI Controller Pods are Running:<br/>kubectl get pods -A | grep csi"]
    CheckDriverPods -- CSI Pods Down --> FixCSI["Restart CSI controller deployment"]
    CheckDriverPods -- Running --> CheckLogs["Inspect CSI provisioner logs:<br/>kubectl logs -n kube-system <csi-provisioner-pod>"]
    CheckLogs --> IAMAuth["Cloud IAM permission denied / invalid disk type parameter in StorageClass"]

Diagnostic Explanation:
Dynamic volume provisioning depends on the in-cluster CSI storage controller. If PVC provisioning fails without events, inspect the csi-provisioner container logs in kube-system.

53. RBAC Unauthorized (403 Forbidden) Audit

flowchart TD
    RBACFail["API Error: 403 Forbidden: User 'X' cannot 'verb' 'resource' in namespace 'Y'"] --> Step1["Test permissions with auth can-i:<br/>kubectl auth can-i <verb> <resource> --as=<user> -n <namespace>"]
    
    Step1 --> CheckRole["Inspect Role / ClusterRole:<br/>Does any rule grant verb on resource?"]
    CheckRole -- Missing Verb/Resource --> UpdateRole["Add required apiGroups, resources, and verbs to Role"]
    
    CheckRole -- Rule Exists --> CheckBinding["Inspect RoleBinding / ClusterRoleBinding"]
    CheckBinding --> CheckSubject{"Does subjects[] match User/Group/ServiceAccount exactly?"}
    CheckBinding -- Namespace Mismatch --> FixBinding["Ensure ServiceAccount namespace in subjects matches its true namespace"]
    CheckBinding -- Typo in Name --> FixSubject["Correct user/group/serviceaccount name"]

Diagnostic Explanation:
Always use kubectl auth can-i <verb> <resource> --as=<user> -n <ns> to verify permissions without switching contexts. Common errors include forgetting apiGroups (e.g., apps for deployments) or referencing a ServiceAccount in the wrong namespace in subjects.

54. Certificate Expiration & kubeadm certs Renewal

flowchart TD
    CertAlert["Cluster Down / Cert Expiration Warning"] --> CheckDates["Run: kubeadm certs check-expiration"]
    CheckDates --> ScanTable["Inspect Expiration Date for all Control Plane Certificates"]
    
    ScanTable --> Expired{"Any Certificate Expired?"}
    Expired -- Yes --> RenewCommand["Run: kubeadm certs renew all"]
    RenewCommand --> RestartPods["Restart Control Plane Static Pods:<br/>Move manifests temporarily out of /etc/kubernetes/manifests/ and back<br/>or: kill -s SIGHUP $(pgrep kube-apiserver)"]
    RestartPods --> UpdateAdminConf["Update local admin.conf:<br/>cp /etc/kubernetes/admin.conf ~/.kube/config"]
    UpdateAdminConf --> Verify["Verify kubectl get nodes succeeds"]

Diagnostic Explanation:
Kubernetes control plane certificates created by kubeadm expire after 1 year. The renewal process takes less than 2 minutes using kubeadm certs renew all, followed by reloading the static pods.

55. Cluster Upgrade Failure & Node Version Skew Recovery

flowchart TD
    UpgradeFail["kubeadm upgrade plan or apply fails"] --> CheckError{"Analyze Upgrade Error"}
    
    CheckError -- "version skew violation" --> SkewRule["Kubernetes requires upgrading minor versions sequentially (e.g. 1.30 -> 1.31 -> 1.32). NEVER skip minor versions!"]
    
    CheckError -- "control plane not ready" --> HealthCheck["Ensure all nodes are Ready and etcd is healthy before starting upgrade"]
    
    CheckError -- "apt-get / package hold error" --> Unhold["Unhold packages: sudo apt-mark unhold kubeadm kubelet kubectl<br/>Install target version: sudo apt-get install -y kubeadm=1.31.2-1.1<br/>Hold packages: sudo apt-mark hold kubeadm"]
    
    Unhold --> Reapply["Run: sudo kubeadm upgrade apply v1.31.2"]

Diagnostic Explanation:
Upgrades must follow strict sequence: Control Plane first (kubeadm upgrade apply, then upgrade kubelet), followed by worker nodes one-by-one (kubeadm upgrade node, drain, upgrade kubelet, uncordon).

56. Worker Node Join Failure: Token & Discovery Timeout

flowchart TD
    JoinFail["kubeadm join times out or throws token error"] --> CheckMsg{"Analyze Join Error"}
    
    CheckMsg -- "token has expired" --> CreateToken["Generate new join token on Control Plane:<br/>kubeadm token create --print-join-command"]
    
    CheckMsg -- "discovery token CA hash mismatch" --> GetHash["Compute correct CA hash on Control Plane:<br/>openssl x509 -pubkey -in /etc/kubernetes/pki/ca.crt |<br/>openssl rsa -pubin -outform der 2>/dev/null |<br/>openssl dgst -sha256 -hex | sed 's/^.* //'"]
    
    CheckMsg -- "connection refused / timeout to 6443" --> TestNet["Test network connectivity from worker to control plane:<br/>nc -zvw3 <control-plane-ip> 6443"]
    
    CreateToken --> RunJoin["Execute corrected kubeadm join command on worker"]

Diagnostic Explanation:
Bootstrap tokens expire after 24 hours. If joining a worker node after token expiration, generate a fresh join command using kubeadm token create --print-join-command.

57. High CPU & Memory Throttling: CFS Quota Investigation

flowchart TD
    Throttle["Application Latency Spikes / High CPU Alarm"] --> Step1["Check Pod Resource Metrics:<br/>kubectl top pods -n <namespace>"]
    Step1 --> Step2["Check Node Resource Metrics:<br/>kubectl top nodes"]
    
    Step2 --> CheckCgroup["SSH to Worker Node & Inspect CFS Throttling:<br/>cat /sys/fs/cgroup/cpu/kubepods/.../cpu.stat"]
    CheckCgroup --> MetricEval{"nr_throttled > 0 and throttled_time is high?"}
    
    MetricEval -- Yes --> FixLimit["Pod is hitting CPU Limits ceiling.<br/>Increase resources.limits.cpu or remove CPU limit (best practice for latency-sensitive apps)"]
    MetricEval -- No --> AppProfile["Application thread contention or external bottleneck"]

Diagnostic Explanation:
CPU throttling occurs when a container attempts to burst beyond its CFS quota. Removing or raising CPU limits allows workloads to utilize idle CPU cycles without degrading latency.

58. DiskPressure & Node Eviction: Ephemeral Storage Cleanup

flowchart TD
    DiskPress["Node Condition: DiskPressure = True<br/>Pods Evicted with The node was low on resource: ephemeral-storage"] --> SSH["SSH to Affected Node"]
    SSH --> CheckDisk["Check Disk Usage: df -h /"]
    
    CheckDisk --> Step1["1. Prune unused images and exited containers:<br/>crictl rmi --prune"]
    Step1 --> Step2["2. Clean up large container logs:<br/>truncate -s 0 /var/log/pods/*/*/*.log"]
    Step2 --> Step3["3. Inspect /var/lib/kubelet for orphaned pod directories"]
    
    Step3 --> Recover["Disk utilization drops below threshold (85%) -> Node DiskPressure clears to False"]

Diagnostic Explanation:
When node disk utilization exceeds 85%, kubelet initiates hard eviction of Pods consuming excessive ephemeral storage. Clearing old container images with crictl rmi --prune immediately restores disk headroom.

59. PID Pressure & Kernel Process Exhaustion

flowchart TD
    PIDPress["Node Condition: PIDPressure = True"] --> CheckPIDs["SSH to Node & Check Process Count:<br/>ps -eLf | wc -l<br/>sysctl kernel.pid_max"]
    
    CheckPIDs --> FindCulprit["Identify container leaking threads/processes:<br/>ps aux --sort -nlwp | head -n 20"]
    FindCulprit --> KillProcess["Drain node & restart runaway container"]
    
    KillProcess --> EnforceLimit["Add Pod PID Limits in kubelet configuration:<br/>podPidsLimit: 4096 in /var/lib/kubelet/config.yaml"]

Diagnostic Explanation:
A fork-bomb or process leak inside a container can exhaust the Linux kernel PID table, causing the entire host to freeze. Modern kubelets enforce podPidsLimit to isolate process leaks to the offending container.

60. StatefulSet Out-of-Order Pod Startup & PVC Orphan Cleanup

flowchart TD
    StatefulFail["StatefulSet pod db-1 will not start"] --> Step1["Check previous ordinal db-0:<br/>kubectl get pod db-0"]
    Step1 --> IsReady{"Is db-0 in Running & Ready state?"}
    
    IsReady -- No --> FixDB0["StatefulSets require sequential startup.<br/>Fix db-0 before db-1 can be scheduled!"]
    
    IsReady -- Yes --> CheckPVC["Check PVC: kubectl get pvc data-db-1"]
    CheckPVC --> PVCMissing{"Does PVC exist?"}
    PVCMissing -- No --> RecreatePVC["Ensure volumeClaimTemplates is correctly defined in StatefulSet spec"]

Diagnostic Explanation:
By default (podManagementPolicy: OrderedReady), StatefulSets require pod $N-1$ to pass all readiness checks before pod $N$ is created. If pod 0 is crashing, pod 1 will never start.

61. Helm Release Stuck in Pending-Install / Pending-Upgrade

flowchart TD
    HelmStuck["Helm command fails: release X has another operation in progress"] --> ListReleases["Run: helm list -a"]
    ListReleases --> CheckStatus{"Status: pending-install or pending-upgrade?"}
    
    CheckStatus -- Yes --> Step1["Identify Helm Secret storing release state:<br/>kubectl get secret -l owner=helm,name=<release-name>"]
    Step1 --> Step2["Delete the pending secret version:<br/>kubectl delete secret sh.helm.release.v1.<name>.v<latest-rev>"]
    Step2 --> Step3["Re-run helm rollback or helm upgrade"]

Diagnostic Explanation:
Helm 3 stores release state in Kubernetes Secrets within the release namespace. When a deployment is interrupted, the release state gets stuck in pending-upgrade. Deleting the hung secret releases the state lock.

62. Custom Resource Definition (CRD) Validation & Operator Errors

flowchart TD
    CRDFail["Error creating Custom Resource: validation failure"] --> Step1["Inspect CRD Schema: kubectl describe crd <crd-name>"]
    Step1 --> CheckVersion["Verify apiVersion and kind match CRD spec.group and spec.names"]
    
    CheckVersion --> Step2["Inspect Operator Logs:<br/>kubectl logs -n <operator-ns> -l app=<operator-name>"]
    Step2 --> OperatorCrash{"Is operator reconciler throwing panic / errors?"}
    OperatorCrash -- Yes --> RBACCheck["Verify Operator ServiceAccount has RBAC rights to manage the Custom Resource"]

Diagnostic Explanation:
Custom Resources require valid OpenAPI v3 schemas in the CRD definition. If a valid CR is created but never reconciled, inspect the operator pod logs for RBAC authorization failures.

63. DaemonSet Pod Missing on Specific Worker Node

flowchart TD
    DSMissing["DaemonSet pod not running on worker-02"] --> DescribeNode["Inspect Node: kubectl describe node worker-02"]
    DescribeNode --> CheckTaints["Check Taints on worker-02"]
    
    CheckTaints --> HasCustomTaint{"Does node have custom taints?"}
    HasCustomTaint -- Yes --> AddToleration["Add matching toleration to DaemonSet spec.template.spec.tolerations"]
    
    HasCustomTaint -- No --> CheckSelector["Check nodeSelector & nodeAffinity in DaemonSet spec"]
    CheckSelector --> LabelMatch{"Does worker-02 have required labels?"}
    LabelMatch -- No --> LabelNode["Label node: kubectl label node worker-02 <key>=<value>"]
    LabelMatch -- Yes --> CheckResources["Check if worker-02 is out of CPU or memory"]

Diagnostic Explanation:
DaemonSet pods automatically tolerate standard node conditions, but custom operational taints (e.g., gpu=true:NoSchedule) or specific nodeSelectors will prevent DaemonSet scheduling unless declared.

64. Master Exam Day Time-Management & Emergency Task Rescue

flowchart TD
    ExamTask["New CKA Exam Task Presented"] --> Step1["1. IMMEDIATELY SWITCH CONTEXT:<br/>kubectl config use-context <context-name>"]
    Step1 --> Step2["2. Set Namespace Variable:<br/>NS=<task-namespace> (or append -n $NS to all commands)"]
    
    Step2 --> AssessTime{"Can this task be completed in under 6 minutes?"}
    AssessTime -- No / Complex & Confusing --> FlagTask["FLAG TASK FOR REVIEW & MOVE TO NEXT TASK IMMEDIATELY"]
    
    AssessTime -- Yes --> ImperativeFirst["Use Imperative Generators:<br/>kubectl create / run ... --dry-run=client -o yaml > task.yaml"]
    ImperativeFirst --> ApplyTask["vim task.yaml && kubectl apply -f task.yaml"]
    ApplyTask --> FastVerify["Run Quick Verification:<br/>kubectl get pod/svc -n $NS && curl test"]
    FastVerify --> PassNext["Task Complete -> Proceed to Next Task"]

Diagnostic Explanation:
The golden rule of the CKA exam: Never spend more than 8 minutes on a single task on the first pass. If a task is unclear, flag it, skip it, and capture all easy points across the remaining tasks before returning.

VOLUME IV: THE CKA COMMAND AND TROUBLESHOOTING FIELD GUIDE

PART 1: KUBECTL ADMINISTRATION, JSONPATH & WORKLOAD COMMANDS

1. ESSENTIAL SHELL ALIASES AND SPEED BOILERPLATE

To pass the CKA exam within the 120-minute limit, configure these shell aliases immediately upon opening the remote desktop terminal:

# 1. Instant Command Aliases
alias k=kubectl
alias kgp="kubectl get pods -o wide"
alias kgs="kubectl get svc -o wide"
alias kgn="kubectl get nodes -o wide"
alias kd="kubectl describe"

# 2. Bash Autocompletion for 'k'
source <(kubectl completion bash)
complete -o default -F __start_kubectl k

# 3. Imperative Generation Fast Variables
export do="--dry-run=client -o yaml"
export now="--force --grace-period=0"

# 4. Vim Configuration for Flawless YAML Editing
cat <<EOF > ~/.vimrc
set tabstop=2
set shiftwidth=2
set expandtab
set autoindent
set number
EOF

2. KUBECTL WORKLOAD COMMAND REFERENCE

Command: Imperative Pod Generation

  • Syntax: kubectl run <pod-name> --image=<image> [--port=<port>] [--env="KEY=VAL"] [--labels="k=v"] [--dry-run=client -o yaml]
  • Purpose: Generates a standalone Pod manifest without authoring YAML manually.
  • Example:
    kubectl run redis-cache --image=redis:7.2-alpine --port=6379 --env="ALLOW_EMPTY_PASSWORD=yes" $do > redis.yaml
  • Flag Explanation:
    • --image: Container image to pull from registry.
    • --port: Container port declaration (containerPort: 6379).
    • --env: Key-value environment variables injected into the container.
    • --dry-run=client -o yaml: Evaluates client-side syntax and prints raw YAML to stdout without sending requests to the API server.
  • Expected Output:
    apiVersion: v1
kind: Pod
metadata:
  labels:
    run: redis-cache
  name: redis-cache
spec:
  containers:
  - env:
    - name: ALLOW_EMPTY_PASSWORD
      value: "yes"
    image: redis:7.2-alpine
    name: redis-cache
    ports:
    - containerPort: 6379
  • Common Errors: Forgetting $do (--dry-run=client -o yaml) and accidentally deploying the pod to the live cluster immediately.
  • Safety Warning: Pods created directly via kubectl run are unmanaged; they lack self-healing or auto-recreation if the worker node crashes.

Command: Imperative Deployment Creation & Scaling

  • Syntax: kubectl create deployment <name> --image=<image> [--replicas=<count>] [--port=<port>]
  • Purpose: Creates a self-healing Deployment managing a ReplicaSet.
  • Example:
    kubectl create deployment web-frontend --image=nginx:1.27-alpine --replicas=3 --port=80 $do > deploy.yaml
kubectl apply -f deploy.yaml
  • Scaling Example:
    kubectl scale deployment web-frontend --replicas=5
  • Expected Output: deployment.apps/web-frontend created deployment.apps/web-frontend scaled
  • Common Errors: Attempting to specify pod resource requests/limits directly on the CLI; resource constraints must be edited inside the generated YAML.

Command: Rolling Update & Revision Recording (Modern Replacement for Removed --record)

  • Syntax:
    kubectl set image deployment/<name> <container-name>=<new-image>
kubectl annotate deployment/<name> kubernetes.io/change-cause="<description>" --overwrite
  • Purpose: Performs a zero-downtime rolling update and records the reason in the rollout revision history.
  • Example:
    # Step 1: Update container image in-place
kubectl set image deployment/web-frontend nginx=nginx:1.27.2-alpine

# Step 2: Record change cause using official Kubernetes annotation
kubectl annotate deployment/web-frontend kubernetes.io/change-cause="Upgraded nginx to 1.27.2-alpine" --overwrite

# Step 3: Monitor rollout progress
kubectl rollout status deployment/web-frontend

# Step 4: Inspect rollout history
kubectl rollout history deployment/web-frontend
  • CRITICAL NOTE ON REMOVED --record FLAG:

    [!IMPORTANT] The --record flag was deprecated in Kubernetes v1.19 and permanently removed in modern kubectl releases (v1.26+). Running kubectl set image ... --record produces warnings/errors and will not populate the change-cause field. The official, supported standard is annotating the Deployment with kubernetes.io/change-cause="...".

  • Expected Output:
    deployment.apps/web-frontend image updated
deployment.apps/web-frontend annotated
Waiting for deployment "web-frontend" rollout to finish: 1 out of 5 new replicas have been updated...
deployment "web-frontend" successfully rolled out
REVISION  CHANGE-CAUSE
1         Initial deployment
2         Upgraded nginx to 1.27.2-alpine

Command: Instant Deployment Rollback

  • Syntax: kubectl rollout undo deployment/<name> [--to-revision=<rev>]
  • Purpose: Immediately reverts a failing rollout to the previous revision or a specific revision number.
  • Example:
    # Rollback to immediate previous revision
kubectl rollout undo deployment/web-frontend

# Rollback to specific stable revision
kubectl rollout undo deployment/web-frontend --to-revision=1
  • Expected Output: deployment.apps/web-frontend rolled back

Command: Job and CronJob Generation

  • Syntax:
    • Job: kubectl create job <name> --image=<image> [--dry-run=client -o yaml] -- <command>
    • CronJob: kubectl create cronjob <name> --image=<image> --schedule="<cron>" [--dry-run=client -o yaml] -- <command>
  • Example:
    # Batch Job
kubectl create job db-backup --image=busybox:1.36 $do -- sh -c "echo 'Backup completed' && exit 0" > job.yaml

# Scheduled CronJob (Runs every 15 minutes)
kubectl create cronjob sync-task --image=curlimages/curl:8.10.1 --schedule="*/15 * * * *" $do -- curl -s https://api.internal/sync > cronjob.yaml
  • Common Errors: CronJobs require the 5-field standard cron syntax (minute hour day-of-month month day-of-week). Forgetting quotes around --schedule="* * * * *" causes bash expansion syntax errors.

Command: ConfigMap and Secret Generation

  • Syntax:
    • ConfigMap from literal: kubectl create configmap <name> --from-literal=<key>=<val>
    • ConfigMap from file: kubectl create configmap <name> --from-file=<path>
    • Generic Secret: kubectl create secret generic <name> --from-literal=<key>=<val>
    • TLS Secret: kubectl create secret tls <name> --cert=<cert.crt> --key=<key.key>
    • Docker Registry Secret: kubectl create secret docker-registry <name> --docker-server=<server> --docker-username=<u> --docker-password=<p>
  • Examples:
    kubectl create configmap app-settings --from-literal=ENVIRONMENT=production --from-literal=DB_PORT=5432
kubectl create secret generic app-credentials --from-literal=DB_PASS="s3cur3P@ssw0rd!"
kubectl create secret tls ingress-tls --cert=/etc/ssl/tls.crt --key=/etc/ssl/tls.key
  • Safety Warning: Values stored in standard Kubernetes Secrets are merely Base64-encoded, not encrypted at rest by default unless KMS / encryption-provider is enabled on kube-apiserver.

3. NETWORKING, SERVICES & GATEWAY API COMMANDS

Command: Exposing Workloads via Service

  • Syntax: kubectl expose (deployment|pod) <name> --port=<svc-port> [--target-port=<pod-port>] [--type=(ClusterIP|NodePort|LoadBalancer)]
  • Purpose: Automatically generates a Service that inherits the target deployment's exact label selector.
  • Example:
    # Expose as internal ClusterIP
kubectl expose deployment web-frontend --port=80 --target-port=8080 --name=web-svc

# Expose as external NodePort
kubectl expose deployment web-frontend --type=NodePort --port=80 --target-port=8080 --name=web-np
  • Verification:
    kubectl get svc web-svc -o wide
kubectl get endpointslices -l kubernetes.io/service-name=web-svc

Command: Inspecting Gateway API Resources

  • Syntax:
    kubectl get gatewayclasses
kubectl get gateways -A
kubectl get httproutes -A
kubectl describe httproute <route-name>
  • Purpose: Queries modern Gateway API infrastructure and identifies routing acceptance status.
  • Example:
    kubectl describe httproute payments-route
  • Expected Output:
    Status:
  Parents:
    Conditions:
      Last Transition Time:  2026-11-09T18:00:00Z
      Message:               Route was accepted
      Reason:                Accepted
      Status:                True
      Type:                  Accepted
      Status:                True
      Type:                  ResolvedRefs
    Controller Name:         gateway.networking.k8s.io/gateway-controller
    Parent Ref:
      Group:  gateway.networking.k8s.io
      Kind:   Gateway
      Name:   prod-gateway

4. ADVANCED JSONPATH, SORTING & CUSTOM COLUMNS

Filter: List Pods Sorted by Creation Timestamp

kubectl get pods -A --sort-by=.metadata.creationTimestamp

Purpose: Immediately displays the most recently created or restarted pods across all namespaces.

Filter: Extract Node Internal IP Addresses

kubectl get nodes -o jsonpath='{range .items[*]}{.metadata.name}{"\t"}{range .status.addresses[?(@.type=="InternalIP")]}{.address}{"\n"}{end}{end}'

Purpose: Cleanly outputs node hostnames paired with their physical internal IP addresses for cluster-level SSH operations.

Filter: Extract All Pod Names Running on a Specific Node

kubectl get pods -A --field-selector spec.nodeName=worker-01 -o jsonpath='{.items[*].metadata.name}'

Purpose: Finds every pod placed on an affected worker node prior to draining or troubleshooting.

Filter: List Container Images across All Deployments

kubectl get deployments -A -o custom-columns=NAMESPACE:.metadata.namespace,DEPLOYMENT:.metadata.name,IMAGE:.spec.template.spec.containers[*].image

Purpose: Verifies container image tags across all production deployments in a formatted table.

Filter: Extract Non-Ready Nodes

kubectl get nodes -o jsonpath='{range .items[*]}{.metadata.name}{"\t"}{range .status.conditions[?(@.type=="Ready")]}{.status}{"\n"}{end}{end}' | grep -v "True"

Purpose: Instant programmatic filter isolating nodes in NotReady or Unknown condition.

5. RBAC & PERMISSION AUDITING COMMANDS

Command: Permission Checking (auth can-i)

  • Syntax: kubectl auth can-i <verb> <resource> [--as=<user>] [--as-group=<group>] [-n <namespace>]
  • Purpose: Verifies RBAC authorization without requiring context switching or certificates.
  • Examples:
    # Can current user delete pods in production?
kubectl auth can-i delete pods -n production

# Can ServiceAccount 'deployer' in namespace 'cicd' create deployments in 'default'?
kubectl auth can-i create deployments --as=system:serviceaccount:cicd:deployer -n default

# Can developer user 'jane' view secrets in any namespace?
kubectl auth can-i get secrets -A --as=jane
  • Expected Output: yes or no

Command: Imperative Role & RoleBinding Creation

# 1. Create a Role with specific verbs and resources
kubectl create role pod-admin --verb=get,list,watch,create,delete --resource=pods,pods/log -n staging

# 2. Bind the Role to a User
kubectl create rolebinding pod-admin-binding --role=pod-admin --user=jane -n staging

# 3. Create a ClusterRole across all namespaces
kubectl create clusterrole node-reader --verb=get,list,watch --resource=nodes

# 4. Bind ClusterRole to a ServiceAccount
kubectl create clusterrolebinding node-reader-binding --clusterrole=node-reader --serviceaccount=kube-system:monitor-sa

VOLUME IV: THE CKA COMMAND AND TROUBLESHOOTING FIELD GUIDE

PART 2: CLUSTER ADMINISTRATION, ETCD, CRICTL & LINUX DIAGNOSTICS

1. KUBEADM CLUSTER LIFECYCLE & CERTIFICATE MANAGEMENT

Command: Certificate Expiration Inspection

  • Syntax: sudo kubeadm certs check-expiration
  • Purpose: Inspects cryptographic TLS certificates in /etc/kubernetes/pki/ and reports remaining lifespan.
  • Example:
    sudo kubeadm certs check-expiration
  • Expected Output:
    CERTIFICATE                EXPIRES                  RESIDUAL TIME   CERTIFICATE AUTHORITY
admin.conf                 Nov 09, 2027 12:00 UTC   364d            ca
apiserver                  Nov 09, 2027 12:00 UTC   364d            ca
apiserver-etcd-client      Nov 09, 2027 12:00 UTC   364d            etcd-ca
apiserver-kubelet-client   Nov 09, 2027 12:00 UTC   364d            ca
controller-manager.conf    Nov 09, 2027 12:00 UTC   364d            ca
etcd-healthcheck-client    Nov 09, 2027 12:00 UTC   364d            etcd-ca
etcd-peer                  Nov 09, 2027 12:00 UTC   364d            etcd-ca
etcd-server                Nov 09, 2027 12:00 UTC   364d            etcd-ca
front-proxy-client         Nov 09, 2027 12:00 UTC   364d            front-proxy-ca
scheduler.conf             Nov 09, 2027 12:00 UTC   364d            ca
  • Common Mistakes: Running without sudo, causing permission denied errors when accessing /etc/kubernetes/pki/*.key.

Command: Certificate Renewal Procedure

  • Syntax: sudo kubeadm certs renew (all|<cert-name>)
  • Purpose: Generates fresh 1-year cryptographic TLS certificates signed by the local CA.
  • Runbook:
    # Step 1: Renew all certificates
sudo kubeadm certs renew all

# Step 2: Restart control plane static pods by moving manifests temporarily
sudo mv /etc/kubernetes/manifests/*.yaml /tmp/
sleep 5
sudo mv /tmp/*.yaml /etc/kubernetes/manifests/

# Step 3: Update local user kubeconfig with renewed admin credentials
mkdir -p $HOME/.kube
sudo cp -i /etc/kubernetes/admin.conf $HOME/.kube/config
sudo chown $(id -u):$(id -g) $HOME/.kube/config

# Step 4: Verify API access
kubectl get nodes
  • Safety Warning: Renewing certificates updates /etc/kubernetes/admin.conf. If you forget Step 3, your local kubectl commands will immediately fail with 401 Unauthorized / certificate has expired!

Command: Cluster Version Upgrade (Control Plane)

  • Syntax:
    • sudo kubeadm upgrade plan
    • sudo kubeadm upgrade apply <version>
  • Runbook:
    # 1. Unhold kubeadm and install target version
sudo apt-mark unhold kubeadm
sudo apt-get update && sudo apt-get install -y kubeadm=1.31.2-1.1
sudo apt-mark hold kubeadm

# 2. Plan the upgrade to verify compatibility
sudo kubeadm upgrade plan

# 3. Apply the upgrade to the control plane
sudo kubeadm upgrade apply v1.31.2 -y

# 4. Drain control plane node
kubectl drain <cp-node> --ignore-daemonsets

# 5. Upgrade kubelet and kubectl
sudo apt-mark unhold kubelet kubectl
sudo apt-get install -y kubelet=1.31.2-1.1 kubectl=1.31.2-1.1
sudo apt-mark hold kubelet kubectl

# 6. Restart kubelet daemon
sudo systemctl daemon-reload
sudo systemctl restart kubelet

# 7. Uncordon control plane node
kubectl uncordon <cp-node>

Command: Worker Node Upgrade Runbook

# 1. Drain worker node from control plane / bastion
kubectl drain <worker-node> --ignore-daemonsets --delete-emptydir-data

# 2. SSH into worker node
ssh <worker-node>

# 3. Upgrade kubeadm binary
sudo apt-mark unhold kubeadm
sudo apt-get update && sudo apt-get install -y kubeadm=1.31.2-1.1
sudo apt-mark hold kubeadm

# 4. Upgrade local worker configuration
sudo kubeadm upgrade node

# 5. Upgrade kubelet and kubectl
sudo apt-mark unhold kubelet kubectl
sudo apt-get install -y kubelet=1.31.2-1.1 kubectl=1.31.2-1.1
sudo apt-mark hold kubelet kubectl

# 6. Restart kubelet
sudo systemctl daemon-reload
sudo systemctl restart kubelet
exit

# 7. Uncordon worker node from control plane
kubectl uncordon <worker-node>

Command: Generate Node Join Token

  • Syntax: kubeadm token create --print-join-command
  • Purpose: Generates a new bootstrap token and prints the complete kubeadm join command with CA hash for joining new worker nodes.
  • Example:
    kubeadm token create --print-join-command
  • Expected Output:
    kubeadm join 192.168.1.10:6443 --token abcdef.0123456789abcdef \
    --discovery-token-ca-cert-hash sha256:7c8b4e2f9d1a3b5c7e9f...

2. ETCD DISASTER RECOVERY & ETCDCTL RUNBOOK

Command: etcd Snapshot Backup

  • Syntax:
    ETCDCTL_API=3 etcdctl --endpoints=<endpoint> \
  --cacert=<ca-path> --cert=<cert-path> --key=<key-path> \
  snapshot save <output-file.db>
  • Standard Control Plane Backup Command:
    ETCDCTL_API=3 etcdctl --endpoints=https://127.0.0.1:2379 \
  --cacert=/etc/kubernetes/pki/etcd/ca.crt \
  --cert=/etc/kubernetes/pki/etcd/server.crt \
  --key=/etc/kubernetes/pki/etcd/server.key \
  snapshot save /opt/etcd-backup.db
  • Verify Snapshot Integrity:
    ETCDCTL_API=3 etcdctl --write-out=table snapshot status /opt/etcd-backup.db
  • Expected Output:
    +----------+----------+------------+------------+
|   HASH   | REVISION | TOTAL KEYS | TOTAL SIZE |
+----------+----------+------------+------------+
| fe84a10c |    15820 |       1450 |     3.2 MB |
+----------+----------+------------+------------+

Command: etcd Disaster Recovery Restore Procedure

When etcd is corrupted, execute this step-by-step restoration:

# Step 1: Restore snapshot to a NEW data directory
ETCDCTL_API=3 etcdctl snapshot restore /opt/etcd-backup.db \
  --data-dir=/var/lib/etcd-restored \
  --initial-cluster="controlplane=https://127.0.0.1:2380" \
  --initial-cluster-token="etcd-cluster-1" \
  --initial-advertise-peer-urls="https://127.0.0.1:2380" \
  --name="controlplane"

# Step 2: Update the hostPath mount in the etcd static pod manifest
sudo sed -i 's|path: /var/lib/etcd|path: /var/lib/etcd-restored|g' /etc/kubernetes/manifests/etcd.yaml

# Step 3: Wait for kubelet to restart the etcd static pod (30-60 seconds)
# Step 4: Verify health
kubectl get nodes
  • Safety Warning: NEVER restore directly into /var/lib/etcd without specifying --data-dir=/var/lib/etcd-restored. Restoring over an existing directory causes write collisions and corrupts the Raft log.

3. CRICTL CONTAINER RUNTIME REFERENCE

crictl communicates directly with the CRI socket (/run/containerd/containerd.sock). It is the primary troubleshooting tool when kubectl is unavailable.

Common CRICTL Operations:

# Configure endpoint once if not set
cat <<EOF | sudo tee /etc/crictl.yaml
runtime-endpoint: unix:///run/containerd/containerd.sock
image-endpoint: unix:///run/containerd/containerd.sock
timeout: 10
EOF

# 1. List all active and stopped Pod sandboxes
sudo crictl pods

# 2. List all containers (including exited and crashing containers)
sudo crictl ps -a

# 3. Filter containers by name (e.g. static control plane pods)
sudo crictl ps -a --name kube-apiserver

# 4. View raw stdout/stderr logs directly from the runtime
sudo crictl logs <container-id>

# 5. Execute interactive shell inside a container
sudo crictl exec -it <container-id> sh

# 6. Prune all unused container images to free disk space
sudo crictl rmi --prune

4. LINUX SYSTEMD & KERNEL DIAGNOSTICS

Inspecting System Daemons with systemctl & journalctl

# Check service status
sudo systemctl status kubelet
sudo systemctl status containerd

# Tail kubelet systemd journal logs
sudo journalctl -u kubelet -n 100 --no-pager
sudo journalctl -u kubelet -f

# Tail containerd runtime logs
sudo journalctl -u containerd -n 100 --no-pager

# Reload daemon configuration after editing systemd service files
sudo systemctl daemon-reload
sudo systemctl restart kubelet

5. NETWORK & CONNECTIVITY DIAGNOSTICS

# 1. Check IP addresses and network interfaces
ip -br addr show
ip route show

# 2. Check listening TCP ports and process owners (verify port 6443, 2379, 10250)
ss -tulpen | grep -E "6443|2379|10250|10259|10257"

# 3. Test TCP port reachability with 3-second timeout
nc -zvw3 192.168.1.10 6443

# 4. Inspect raw HTTP/HTTPS API response with TLS verbose output
curl -Iv -k https://127.0.0.1:6443/version

# 5. Capture live packet traffic on CNI bridge interface
sudo tcpdump -nn -i any port 6443 -c 10
sudo tcpdump -nn -i cbr0 icmp

# 6. Test CoreDNS resolution from inside the cluster
kubectl run dns-test --rm -it --image=busybox:1.28 -- nslookup kubernetes.default
kubectl run dns-test --rm -it --image=busybox:1.28 -- nslookup kube-dns.kube-system.svc.cluster.local

1. COMPREHENSIVE CANDIDATE SCORECARD

┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┬──────────┬─────────────┬───────────┐

├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┼──────────┼─────────────┼───────────┤
